The one thing shaping every game on this card

Twenty-three fixtures, one round in each of the five biggest European leagues, and the single structural fact running through all of them is the summer’s selling. Club after club on this card banked a career-high fee for its most valuable asset and did not replace it with anything of comparable price: Newcastle sold Bruno Guimarães to Arsenal for £75m and Sandro Tonali to Tottenham; Aston Villa sold Morgan Rogers, Ezri Konsa, Ollie Watkins and their goalkeeper; Brighton sold Carlos Baleba for £70m and Jan Paul van Hecke for £52m; Osasuna sold Víctor Muñoz for €40m; Rayo sold Nobel Mendy and Pep Chavarría for €42.4m between them; Udinese banked close to €50m for Arthur Atta and Thomas Kristensen. Several of those fees were paid by other clubs playing on this same card, and one of them — Mendy to Hull — moved between two clubs that both kick off this afternoon.

That matters here for one reason only, and it is a reason about evidence rather than about quality. Every rate on this board carrying a 2025–26 label was produced by a squad that, in at least one of the two positions deciding most market families — the centre-back line and the centre-forward — no longer exists. Brighton replaced all four senior centre-backs; Tottenham sold both of theirs; Cagliari sold both centre-forwards; Aston Villa sold the goalkeeper, a centre-back and the striker in one window. Against that, the current sample is three league matches in the Bundesliga, four in the Premier League, Serie A and Ligue 1, and six or seven in LaLiga — so the division with the most turnover to absorb is the one with the least evidence of how it has gone.

The second fact, quieter but touching every card, is that four of the five divisions are scoring materially above their own completed 2025–26 rate: the Bundesliga at 3.96 goals per match against 3.24, Serie A at 3.02 against 2.43, LaLiga at 3.07 against 2.69, the Premier League at 2.85 against 2.75. Ligue 1 alone is below, at 2.76 against 2.82, and it is also running at a 29.7% home-win rate against 46.1% last season. Those new baselines rest on 28 to 60 matches each.

Structural consequence. On this card the two numbers a family is normally read from — the club’s own rate and the divisional baseline — have both moved, and neither has moved for a settled reason. Where a card below rates a family well-suited, it is because a rate, a mechanism in the shot or pressing data, and a transfer ledger all say the same thing; where it rates one dangerous, it is usually because the club rate and the divisional rate now disagree about direction and four matches cannot arbitrate between them.

Provenance. No figure on this board was supplied to me and none was read off a price. Every club rate — goals, over 2.5, BTTS, clean sheets, first-half goals, cards, fouls, corners, shots, possession — was counted from ESPN’s public match record, match by match, for both completed seasons; expected goals, non-penalty xG, PPDA and deep completions come from Understat. ESPN’s usual host returned HTTP 403 all day from this machine, so the same data was taken from site.web.api.espn.com, and the goal-timing parser behind every first-half figure was reconciled against final scores on 203 matches across all five divisions and both seasons before use, matching 203 of 203 including own goals. Referee records here are computed, not quoted: every completed match in each division was scanned for its appointed official, and each referee’s cards, fouls, fouls-per-card and penalties counted from the matches he actually took — 380 Premier League, 380 LaLiga, 380 Serie A, 306 Bundesliga and 305 Ligue 1 matches of 2025–26. Only the appointment for today cost a search. Unpublished across the slate: no source reached characterises any official’s reading of contact in the box in words, so every penalty figure here is a counted rate and nothing more; ESPN publishes no xG for 2025–26, which Understat supplies for the top five divisions and nobody supplies below them; and no 2025–26 xG exists for any promoted club on this slate — Hull, Coventry, Ipswich, Racing Santander, Venezia, Le Mans and Troyes spent last season in divisions Understat does not cover, and none is estimated. Last season standing in for a thin current sample: every 25/26 figure printed beside a 26/27 one is there because the current sample is under five matches, which is true of every Premier League, Serie A, Bundesliga and Ligue 1 club on this board. League baselines are in baselines_0919.md; the 2025–26 lines are carried unchanged from baselines_0918.md and the 2026–27 lines were recomputed today.
Where this board stands. 16 of 23 fixtures are carded: twelve at full depth, four at reduced depth (f1, f4, f9, f17), where sections B and E are omitted and each card names which fields the tier did not pursue. Seven are not built — f3, f5, f6, f7, f8, f13 and f14 — and the reason is timing rather than judgement. All seven were set aside at selection because a card on them would have rested on thinner evidence: a side with only three or four competitive matches played, or no promotion, coaching change or divisional re-entry on record for either club. That decision was later overridden and all ten listed fixtures were to be researched at full depth — but by then fourteen of the twenty-three had kicked off, and a pre-match context card cannot honestly be written for a match already under way. The three the override still reached — f21, f22 and f23 — were built at full depth, and two of them turned out to be among the best-documented fixtures on the slate. One fixture also changed tier during research: f12, Athletic Club–Alavés, was promoted from reduced to full depth when its first search turned up a head-coach change — Edin Terzić for Ernesto Valverde — that the selection had recorded as unknown. Nothing was demoted to pay for either change. The selection was made on evidence available, never on the merit of the games, and all five divisions keep at least one card. A companion board, context-board_0919_evening.html, carries only the six fixtures that had still not kicked off when this one was cut.

A · Squad, promotion & transfer context

  • Not sourced, and not pursued at this tier. This is a reduced-depth card: three searches per club rather than the full ceiling, and sections B and E are omitted. Fields the tier did not pursue: the full style read — press height, crossing and set-piece volume — beyond the pressing and shot figures quoted below; the complete academy and loan ledger for either club; and any fee for the four loan arrivals. Fields pursued and still empty: two of them are contradictions rather than gaps, and both are reported rather than resolved — Sandro Tonali’s fee is given as £92.5m by Wikipedia’s season page and £100m by Sports Mole, and Morgan Rogers’ as £117m by Wikipedia and £120m in press reporting. Tottenham’s net spend is £163m on one source and £213m on the other, because they disagree on the income column, not the outlay. Nothing else on this card is missing: the Premier League publishes match-level xG, shot and possession data, complete fees, and a per-referee record deep enough to count by hand, and every rate in the strip was counted from the match record rather than read off a table.
  • Tottenham — turnover very high, and concentrated in exactly the two units that decide this card: centre-back and centre-forward. Manager changed on 31 March 2026. Roberto De Zerbi took over in March and this is his first window. Centre-back — three in, three out: out — Cristian Romero to Atlético Madrid, £34.2m, Luka Vušković to Brighton, £46m, and Ashley Phillips to Middlesbrough, £7m; in — Jan Paul van Hecke from Brighton, £52m, Tosin Adarabioyo from Chelsea (£7m up front plus £2m of add-ons on one source, undisclosed on the other), and Marcos Senesi free from Bournemouth. Central and defensive midfield: in — Sandro Tonali from Newcastle, £92.5m or £100m, and Mateus Fernandes from West Ham, £85m; out — Yves Bissouma free to Ajax. Attack: in — Sávio from Manchester City, £75m, plus Omar Marmoush on loan from Manchester City and Mykhailo Mudryk on loan from Chelsea; out — Will Lankshear to Middlesbrough £10m, Manor Solomon to West Ham £5m, Tynan Thompson to Manchester United £8m. Goalkeeper: in — Martin Dúbravka free from Burnley. Full-back: in — Andrew Robertson free from Liverpool; out — Djed Spence to Inter, £30m. Outlay £311.5m–£312m against £99m–£148.5m of income. Fifty-four movements in and out in one window on one source’s count.
  • Aston Villa — turnover very high and financially the reverse of Tottenham’s: the three best-paid outfield assets were sold, including the goalkeeper. Manager unchanged. Unai Emery remains head coach, no change this window. Out, the headline: Morgan Rogers to Chelsea, £117m or £120m — reported as the largest fee ever paid between two English clubs; Ezri Konsa to Arsenal, £51m plus £4m; Ollie Watkins to Al Hilal, £50m; Youri Tielemans to Manchester United, £35m; Emiliano Martínez to Chelsea, £7.5m. That is the first-choice goalkeeper, a first-choice centre-back, the centre-forward and two of the midfield, in one window. Goalkeeper: in — Zion Suzuki from Parma, £25.6m plus £4.3m. Centre-back: in — Taylor Harwood-Bellis from Southampton, £25m plus £5m, and Modou Kéba Cissé from LASK, £4m. Defensive midfield: in — Johan Manzambi from Freiburg, £52m, João Gomes from Wolves, £34m plus £4m, and Leon Goretzka free from Bayern. Centre-forward: in — Nicolas Jackson from Chelsea, £47.5m plus £17.5m. Left-back: in — Matteo Ruggeri from Atlético Madrid, £17m. Ten arrivals against eighteen departures, with a sales column reported around £310m.
  • Suspensions / absences. No suspension is reported for either side. Tottenham: Mykhailo Mudryk, Xavi Simons and Wilson Odobert are out; Dejan Kulusevski is back in full training but is not expected to feature until later in the season; Pedro Porro and Sandro Tonali have recovered but their starting status is unresolved at the time of writing — Tonali being the £92.5m–£100m midfielder this window was built around. Aston Villa are missing seven: Marco Bizot, Pau Torres, Ian Maatsen, Leon Goretzka, Brian Madjo, Amadou Onana and Modou Kéba Cissé. That list is structurally worse than its length suggests — Pau Torres and Cissé are two of the centre-backs meant to replace Konsa, and Goretzka and Onana are two of the midfielders, so two of the three units Villa rebuilt this summer are missing a rebuilt part of themselves.
  • Sample. Tottenham: six competitive matches, four in the league — 0–3 at Brentford (22 August), 0–2 v Newcastle (29 August), 0–0 at Nottingham Forest (5 September), 0–0 v Everton (12 September) — plus EFL Cup ties against Charlton (26 August) and away at Liverpool (15 September). Seventeenth on 2 points. They have not scored a Premier League goal this season: zero in four, a 100% failed-to-score rate against a divisional 29.3%. Aston Villa: seven competitive matches, four in the league — 0–4 at Brighton (23 August), 0–1 v Arsenal (31 August), 0–0 at Hull (5 September), 1–2 v Nottingham Forest (12 September) — plus the UEFA Super Cup at Paris Saint-Germain (12 August), a Champions League tie at Club Brugge (8 September) and an EFL Cup tie at Coventry (16 September). Nineteenth on 1 point, one league goal in four matches. Between them these two clubs have scored once in eight league matches.
  • Referee. Samuel Barrott, confirmed, with Adam Nunn and Lee Betts on the lines, Tom Bramall fourth official, John Brooks on VAR and Ian Hussin assisting. Record, counted from every match he refereed in ESPN’s public record rather than read off a published table: Premier League 2025–26, 24 matches, 3.75 cards per match, 19.38 fouls per match — 5.17 fouls per card — 0.29 penalties per match and one red card all season; 2026–27 so far, 2 matches, 3.00 cards, 18.00 fouls, 6.00 fouls per card, 0.50 penalties. Against the division he books slightly below average on volume (3.75 against 3.86 last season) but on distinctly fewer fouls, so his 5.17 fouls per card is a shorter leash than the divisional 5.60, and his 19.38 fouls per match was the lowest of any official with more than eleven appointments last season. History with these clubs: five matches in 2025–26 involving one of them, and he took Aston Villa–Tottenham on 3 May 2026 — 7 cards and 24 fouls, his highest card count in any of the five; also Tottenham–Manchester United (6 cards), Nottingham Forest–Tottenham (4), Sunderland–Aston Villa (4) and Aston Villa–Sunderland (4). The penalty rate is published here because it was counted; no source reached characterises his reading of contact in the box in words, and none is invented.

C · Market-type suitability

1X2 / MoneylineDangerous
Both clubs replaced the spine that produced last season’s record, and the two records point in opposite directions: Villa finished 4th on 65 points and won the Europa League, Tottenham finished 17th on 41. This season they are 19th and 17th with one goal between them. The structural reason the old evidence does not carry is specific rather than general — Villa sold their goalkeeper, a first-choice centre-back, their centre-forward and two midfielders; Tottenham sold both first-choice centre-backs and changed manager in March — and the divisional home-win rate has itself fallen to 34.1% from 42.6%, with draws at 34.1%. Four matches of a rebuilt side is not a basis for a result.
Asian handicap / DNBDangerous
A handicap needs a readable margin and neither club has produced one. Tottenham’s four league margins are −3, −2, 0, 0; Villa’s are −4, −1, 0, −1. Seven of those eight results are a defeat or a goalless draw, and the two zeroes on Tottenham’s side are 0–0s, not draws with goals. Last season’s home and away splits — Tottenham 1.16/1.63 at home, Villa 1.26/1.42 away — belong to different teams in the relevant positions. The central estimate is unknowable and the spread around it is the whole story.
Totals (O/U goals)OK — the rates are real, the sample is four matches each
This is the family where the evidence is at least pointing one way in every column, which is more than any other row here can say. Tottenham’s four league matches have produced 5 goals in total, Villa’s 8; both clubs’ over-2.5 rates (25.0% and 50.0%) sit under a divisional 53.7%, and the xG behind them corroborates rather than contradicts — Tottenham 1.02 for and Villa 0.82, against a divisional 1.64 per team. The caution is that both 25/26 rates were higher than this season’s (63.2% and 52.6%) and the division is running slightly above last season at 2.85 goals per match, so the club figures and the divisional figure disagree about direction. Four matches per club is what keeps this out of the top rating.
BTTSWell-suited — the clearest structural signal on the card
This is the one family where the evidence is extreme, consistent across both halves of the fixture, and mechanically explained by the transfer ledger rather than by form. Tottenham have failed to score in 4 of 4 league matches and Villa in 3 of 4; their BTTS rates are 0.0% and 25.0% against a divisional 51.2%. Both clubs sold their centre-forward or their leading attacking asset in the same window — Villa lost Watkins and Rogers, Tottenham lost Lankshear and rely on two loaned forwards — and the shot data says the same thing as the goals: Tottenham 3.00 shots on target per match, Villa 1.50, both against 25/26 rates of 3.84 and 4.47. The honest caveat is that both clubs’ 25/26 BTTS rates were above the divisional line (60.5% and 57.9%), so this is the summer’s reading, not the clubs’.
Team goalsSplit — the scoring side of each is well evidenced, the level is not
A team-goals question needs a scoring rate, and both clubs have one this season that is as clear as it is small: 0.00 and 0.25 per league match, with 1.02 and 0.82 xG behind them. That xG gap matters — both are underperforming, but not by a distance that makes the goal counts a fluke, and Villa’s 0.82 xG per match is the lower figure on a side whose centre-forward arrived in August. Against that, last season Tottenham scored 48 and Villa 56, at 1.26 and 1.47 per match, so the two seasons disagree by a factor of five on one side. Read the direction, not the number.
Halves (1H result, 1H goals)Split — 1H goals OK, 1H result Dangerous
The first-half column is unusually clean for once, and it is clean in the same direction as everything else: neither club has scored a first-half league goal this season — 0.00 per match each, across eight matches between them — against a divisional 1.27 per match, with Tottenham conceding 0.50 and Villa 1.00 before the break. Last season they scored 0.53 and 0.55 first-half goals per match, so the collapse is this season’s, not a standing property. A first-half result is a different question and an unreadable one: eight first halves with no goals scored by either club is a sample that makes every outcome look alike.
Discipline (cards, fouls)OK — referee well documented, club rates pull apart
Barrott is the best-evidenced input on this card: 24 Premier League matches last season at 3.75 cards and 19.38 fouls — 5.17 fouls per card against a divisional 5.60, a shorter leash than average on fewer fouls — and two matches this season at 3.00 and 6.00 fouls per card. His one previous meeting between these clubs, Aston Villa–Tottenham on 3 May 2026, produced 7 cards on 24 fouls, the highest of his five with either side. The clubs, though, disagree with each other and with themselves: Tottenham commit 14.0 fouls per match but take only 1.00 card; Villa commit 10.5 and take 2.25, against a divisional 11.57 fouls per team and 3.93 cards per match. Tottenham’s foul rate has risen from 12.2 and their card rate has fallen from 2.71 — two four-match figures moving in opposite directions, which is why this is not rated higher.
CornersOK — asymmetric, and the mechanism is visible
The two sides read differently and both readings have something behind them. Tottenham win 6.75 corners per match and concede 5.50, against 5.50/4.82 last season and a divisional 4.65 — consistent with 59.3% possession and 13.0 shots per match producing pressure without goals. Villa win 3.50 and concede 3.25, well down on last season’s 5.24/4.92, consistent with 8.25 shots per match and a PPDA of 17.87 — a side pressing far less and creating far less than the 11.53 and 12.71 of last season. Both figures rest on four matches, but unlike the goal columns they agree with the possession and shot data rather than sitting on top of it.

D · Early-season risk notes

  • Neither club’s 2025–26 record describes the team that will play. Villa finished 4th on 65 points and won the Europa League with Martínez in goal, Konsa at centre-back, Watkins at centre-forward and Rogers behind him — all four were sold. Tottenham finished 17th with Romero and Vušković at centre-back — both sold — under a manager appointed in March whose first window this is. Every 25/26 figure in the strip should be read as history rather than as a current property, on both sides of the fixture rather than just one.
  • Four league matches each is the whole current sample, and it contains two 0–0s and a 0–4. Tottenham’s 100% failed-to-score rate and Villa’s 75% are among the most extreme figures that will appear on this board, and they are computed over four matches. The xG columns support the direction — 1.02 and 0.82 per match against a divisional 1.64 — but a rate of zero cannot be extrapolated and is not evidence that scoring is impossible, only that it has not happened yet.
  • Villa’s absence list removes part of the rebuild itself. Pau Torres and Cissé are centre-backs and Goretzka and Onana midfielders, on a side that has just replaced its centre-back and midfield units. The same is half-true at Tottenham, where Tonali — the most expensive signing of the window on either side — has only just recovered and may not start. The teams that take the field may be a further step away from the squads the transfer ledger describes.
  • The division is not where it was either. The Premier League is running at 34.1% home wins and 34.1% draws this season against 42.6% home wins in 2025–26, and at 51.2% BTTS against 56.1%. That is 41 matches against 380. Any read that leans on the 26/27 divisional line is leaning on a tenth of a season, and this card leans on it in the BTTS row in particular.
  • Most sensitive: 1X2 and the Asian handicap — both need a margin from two sides that have produced seven defeats or goalless draws in eight, off squads rebuilt six weeks ago, and both would move on whether Tonali and Porro start. Least sensitive: BTTS and the discipline row — the first because the failure to score is corroborated by shots, shots on target, xG and the transfer ledger on both sides at once, the second because Barrott’s 24-match record is the largest verified sample anywhere on this card.

Spain · LaLiga · Jornada 7

CA Osasuna – Rayo Vallecano

A · Squad, promotion & transfer context

Neither club is promoted and neither is new to this division. Osasuna finished 17th in 2025–26 on 42 points — 11 wins, 9 draws, 18 defeats, 44 scored, 50 conceded — and stayed up only on the final matchday. Rayo finished 8th on 50 points — 12 wins, 14 draws, 12 defeats, 41 scored, 44 conceded, with the lowest over-2.5 rate of the two by a distance: 36.8% against a divisional 50.0%. The structural headline is that these two sides arrived at a similar place by opposite routes and then made the same two decisions in the same six weeks. Both dismissed or lost their head coach at the end of May and appointed a replacement in June; both sold their most valuable player for a fee they did not reinvest. Osasuna banked €40m for Víctor Muñoz and spent €8m; Rayo banked €42.4m for Nobel Mendy and Pep Chavarría and spent €2.6m plus four loans. Last season’s numbers on this card describe two squads that have each lost their best asset and their coach.

  • Osasuna — turnover medium by volume, extreme by value; manager changed 10 June 2026. Alessio Lisci left on 25 May 2026, announced the day after survival was secured on the last matchday, with a year still to run on his contract. Luis Miguel Ramis was appointed on or about 10 June 2026 on a deal to 30 June 2028, from Burgos in LaLiga Hypermotion — the sporting department’s first choice, Carles Martínez, went to Bayer Leverkusen instead, so Ramis is a second-plan appointment and a second-tier one. Out, the headline: Víctor Muñoz to Liverpool, €40m, on 18 June 2026 — the single largest fee on either side of this fixture, paid eight days after the new coach arrived. Attack: out — Sheraldo Becker to Mainz 05, €500,000, and Iker Benito to Querétaro, €520,000; in — Jonathan Dubasin from Sporting Gijón, €4m (27 July), Rockson Yeboah from IFK Göteborg, €3m (12 August), and Romain Del Castillo from Brest, €1m (1 September). Defence: out — Javi Galán free to Celta Vigo; in — Diego Rico free from Getafe (12 August). Out, other: Juan Cruz free to Real Oviedo, Jon García free to Cultural Leonesa, Roberto Arroyo free to Racing Ferrol, Martín Pedroarena free to Arenas. Roughly €8m spent against €41m of income. No goalkeeper and no centre-forward was bought.
  • Rayo Vallecano — turnover high, and almost entirely on loan; manager changed 18 June 2026. Íñigo Pérez left at the end of his contract on 29 May 2026 and Beñat San José was appointed on 18 June 2026 — a fact carried from this board’s own card of 15 September (fx_0915_f2), not re-sourced. Out, the headline: Nobel Mendy to Hull City, €23.4m, and Pep Chavarría to Chelsea, €19m — €42.4m from two players, and Mendy has gone to a club playing on this same slate. Centre-back: out — Abdul Mumin free to Girona; in — Marash Kumbulla on loan from Roma and Mujaid Sadick on loan from Genk. Goalkeeper: in — Emil Audero on loan from Como. Full-back: out — Pacha Espino free to Racing Club; in — Adrià Pedrosa on loan from Sevilla. Permanent signings, all four of them small: Jozhua Tomayo Vertrouwd from Castellón, €1.5m; Gnangoro Bouare Samake from Real Betis, €600,000; Rayane Belaid Khellil from Atlético Madrid B, €500,000; Giorgi Tsitaishvili free from Dynamo Kyiv. Roughly €2.6m spent against €42.4m of income, with the defence rebuilt on four loans rather than on transfers.
  • Suspensions / absences. No suspension is reported for either side. Osasuna are missing four, two in each of defence and midfield: Jorge Herrando (adductor, out until November), Valéry Rosier (muscular), Aimar Oroz (biceps femoris, out until October) and Moi Gómez (muscular). Rayo are missing five, and the list runs straight through the units they rebuilt: Augusto Batalla (fractured tibia, out until January — which is why Audero was loaned in), Jozhua Vertrouwd (muscular, out until October — their biggest permanent signing of the window), Luiz Felipe (muscular), Isi Palazón (quadriceps) and Randy Nteka (doubtful). Both clubs are therefore fielding a rebuilt unit with part of the rebuild unavailable.
  • Sample. Osasuna: six competitive matches, all league — 0–0 v Levante (24 August), 2–1 at Celta Vigo (27 August), 1–0 v Getafe (31 August), 2–5 at Alavés (6 September), 0–2 v Espanyol (12 September), 0–4 at Atlético Madrid (16 September). Thirteenth on 7 points, 5 scored and 12 conceded. The home and away split is violent: 0.33 scored and 0.67 conceded per match at El Sadar, against 1.33 and 3.33 away. Rayo: six competitive matches, all league — 1–2 at Sevilla (15 August), 1–1 v Alavés (20 August), 2–5 at Barcelona (31 August), 3–2 v Racing Santander (5 September), 1–4 at Real Madrid (12 September), 2–1 v Espanyol (15 September). Eleventh on 7 points, 10 scored and 15 conceded. Every one of Rayo’s six matches has seen both teams score; five of the six went over 2.5. Neither club has played outside the league.
  • Referee. Manuel Jesús Orellana Cid, confirmed by the club press and by the matchday listing. He is newly promoted to Primera: this is his fourth match at this level and he has no 2025–26 top-flight record at all. Across the three he has taken: 6.67 cards per match, 28.33 fouls per match — 4.25 fouls per card — and 0.33 penalties per match, counted from the ESPN match record. Two independent counts agree exactly: the Rayo club press reports 19 yellows and 1 red across his three matches, which is the same 20 cards, the same 6.67. Against the division he is the heaviest-booking official in LaLiga this season — 6.67 against a divisional 4.48 — and the second-shortest leash at 4.25 fouls per card against a divisional 5.52. History: none with either club — this is his first Rayo Vallecano match and his first Osasuna match. Three matches is a very small record, and an official with no track record is itself a source of variance rather than a settled input; both facts are carried into the Discipline row below.
  • Not sourced. Positions for several of Osasuna’s outgoing free transfers; fees for Rayo’s four loan arrivals, none of which is published. Any characterisation in words of how Orellana reads contact in the box — his 0.33 penalties per match is counted, but no source reached describes his tendency, and none is invented. Everything else the spec asks for is published by this division: LaLiga carries match-level xG through Understat, complete shot and possession data, per-match officials and complete fees, and every rate above was counted from the match record rather than read off a table.

B · Style & early-season reality

Osasuna are the same side they were, playing worse away from home and with a hole where their best player used to be. Possession 47.1% against 46.8% last season, PPDA 11.99 against 12.07 — the press has not moved at all, which is notable given the coach has. What has moved is everything downstream of it: shots have fallen from 11.84 to 9.33 per match, deep completions from 5.05 to 3.17, and corners won from 3.76 to 3.00, the lowest corner rate of either side here and well under a divisional 4.94. The clearest single number on their card is the penalty dependence: xG 1.33 per match but non-penalty xG 0.95, so roughly 29% of what they generate comes from the spot, against a last-season split of 1.35 and 1.20 where it was 11%. A side that has lost a €40m forward-line asset and replaced him with €8m of signings is creating less from open play and converting less — 0.83 goals per match against 1.16 — and doing it while conceding 2.00. The El Sadar record is the counterweight and it is real: 0.67 conceded per home match, three home matches, against 3.33 away.

Rayo have changed shape and the change is measurable rather than asserted, but it has gone the wrong way at both ends. Possession has dropped from 53.42% to 46.32% and PPDA from 8.46 to 9.51 — they press slightly less and have the ball considerably less — while the defensive side has collapsed in a way the deep-completions column states plainly: deep completions conceded have risen from 6.26 to 11.83 per match, nearly double, and shots faced from 12.24 to 17.67. The back line doing that work is Kumbulla and Sadick on loan, behind a loaned goalkeeper, after Mumin left free and Chavarría was sold for €19m. At the other end the story is the mirror image and it is the most extreme swing on this card: a side that went over 2.5 goals in 36.8% of matches last season has done it in 83.3% of six this season, and has had both teams score in 100% of them after 44.7% last season. Under San José they are scoring more (1.67 against 1.08) and conceding far more (2.50 against 1.16). The evidence that this is a system change is a coach’s start date, six matches of pressing and deep-completion data, and a defence assembled on loan — not a season.

C · Market-type suitability

1X2 / MoneylineDangerous
Both clubs changed head coach in June and both sold their most valuable player without reinvesting, so the two full-season records that would settle this — Osasuna 17th on 42, Rayo 8th on 50 — describe squads that no longer exist in the places that matter. Six matches each is the current evidence and it splits oddly: identical points totals (7) from opposite shapes, with Osasuna at 5 goals scored and Rayo at 10. The divisional home-win rate has held near last season’s (45.0% against 48.9%), which is context and not a finding. Nothing here supports a result.
Asian handicap / DNBDangerous
A handicap needs a readable margin and Osasuna’s margins are the least readable thing on this card. Their six results run +0, +1, +1, −3, −2, −4 — three narrow home results and three heavy away defeats, a 0.33-scored/0.67-conceded home rate against 1.33/3.33 away. Rayo’s are −1, 0, −3, +1, −3, +1, equally bimodal. Two sides whose results are either tight or routs, with no middle, is precisely the distribution a handicap reads worst.
Totals (O/U goals)Split — well evidenced on Rayo, contradictory on Osasuna
Rayo’s side of this is as clear as anything on the card: 83.3% over 2.5 across six matches, 4.17 goals per match in games they have played, and an xG line of 1.29 for and 2.02 against that agrees with it. Osasuna’s is the opposite of clear — 50.0% over 2.5, exactly their 25/26 rate and near the divisional 56.7%, but composed of three matches with one goal or none and three with five or more. The division is running at 3.07 against 2.69 last season, so the divisional baseline and Osasuna’s own rate disagree about direction. One half of this fixture is readable, the other is an average of two extremes.
BTTSWell-suited — best-evidenced family here
This is the one row where a structural mechanism, a club rate and a divisional baseline all point the same way. Rayo have had both teams score in 6 of 6, have not kept a clean sheet and have not failed to score — 0.0% on both columns against a divisional 25.0% — and the reason is in the deep-completions row rather than in form: 11.83 conceded per match against 6.26 last season, behind a centre-back pairing on loan and a loaned goalkeeper covering Batalla’s fractured tibia. Osasuna’s 33.3% is the softer input and the honest caveat, driven by two home clean sheets in three; but they have scored in three of six and their 18.4% clean-sheet rate last season was the lowest of the two. The defensive half of the question is exceptionally well covered; the Osasuna attack is the weaker input.
Team goalsSplit — asymmetric, and the asymmetry is venue-shaped
Rayo’s team-goal record is usable: 1.67 per match this season against 1.08 last, with 1.29 xG behind it and a goal in every one of six matches. Osasuna’s is not one number but two — 0.33 per match at El Sadar and 1.33 away, from a side whose non-penalty xG is 0.95 and whose €40m attacking asset left in June for a €8m replacement budget. Reading their home rate off the season figure of 0.83 would average across the very split that defines them. Last season the same split ran the other way round (1.63 home, 0.68 away), which is a further reason not to treat either version as settled.
Halves (1H result, 1H goals)OK — 1H goals evidenced, 1H result not
The goal-timing rates exist on both sides and they disagree with each other in a way that is informative: Osasuna have scored one first-half goal in six matches (0.17 per match) and conceded 1.00; Rayo have scored 1.00 and conceded 1.17, against a divisional 1.43 per match. Both clubs ran at 0.50–0.55 for and against last season, so Osasuna’s first-half attack is this season’s collapse and not a standing property. That is enough to read a first-half total. A first-half result is not readable from it: one goal in six first halves on one side is a sample in which every outcome looks alike.
Discipline (cards, fouls)Well-suited on the official, thin on the clubs — and the two disagree
Orellana Cid is booking at 6.67 cards per match on 28.33 fouls — 4.25 fouls per card, against a divisional 4.48 cards and 5.52 fouls per card. He is the heaviest-booking official in LaLiga this season and close to the shortest leash, and two independent counts of his three matches agree exactly (19 yellows and 1 red, per the Rayo club press; 20 cards over three, per the match record). The structural point is that the clubs point the other way: Osasuna take 2.33 cards per match and Rayo 2.83, both well under the divisional 4.48, on foul rates of 12.8 and 13.5 that are at or just above a divisional 12.36. So this is a low-card pair meeting a high-card referee. The rating is high because the collision is well documented on both sides, not because the outcome is obvious — and it must be read alongside the fact that three matches is his entire top-flight record, and an unknown official is a source of variance in his own right.
CornersOK — stable rates, and the gap holds in both seasons
This is the quietest row on the card and that is its merit: both clubs’ corner rates have barely moved across the coaching change. Osasuna won 3.76 and conceded 4.82 per match last season, and win 3.00 and concede 4.67 now; Rayo won 5.71 and conceded 5.26, and now win 5.50 and concede 6.50, against a divisional 4.94 and 4.83 in the two seasons. The direction — Osasuna below the line at both ends, Rayo above it — is the same in 38 matches and in 6, which is more than the goal columns can say. The mechanism is visible too: Osasuna’s 9.33 shots and 3.17 deep completions per match are the lowest figures here.

D · Early-season risk notes

  • Both clubs’ 2025–26 numbers were produced under a different head coach and with a player who has been sold. Osasuna’s 44 goals and 42 points came under Lisci with Víctor Muñoz; Rayo’s 50 points and 44 conceded came under Íñigo Pérez with Chavarría and Mumin in the defence. Ramis arrived on 10 June and San José on 18 June. Every 25/26 figure in the strip is history on both sides of this fixture, not just one.
  • Osasuna’s season rate is an average of two different teams. Three home matches at 0.33 scored and 0.67 conceded; three away at 1.33 and 3.33. The 0.83/2.00 season line describes neither. Last season the same club ran 1.63/1.26 at home and 0.68/1.37 away — a split of the same size pointing the opposite way — so the venue effect is large, unstable, and computed here over three matches per venue.
  • Rayo’s defensive collapse is a loan-market artefact as much as a tactical one. Deep completions conceded have risen from 6.26 to 11.83 and shots faced from 12.24 to 17.67, behind Kumbulla and Sadick on loan and Audero on loan covering a goalkeeper out until January. Their most expensive permanent signing, Vertrouwd at €1.5m, is injured until October. The 2.50 conceded per match is real and six matches deep, but the unit producing it has been together for six weeks.
  • The referee has no record worth the name. Three Primera matches, no 2025–26 top-flight season, no history with either club. His 6.67 cards per match is the most extreme figure in the division and it rests on three appointments; it could be who he is, or it could be three matches. The Discipline row is rated well-suited because the collision between a high-card official and two low-card clubs is documented on both sides — not because 6.67 is a settled rate.
  • The division has moved more than either club has. LaLiga is running at 3.07 goals per match this season against 2.69 last, on 60 matches against 380. Where this card uses the 26/27 divisional line — in the totals and BTTS rows especially — it is leaning on a sixth of a season.
  • Most sensitive: the Asian handicap and 1X2 — both need a margin from two clubs whose six results are either one-goal or three-plus with nothing between, and both would move on whether Osasuna’s home or away version turns up. Least sensitive: BTTS and corners — the first because Rayo’s 6-from-6 is corroborated by xG against, shots faced, deep completions and a loan-built defence all at once; the second because both clubs’ corner rates survived a change of coach almost unchanged.

E · Short market-type guide

  • BTTS is more robust than the match result here because Rayo’s 100% both-scored rate rests on four independent columns that agree — 2.50 conceded, 2.02 xG against, 17.67 shots faced, 11.83 deep completions conceded — while the result question depends on two squads that changed coach and sold their best player eleven weeks ago.
  • Corners are more robust than total goals here because both clubs’ corner rates are nearly identical across 38 matches last season and 6 this (Osasuna 3.76→3.00, Rayo 5.71→5.50), whereas Rayo’s over-2.5 rate has moved from 36.8% to 83.3% across the same coaching change.
  • The discipline row is more robust than the first-half result because Orellana’s 6.67 cards and 4.25 fouls per card are confirmed by two independent counts and set against two club card rates measured over six matches each, while the first-half result rests on Osasuna having scored one goal before the break all season.
  • A first-half total is more robust than a team-goals line on Osasuna because the timing rates exist on both sides for both seasons, whereas Osasuna’s scoring rate is 0.33 at home and 1.33 away and their non-penalty xG is 0.95 — three numbers for one question.
  • The single fact that would most change which family I choose is Orellana Cid’s real card rate. At 6.67 per match against a divisional 4.48 he is the most extreme input on this card and he has three matches of record. What would settle it is either a fourth and fifth appointment at this level, or a published LaLiga Hypermotion record for his seasons below — neither of which exists in any source reached today. Until one does, the discipline row is a documented collision rather than a documented rate.

A · Squad, promotion & transfer context

  • Not sourced, and not pursued at this tier. Reduced-depth card: three searches per club, sections B and E omitted. Fields the tier did not pursue: fees for most of the movements below — Serie A publishes them and they were simply not chased at this depth, so what follows names players and clubs but prices only two deals; the full loan and youth ledger on both sides; and the style read beyond the pressing, shot and corner figures quoted in section C. Fields pursued and still contradictory: the team news, which three sources tell three ways. Sky’s matchday page lists no unavailable players for either club and says only that Zaniolo is fit but likely to start on the bench; a second preview reports Ehizibue and Payero suspended and Ekkelenkamp injured for Udinese while naming Ekkelenkamp in its own starting eleven, and adds Thauvin and Lucca as continuing absentees; the same piece places Zanoli as recovered but benched and Massolin in doubt. Ehizibue is listed by the transfer tabellone as having left Udinese this summer, which makes at least one of those reports stale. None of it is resolved here. Read the absence bullet below as a set of claims, not a team sheet.
  • Udinese — turnover high in volume and concentrated in defence; manager unchanged. Kosta Runjaić remains head coach, no change this window. Out, the headline: Arthur Atta to Fiorentina and Thomas Kristensen to Atalanta — reported together as close to €50m, which is the whole financial story of Udinese’s summer. Midfield: in — Unai Gómez from Athletic Club, €4.5m, named as Atta’s direct replacement, plus Giorgi Chakvetadze from Watford; Nicolò Zaniolo was bought outright from Galatasaray and re-signed, which the Italian press treats as the window’s main piece of business. Defence — four in: Ousmane Traoré from Heidenheim, Mergim Vojvoda from Como, James Abankwah from Watford and Palma from Sampdoria. Goalkeeper: in — Mrozek from Lech Poznań; out — Răzvan Sava to Craiova. Out, other: Ehizibue, Nunziante, Zemura, Buksa, Amadou Camara, Mlacic and Goglichidze. The back line that will play is substantially not the back line that conceded 48 goals last season, and neither is the goalkeeper behind it.
  • Cagliari — turnover very high in volume, almost all of it in midfield and attack; manager unchanged. Fabio Pisacane remains head coach; no change is reported this summer. Out, the headline: Gianluca Gaetano to Atalanta, around €12m plus bonuses. Midfield — the rebuild: in — Harry Winks from Leicester, Romano from Spezia, Fazzini, Akarakiri, Daniel Maldini from Atalanta and Roberto Gagliardini; out — Michael Folorunsho and Sebastiano Esposito. Attack: in — Kevin Carlos, M’Bala Nzola, Fadera and Ciervo; out — Andrea Belotti and Leonardo Pavoletti, the two senior centre-forwards. Defence: in — Kofler from Südtirol and Yukinari Sugawara; out — Marco Palestra, Gabriele Zappa, Raterink, Albarracín and Alberto Dossena on loan. Sources disagree on where Akarakiri came from — Fiorentina on one tabellone, Everton on another — and the disagreement is reported, not resolved. Both first-choice centre-forwards left and four midfielders arrived: the shape of the side is the part that changed.
  • Suspensions / absences. This is the weakest part of the card and it is weak because the sources conflict, not because nothing was found. The claims on the table: Udinese — Payero suspended, Ekkelenkamp injured (though named in the same source’s eleven), Thauvin and Lucca continuing absentees, Ehizibue suspended (but listed elsewhere as a summer departure); Cagliari — Zanoli recovered but expected on the bench, Massolin short of fitness and in doubt. Against all of that, Sky’s matchday page lists no unavailable player for either side and notes only that Zaniolo is fit and likely to be benched in favour of Unai Gómez. One preview headline describes Udinese as “decimated by injuries and suspensions”. No source reached agrees with another closely enough to build a list from, and none is preferred here.
  • Sample. Udinese: six competitive matches, four in the league — 1–1 v Como (22 August), 3–2 at Monza (29 August), 1–2 v Lazio (7 September), 3–5 at Internazionale (14 September) — plus Coppa Italia ties against Padova (15 August) and Venezia (2 September). Thirteenth on 4 points, 8 scored and 10 conceded. Both teams have scored in all four and three of the four went over 2.5. Cagliari: six competitive matches, four in the league — 1–0 at Parma (22 August), 0–1 v Internazionale (30 August), 1–0 v Lecce (7 September), 2–1 at Atalanta (12 September) — plus Coppa Italia ties against Arezzo (14 August) and Hellas Verona (3 September). Fifth on 9 points from three wins, with 4 scored and 2 conceded — and they have been outshot 19.00 to 12.25 per match doing it.
  • Referee. Gianluca Manganiello of the Pinerolo section, confirmed, with Bianchini (Perugia) and Pistarelli (Fermo) on the lines, Rapuano (Rimini) fourth official, Cosso (Reggio Calabria) on VAR and Ghersini (Genova) assisting. Record, counted from every Serie A match he refereed in the ESPN record: 2025–26, 10 matches, 3.10 cards per match on 24.30 fouls — 7.84 fouls per card — 0.10 penalties per match and no red cards all season; 2026–27 so far, one match (Milan–Venezia, 28 August), 4.00 cards on 19.00 fouls, 4.75 fouls per card. Against the division he is a long-leash official on both counts: 3.10 cards against a divisional 3.77, on the highest foul count of any regular Serie A referee last season, giving 7.84 fouls per card against a divisional 6.74. History: he took two Udinese matches last season — Hellas Verona–Udinese on 26 January (2 cards, 19 fouls) and Udinese–Cremonese on 17 May (4 cards, 19 fouls) — and no Cagliari match at all. Ten matches is a real but modest record and the one match of this season points the other way; both facts are carried into the Discipline row. His penalty rate is counted at 0.10 per match; no source reached characterises his reading of contact in words, and none is invented.

C · Market-type suitability

1X2 / MoneylineDangerous
The table and the underlying numbers disagree completely, which is the worst possible state for a result question. Cagliari are 5th on 9 points having been outshot in three of four matches, with 1.73 xG against per match and 19.00 shots faced; Udinese are 13th on 4 points with 2.49 xG against. Both clubs rebuilt substantial parts of the side — Udinese four defenders and a goalkeeper, Cagliari four midfielders and both centre-forwards — so last season’s 50 and 43 points do not settle it either. Four matches, and the two available readings of them contradict.
Asian handicap / DNBDangerous
Cagliari’s four league margins are +1, −1, +1, +1 — every match decided by exactly one goal, and three of them won while being outshot. Udinese’s are 0, +1, −1, −2. A margin read off eight one-goal-ish results, on a side whose results and shot counts point opposite ways, is a margin read off variance. Cagliari’s expected-goal difference is −0.53 per match and their actual goal difference is +0.50: the gap between those two is the whole risk in this row.
Totals (O/U goals)Split — well evidenced on Udinese, contradictory on Cagliari
Udinese’s side is clear and corroborated: 75.0% over 2.5, 4.50 goals per match in games they have played, and an xG line of 1.31 for and 2.49 against that agrees. Cagliari’s is the opposite — 25.0% over 2.5 and 1.50 goals per match, on an xG line of 1.20 and 1.73 that implies nearly three. One of these two readings of Cagliari is wrong and four matches cannot say which. The divisional line has moved a long way too: 3.02 goals per match and 58.5% over 2.5 this season against 2.43 and 45.8% across the whole of last.
BTTSSplit — one side at 100%, the other at 25%
The two clubs give the most opposed pair of readings on this board. Udinese have had both teams score in 4 of 4, with no clean sheet and no blank — 0.0% on both columns against a divisional 19.5%, behind an xGA of 2.49 and 20.25 shots faced per match, which is a mechanism rather than a run. Cagliari have had both score in 1 of 4, with two clean sheets — but on 1.73 xG against, so the clean sheets are not supported by the chances conceded. Last season both clubs sat at 44.7% and 50.0%, near the divisional 45.3%. The defensive half of the question is well covered on the home side and actively misleading on the away side.
Team goalsSplit — Udinese readable, Cagliari not
Udinese have scored in every match, at 2.00 per game against 1.18 last season, with 1.31 xG behind it — an overperformance but a modest one, and their away figure of 3.00 per match is inflated by a 3–5 at Inter. Cagliari have scored 4 goals in 4 matches on 1.20 xG and 1.01 non-penalty xG, with 4.75 shots on target per match against 3.47 last season. The two sides of this family are not equally evidenced and should not be rated alike: one has a rate supported by its chances, the other has a rate that its chances do not explain in either direction.
Halves (1H result, 1H goals)OK — 1H goals evidenced, 1H result not
The timing columns are unusually informative here. Udinese have scored 1.50 first-half goals per match and conceded 0.75, against 0.50 and 0.53 across the whole of last season — a tripling, on four matches. Cagliari sit at 0.50 and 0.50, close to last season’s 0.42 and 0.58 and well under a divisional 1.20 per match. So one club’s first-half rate has moved sharply and the other’s has not moved at all, which at least gives a first-half total two distinguishable inputs. A first-half result needs a margin inside 45 minutes from a fixture whose 90-minute margin is unreadable.
Discipline (cards, fouls)OK — referee documented, and everything points low
Manganiello is a long-leash official: 3.10 cards per match on 24.30 fouls last season — 7.84 fouls per card against a divisional 6.74 — with 0.10 penalties per match and not one red card in ten appointments. Both clubs agree with him: Udinese take 2.25 cards and commit 11.8 fouls, Cagliari 1.25 and 10.8, against a divisional 3.12 cards and 11.21 fouls per team, and both were under the divisional card rate last season too (1.92 and 2.08 against 3.77). Three independent low readings pointing the same way is the strongest structural agreement on this card. What holds the rating down: his only 2026–27 appointment produced 4.00 cards on 19.00 fouls — 4.75 fouls per card, the opposite of his season record — and ten matches is a modest base.
CornersWell-suited — the most extreme and best-mechanised number on the card
Udinese have won 1.75 corners per match and conceded 7.75. That is a swing from 4.50 and 4.84 last season, it is the widest corner asymmetry on this slate, and unlike most four-match extremes it has a visible mechanism in three other columns: 41.05% possession (against 45.63%), 11.25 shots against 20.25 faced, and a PPDA of 15.78 against 11.60 — a side pressing much less and defending much deeper than the one that finished 10th. Their per-match corner counts read 1, 3, 2, 1 against 10, 3, 8, 10, so the pattern repeats rather than resting on one rout. Cagliari’s 4.75/4.25 is close to the divisional 4.77 and up from 3.79/5.16. The level is four matches old; the direction is corroborated four different ways.

D · Early-season risk notes

  • Cagliari’s league position is the most misleading number on this card. Fifth on 9 points, 4 scored and 2 conceded — from four matches in which they were outshot 19.00 to 12.25 per match, conceded 1.73 xG per match and 9.50 deep completions, and won three by exactly one goal. Their actual goal difference is +0.50 per match and their expected-goal difference is −0.53. Every rate on their side of the strip — the 50% clean-sheet rate, the 25% BTTS, the 25% over 2.5 — is downstream of that gap.
  • Udinese’s defence is not the defence that produced last season’s 48 goals conceded. Four defenders arrived this summer (Traoré, Vojvoda, Abankwah, Palma), a goalkeeper arrived and another left, and Kristensen was sold to Atalanta as part of a near-€50m outgoing. The 2.50 conceded and 2.49 xG against per match are four matches of a new back line, and the 25/26 figures beside them belong to a different one.
  • The division has moved further than either club. Serie A is running at 3.02 goals per match, 58.5% over 2.5 and 61.0% BTTS this season, against 2.43, 45.8% and 45.3% across the whole of 2025–26 — and at a 14.6% draw rate, the lowest of the five divisions on this board. That is 41 matches against 380. Both clubs’ own 25/26 rates sat close to the old divisional line, so where this card uses the new one it is using a sample a tenth the size.
  • The team news does not resolve. Three sources give three accounts, one of them naming a player as injured and then selecting him, and another naming a suspended player the transfer tabellone says left the club in the summer. Anything on this card that depends on who actually starts — and the corner and possession readings in particular depend on Udinese’s shape — carries that unresolved contradiction with it.
  • Most sensitive: the Asian handicap and 1X2 — both need a margin, and the two readings of Cagliari available today (points and shots) disagree about which side is better. Least sensitive: corners and the discipline row — the first because Udinese’s 1.75/7.75 repeats across all four matches and is corroborated by possession, shots and PPDA at once; the second because a long-leash referee with ten matches of record meets two clubs that were both under the divisional card rate last season and are under it again this season.

A · Squad, promotion & transfer context

  • Not sourced, and not pursued at this tier. Reduced-depth card: three searches per club, sections B and E omitted. Fields the tier did not pursue: fees for Arsenal’s window beyond the four that are published in the sources reached — Christos Tzolis’ fee is described as “significant” and never stated, and the Hincapié option fee is not given; Brighton’s eleven loan departures individually; the set-piece and crossing detail behind the corner rates. Fields pursued and still contradictory: the officiating team. The Premier League’s own matchweek-5 release pairs Madley with Nick Hopton and Craig Taylor, Lewis Smith as fourth official, Jarred Gillett on VAR and Edward Smart assisting; a preview source instead gives Lee Betts and Simon Bennett, Simon Hooper fourth and a VAR name that appears nowhere in the league’s own list. Both sources agree Madley is the referee and that is the part carried into the strip; the supporting team is reported as disputed and the league’s own release preferred. Everything else the spec asks for is published by this division.
  • Brighton — turnover extreme, and the centre-back line was replaced in its entirety; manager unchanged. Fabian Hürzeler remains head coach, appointed June 2024 and extended in May 2026 to 2029. Centre-back — four out, three in: out — Jan Paul van Hecke to Tottenham, £52m, Diego Coppola to Paris FC, £17m, Igor Júlio to Burnley £3m and Adam Webster released; in — Luka Vušković from Tottenham, £46m, Pascal Struijk from Leeds, £18m, and Michael Svoboda from Venezia, £4.3m. Defensive midfield: out — Carlos Baleba to Manchester United, £70m; in — Chema Andrés from Stuttgart, £12.9m. Wide and attacking: in — Zadok Yohanna from AIK, £26m, Femi Azeez from Millwall, £15m, Rodrigo Rego from Benfica £3m, Promise David on loan from Union Saint-Gilloise; out — Danny Welbeck to Chelsea, £5m and Jeremy Sarmiento to Middlesbrough £3m. Full-back: in — Jaouen Hadjam from Young Boys, £11.1m and Costinha from Olympiacos £11m; out — Joel Veltman free to West Ham. Also out: Solly March released, James Milner retired, plus eleven loan departures. Spend £119.3m against £150m of income — a £30.7m surplus, and the only club on this card with one.
  • Arsenal — turnover medium and concentrated on adding, not replacing; manager unchanged. Mikel Arteta remains head coach, no change this window, on a side that won the 2025–26 title with 85 points. Midfield: in — Bruno Guimarães from Newcastle, £75m, on a four-year deal with an option for a fifth; out — Christian Nørgaard to Everton, permanent, on 5 August 2026. Centre-back: in — Ezri Konsa from Aston Villa, £51m rising to £55m, and Piero Hincapié made permanent from Bayer Leverkusen after his loan; out — Jakub Kiwior’s loan made permanent. Goalkeeper: in — Illan Meslier from Leeds on 9 July, a two-year deal with an option; out — Karl Hein’s loan made permanent. Attack: in — Christos Tzolis, fee not stated by any source reached; out — Leandro Trossard to Beşiktaş, £15.3m, plus Gabriel Jesus and Fábio Vieira. Two of the three clubs Arsenal bought from are on this slate: Newcastle, who play at 16:00 today, and Aston Villa, who play at 13:30.
  • Suspensions / absences. No suspension is reported for either side. Brighton are missing seven with three more in doubt, and the list runs straight through the summer’s business: out — Evan Ferguson (ankle), Zadok Yohanna, the £26m signing, Mats Wieffer (knee), Jack Hinshelwood, Kaoru Mitoma (thigh), Yankuba Minteh (calf) and Stefanos Tzimas (knee); doubtful — Femi Azeez (thigh, £15m), Michael Svoboda (knee, £4.3m, one of the three new centre-backs) and Jaouen Hadjam (knock, £11.1m). Four of the eleven players signed this summer are out or in doubt. Arsenal: William Saliba is confirmed out, which removes one of the two established centre-backs; Ben White and Cristhian Mosquera are doubts, and Arteta indicated in his pre-match press conference that White, Mosquera and Jurriën Timber could all be involved.
  • Sample. Brighton: seven competitive matches, four in the league — 4–0 v Aston Villa (23 August), 3–4 at Chelsea (30 August), 1–1 v Leeds (5 September), 5–0 at Coventry (13 September) — plus two Conference League qualifiers against Tromsø (20 and 27 August) and an EFL Cup tie at Manchester United (16 September). Sixth on 7 points, 13 scored and 5 conceded, the highest scoring rate on this board at 3.25 per match. Arsenal: six competitive matches, four in the league — 3–0 v Coventry (21 August), 1–0 at Aston Villa (31 August), 2–1 v Chelsea (6 September), 2–0 at Sunderland (12 September) — plus a Champions League tie at Napoli (9 September) and an EFL Cup tie at Ipswich (15 September). First on 12 points from four wins, 8 scored and 1 conceded.
  • Referee. Andy Madley, confirmed. Record, counted from every Premier League match he refereed in the ESPN record: 2025–26, 21 matches, 3.48 cards per match on 22.86 fouls — 6.58 fouls per card, against a divisional 5.60, one of the longest leashes in the division — and 0.29 penalties per match. The qualifier on that leniency is sharp: he showed 5 red cards in those 21 matches, the highest total of any Premier League official last season, so he books late but sends off more than anyone. 2026–27 so far, 3 matches, 4.00 cards on 20.67 fouls — 5.17 fouls per card, a markedly shorter leash than last season — and no penalties. History: three matches involving these clubs last season — Brighton–Aston Villa on 3 December 2025 (4 cards, 19 fouls), Brighton–Nottingham Forest on 1 March 2026 (4 cards, 25 fouls) and Arsenal–Everton on 14 March 2026, which produced no cards at all. No penalty was awarded in any of the three.

C · Market-type suitability

1X2 / MoneylineOK — asymmetric evidence, unusually good on one side
This is the rare fixture where one club is almost perfectly documented. Arsenal have reproduced last season’s underlying numbers to the decimal: xG 2.04 both seasons, xGA 0.87 and 0.84, PPDA 9.58 and 9.81, deep completions conceded 3.74 and 4.25 — a title-winning side, coach unchanged, that added Guimarães and Konsa without replacing its shape. Brighton are the opposite: an entirely new centre-back line, £70m midfielder sold, and four matches at 70.9% possession against 54.1% last season. The readable half of the question is the away side. The divisional home-win rate of 34.1% against 42.6% last season is context, not a finding.
Asian handicap / DNBDangerous
A handicap needs a margin and Brighton’s four are +4, −1, 0, +5 — two routs, a one-goal defeat and a draw, from a side whose per-match goal figures at home (2.50) and away (4.00) both differ wildly from last season’s 1.58 and 1.16. Arsenal’s are +3, +1, +1, +2, which is tighter, but three of the four came against Coventry, Aston Villa and Sunderland. Brighton concede 1.85 xG per match while scoring 2.70; Arsenal concede 0.84 while scoring 2.04. Two sides whose results swing between one-goal and five-goal is the distribution a handicap reads worst.
Totals (O/U goals)Split — Brighton’s matches are loud, Arsenal’s are not
The two halves disagree and both are evidenced. Brighton’s four league matches have produced 18 goals — 4.50 per match, 75.0% over 2.5 — behind an xG line of 2.70 for and 1.85 against that fully supports it. Arsenal’s have produced 9, at 50.0% over 2.5, behind 2.04 and 0.84: a side that scores freely and concedes almost nothing produces mid-range totals, and did so last season too (52.6%). The clash is between Brighton’s 1.85 xG conceded and Arsenal’s 0.84 xG conceded — the widest defensive gap on this card, and it points the total in two directions at once.
BTTSOK — both sides score, one side rarely concedes
The scoring half is settled: neither club has failed to score in a league match this season — 0.0% each, against a divisional 29.3% — and Arsenal failed to score in only 7.9% of 38 matches last season, the lowest figure on this board. The conceding half is where they part: Brighton have kept 2 clean sheets in 4 on 1.85 xG against, Arsenal 3 in 4 on 0.84, extending a 50.0% clean-sheet rate across the whole of last season. Arsenal’s defensive record is the best-evidenced single input on this card — 27 conceded in 38 matches, then 1 in 4, with the xG agreeing both times. What holds the rating is that Brighton have scored 13 in 4 and their attack is the one thing about them that has clearly improved.
Team goalsSplit — Arsenal readable at two seasons’ depth, Brighton at four matches
Arsenal’s team-goal record is as stable as anything on this board: 71 goals in 38 last season at 1.87 per match on 2.04 xG, then 2.00 per match on 2.04 xG. The same number twice, from the same coach and shape. Brighton’s is a four-match figure that has more than doubled — 3.25 per match against 1.37 — but it is not an empty overperformance: 19.50 shots per match against 13.03 last season, 6.00 on target against 4.63, and 2.70 xG against 1.54. The direction is corroborated; the level is four matches old and was produced partly against Coventry, who they beat 5–0.
Halves (1H result, 1H goals)OK — 1H goals well evidenced on both sides
Both clubs have first-half rates for two seasons and both have moved in the same direction. Brighton have scored 1.50 first-half goals per match against 0.53 last season and concede 1.00 against 0.66; Arsenal 0.75 against 0.79 and concede 0.25 against 0.26 — Arsenal’s first-half profile is unchanged to within a rounding error, which is a genuinely useful fact, while Brighton’s has tripled. Against a divisional 1.27 per match, that gives a first-half total two distinguishable and differently-sourced inputs. A first-half result is the weaker question: Arsenal’s 0.75-for and 0.25-against over four matches is three goals and one conceded.
Discipline (cards, fouls)OK — deep referee record, but it points two ways
Madley has 21 matches of last season behind him at 3.48 cards and 6.58 fouls per card — a long leash against a divisional 5.60 — and 0.29 penalties per match. Two complications keep this out of the top rating and both are in the record. First, his 2026–27 line reads 4.00 cards on 5.17 fouls per card across three matches: the opposite tendency to his season figure. Second, the 5 red cards he showed in 21 matches were the most of any Premier League official last season — late booking and frequent dismissal are not the same property and both are documented. The clubs both sit well under the divisional card rate: Brighton 2.00 and Arsenal 1.50 against a divisional 3.93, on foul rates of 13.0 and 10.0 against 11.57. His three previous matches with these sides produced 4, 4 and 0 cards.
CornersWell-suited — both rates stable across two seasons, and the mechanisms differ
This is the row where both clubs have rates that survived the summer, which almost nothing else on this card can claim. Arsenal won 5.68 and conceded 3.37 per match last season and win 5.00 and concede 2.75 now — the lowest conceded figure on this board, and the mechanism is in the same data: 9.00 shots faced per match, 4.25 deep completions conceded, a defence that gives up almost nothing. Brighton won 4.87 and conceded 4.82, and now win 5.50 and concede 4.25, on 19.50 shots per match and 70.9% possession. Both figures moved by less than a corner across a summer in which one club replaced its entire centre-back line. The divisional rate is 4.65 per team.

D · Early-season risk notes

  • Brighton’s possession and pressing figures are so far from last season’s that they need saying plainly rather than being used quietly. Possession 70.88% against 54.07%; PPDA 4.56 against 8.88 — they win the ball back inside five opposition passes; and their opponents’ PPDA against them has risen to 24.86 from 14.60, meaning nobody is pressing them back. That is four matches, two of them against Coventry and Leeds, and it is the largest style shift anywhere on this slate. It may be a new team or it may be a fixture list.
  • The centre-back line that produced Brighton’s 46 goals conceded last season is gone in its entirety. Van Hecke (£52m), Coppola (£17m), Igor (£3m) and Webster all left; Vušković, Struijk and Svoboda arrived; Svoboda is a doubt with a knee injury. The 1.25 conceded and 1.85 xG against per match are four matches of a unit assembled in August, and the 25/26 figures beside them describe different players.
  • Arsenal are the opposite risk: their numbers are so stable that the temptation is to treat four matches as thirty-eight. The xG, xGA, PPDA and deep-completion figures do reproduce last season almost exactly, which is real evidence. But the four opponents were Coventry, Aston Villa, Chelsea and Sunderland — two of whom are 19th and 17th on this morning’s table — and Saliba is out, with White and Mosquera doubtful, so the defence that produced the 1 goal conceded may not be the defence that plays.
  • Four of Brighton’s eleven summer signings are unavailable or doubtful — Yohanna (£26m), Azeez (£15m), Hadjam (£11.1m) and Svoboda (£4.3m) — alongside Mitoma, Minteh, Ferguson, Wieffer, Hinshelwood and Tzimas. Ten names is a long list for a side whose attacking rates are the reason this card rates its scoring highly.
  • Most sensitive: the Asian handicap and total goals — both depend on which Brighton turns up, and their four margins are +4, −1, 0, +5. Least sensitive: corners and Arsenal’s side of the BTTS question — the first because both clubs’ corner rates moved less than one corner across a summer of heavy turnover, the second because Arsenal’s 27-goals-conceded season and their 0.84 xG against this season are the same finding measured twice.
