A · Squad, promotion & transfer context

Ipswich Town are promoted, as Championship runners-up on 84 points — 23 wins, 15 draws, 8 defeats, 80 scored, 47 conceded, the second-best defensive record in that division. The Championship treats its promoted sides harshly and this board has no view on whether it will again; what it can say is narrower and firmer. Everton finished 13th in the Premier League on 49 points — 13 wins, 10 draws, 15 defeats, 47 scored, 50 conceded. The structural headline is the shape of the two summers, which is the reverse of what the table would suggest. The promoted club made twelve signings for roughly £150m under a manager appointed on 23 June; the established club sold £108m of players and bought £86.3m, finishing the window a net seller. One of these squads is almost entirely new and the other lost its two leading attackers. Neither one’s 2025–26 record describes it, and for Ipswich the record is not even from this division.

  • Everton — turnover high and concentrated in attack; manager unchanged. David Moyes remains head coach, in his second spell, reappointed in January 2025; no change this window. Out, the headline: Iliman Ndiaye to Manchester City, £60m, Dwight McNeil to Crystal Palace, £22m and Beto to Fiorentina, £15m — the three players who carried the attack. Also out: Iroegbunam to Hull City, fee undisclosed but reported around £15m, which means Everton sold a player to one of the two promoted clubs playing on this card at the same kickoff time. Ten departures in all. In — seven signings, £86.3m of disclosed fees: Brennan Johnson from Crystal Palace, £22m; Merlin Röhl from Freiburg, £21m; Tyrique George from Chelsea, £18m; and Christian Nørgaard from Arsenal, permanent, on 5 August 2026 — a deal confirmed from the other side on this board’s Arsenal card. Net sellers by £22m on disclosed fees. No goalkeeper and no centre-back moved in either direction.
  • Ipswich Town — promoted, and the turnover is total; manager changed 23 June 2026. Kieran McKenna left after four and a half years, having taken them up, citing family; Gary O’Neil was appointed on 23 June 2026 from Strasbourg on a three-year deal. In — twelve players for about £150m: Julio Enciso from Strasbourg, £26m; Emersonn from Toulouse, £24m plus £2.6m; Abdul Fatawu from Leicester, £20m plus £3m; Exequiel Palacios from Bayer Leverkusen, £19.3m plus £4.3m; Abdoul Ouattara from Strasbourg, £17m plus £4.3m; Zian Flemming from Burnley, £17m plus £3m; Florentino Luís from Burnley, £16m plus £3m; Saša Lukić from Fulham, £9m; Kjell Scherpen from Union Saint-Gilloise, £8.5m plus £2.5m — a goalkeeper; Issa Diop from Fulham, £8.5m — a centre-back; Daizen Maeda from Celtic, £8m plus £2m; and Kayne van Oevelen from Volendam, £3.4m plus £800,000. Two of the twelve came from O’Neil’s former club, Strasbourg, who also play on this slate. Out: Arijanet Muric to Sassuolo, £6m — the goalkeeper replaced by Scherpen — George Hirst to Stoke, £6m, Elkan Baggott to Millwall and Harry Clarke to Preston for undisclosed fees, Wes Burns to Leicester for £75,000, and Ashley Young retired. A new goalkeeper, a new centre-back, a new deep midfield and a new forward line, under a manager of twelve weeks.
  • Suspensions / absences. No suspension is reported for either side. Everton: Merlin Röhl and Christian Nørgaard are both unavailable — two of the seven summer signings, £21m and an Arsenal permanent transfer respectively. Harrison Armstrong is expected back after missing the last match with a minor training injury, and Tyler Dibling started the midweek EFL Cup win over Wolves. No injury or suspension list for Ipswich is published by any source reached, which is itself worth stating on a squad twelve players deep in new signings — the predicted eleven contains Scherpen, Diop, Lukić, Palacios, Fatawu, Enciso and Emersonn, seven of the twelve arrivals, so a single absence would change a large fraction of a side nobody has a season of data on.
  • Sample. Everton: six competitive matches, four in the league — 2–0 v Crystal Palace (22 August), 1–1 at Bournemouth (29 August), 2–2 v Manchester United (6 September), 0–0 at Tottenham (12 September) — plus EFL Cup ties at Preston (26 August) and against Wolves (16 September). Ninth on 6 points and unbeaten: one win and three draws, 5 scored and 3 conceded. Ipswich: six competitive matches, four in the league — 2–1 v Sunderland (22 August), 2–5 at Manchester United (30 August), 0–2 v Liverpool (4 September), 3–2 at Crystal Palace (12 September) — plus EFL Cup ties against Leicester (25 August) and Arsenal (15 September). Tenth on 6 points, 7 scored and 10 conceded, with no clean sheet in four. The two clubs have the same points from opposite-looking seasons.
  • Referee. Stuart Attwell, confirmed, with Wade Smith and Steve Meredith on the lines, Ruebyn Ricardo fourth official, Andrew Kitchen on VAR and Josh Smith assisting — a team that matches the Premier League’s own matchweek-5 release exactly. Record, counted from every Premier League match he refereed: 2025–26, 25 matches, 4.72 cards per match on 21.68 fouls — 4.59 fouls per card — 0.32 penalties per match and 3 red cards. Against the division that makes him the heaviest-booking official in the Premier League last season (4.72 against 3.86) and the shortest leash in it (4.59 fouls per card against 5.60). 2026–27 so far, 2 matches, 7.00 cards on 30.00 fouls — 4.29 fouls per card — and 0.50 penalties, which extends the same tendency rather than contradicting it. History: four Everton matches last season, and they produced 7, 6, 5 and 7 cards — an average of 6.25 (Everton–Brighton on 24 August 2025, 7 cards, 22 fouls and a penalty; Fulham–Everton on 7 February, 6; Newcastle–Everton on 28 February, 5; West Ham–Everton on 25 April, 7). No Ipswich match at all — he refereed no Championship fixture last season, so there is no record of him with the away side.
  • Not sourced. Ipswich’s 2025–26 expected goals, for or against — Understat does not cover the Championship and nobody else publishes it, so the xG row on the away side is blank for last season and is not estimated from their goal counts. Fees for two of the six Ipswich departures (Baggott, Clarke) are undisclosed, as is Iroegbunam’s to Hull, where only a press figure of about £15m exists. Any injury or suspension list for Ipswich: no source reached publishes one. No source characterises Attwell’s reading of contact in the box in words; his 0.32 penalties per match is counted, not described. Everything else the spec asks for is published: the Premier League and the Championship both carry complete shot, corner, foul, card and official data, which is how every rate above was counted.

B · Style & early-season reality

Everton are a deep, low-foul block that concedes the ball and does not chase it. Possession 41.67%, against 43.67% last season; PPDA 15.61, against 13.46 — they press less than almost anyone, and their opponents press them at 8.15, the most aggressive figure faced by any side on this board. The consequence shows in the foul column rather than in the possession one: 8.50 fouls per match, the lowest of any club carded on this slate, down from 10.82 last season, because a side that sits off does not have to foul. What has improved is the front end — 14.75 shots per match against 11.11 last season, 5.25 on target against 3.74, and 7.25 deep completions against 5.55 — which is the part that should not be assumed, because the three players who produced last season’s 47 goals (Ndiaye, McNeil, Beto) were all sold and the £22m replacement, Brennan Johnson, has four matches. The record is one win and three draws, with 1.44 xG for and 1.52 against: a side whose chances and results both say “close”.

Ipswich arrive playing a different game from the one that got them promoted, and the numbers say so plainly. In the Championship they had 56.09% of the ball, took 15.07 shots against 10.54 faced, and won 5.70 corners against 3.93 — a side that dominated its division. In the Premier League they have 43.92%, take 11.25 shots against 16.25 faced, and win 3.50 corners against 5.50. Every one of those pairs has inverted. Their PPDA of 11.75 is more aggressive than Everton’s but it is being applied without the ball, which is the ordinary promoted-side experience and is exactly what the Championship record cannot tell you about. The clean-sheet column is the sharpest single illustration: 37.0% in the Championship, the second-best defensive record in that division at 1.02 conceded per match; 0.0% in the Premier League at 2.50 conceded. The evidence that O’Neil’s side is a different team from McKenna’s is a start date, twelve signings and four matches — not a season, and the one figure that would normally arbitrate, last season’s xG, does not exist for them at all.

C · Market-type suitability

1X2 / MoneylineDangerous
Both clubs sit on 6 points and neither record means what it looks like. Everton are unbeaten with one win, from a side that sold its three leading attackers for £97m and replaced them with £61m of new ones. Ipswich are promoted with twelve signings under a manager of twelve weeks, and their 84-point Championship season was produced by a different coach and largely different players. The divisional home-win rate has fallen to 34.1% from 42.6%, with draws at 34.1% — context rather than a finding, but it means the base rate a promoted away side is read against has itself moved. Four matches cannot settle a result here.
Asian handicap / DNBDangerous
Everton’s four margins are +2, 0, 0, 0 — three draws, two of them goalless or near it, and a total goal difference of +2 across four matches. Ipswich’s are +1, −3, −2, +1. A side that has drawn three of four has no margin to read, and a promoted side whose results swing from 3–5 at Manchester United to 3–2 at Crystal Palace has one that is too wide to use. Everton’s home and away split compounds it: 2.00 scored and 1.00 conceded per home match against 0.50 and 0.50 away, across two matches each.
Totals (O/U goals)Split — the two clubs point hard in opposite directions
This is the cleanest disagreement on the card. Everton’s four matches have produced 8 goals — 25.0% over 2.5, the lowest rate of any club carded today — with 1.44 xG for and 1.52 against behind it. Ipswich’s have produced 17 — 75.0% over 2.5 — with 1.51 and 1.93. Both readings are internally consistent, both are four matches deep, and the divisional line of 53.7% sits almost exactly between them. Everton’s 44.7% last season and Ipswich’s 52.2% in the Championship were both near their own divisional baselines, so neither club has a standing tendency to break the tie.
BTTSOK — the away side is well evidenced, the home side is not
Ipswich’s half is documented from several directions at once: no clean sheet in four, 2.50 conceded per match, 1.93 xG against, 16.25 shots faced and 8.00 deep completions conceded — a promoted defence being outshot, with a goalkeeper and centre-back who arrived in the summer. Everton’s half is the weak input and it is weak in an interesting way: they have kept two clean sheets in four on 1.52 xG against, so the clean sheets are running ahead of the chances conceded, and their 28.9% rate last season sat near the divisional line. The scoring side of the question is the softer one for Everton too — 1.25 goals per match from an attack that lost Ndiaye, McNeil and Beto.
Team goalsSplit — neither side’s scoring rate has a usable history
Everton have scored 1.25 per league match against 1.24 last season — an identical figure produced by a completely different forward line, which makes the continuity coincidental rather than informative. Their underlying numbers have improved (14.75 shots against 11.11, 5.25 on target against 3.74) while the goal count has not. Ipswich’s 1.75 per match is their first four matches at this level, and the 1.74 they scored in the Championship is the same number against weaker opposition — a coincidence that should not be read as continuity. One useful fact survives: Ipswich failed to score in only 13.0% of 46 Championship matches and in 25.0% of four here.
Halves (1H result, 1H goals)Split — 1H goals readable, 1H result not
The timing columns separate the two sides more cleanly than the full-time ones. Everton have scored 0.25 first-half goals per match and conceded 0.25 — one each in four matches, against 0.50 and 0.55 last season and a divisional 1.27 per match. Ipswich have scored 1.00 and conceded 1.25, against 0.72 and 0.50 in the Championship. So the home side’s first halves have been almost empty and the away side’s have not, and both figures are consistent with the style read: a deep block that does not press, against a promoted side being outshot. A first-half result needs a margin inside 45 minutes from a side that has drawn three of four.
Discipline (cards, fouls)Well-suited — the deepest and most one-directional evidence on the card
Attwell is the most extreme official on this board and the record behind that is 25 matches: 4.72 cards per match against a divisional 3.86, on 4.59 fouls per card against a divisional 5.60 — the heaviest-booking and shortest leash in the Premier League last season — with 3 reds and 0.32 penalties per match. His 2026–27 line extends it rather than contradicting it: 7.00 cards on 4.29 fouls per card over two matches. And he has four Everton matches on record, producing 7, 6, 5 and 7 cards — 6.25 per match, against Everton’s own season rate of 2.00. The counterweight is real and must be stated: Everton commit 8.50 fouls per match, the lowest figure on this slate, and Ipswich 11.50 against a divisional 11.57; both clubs took around 2.00 cards per match last season. This is a documented collision between a short-leash official with a specific history against this home club and two clubs that do not foul much — which is information either way, and the reason this is the best-evidenced row here.
CornersOK — Everton stable, Ipswich’s rate has inverted
Everton’s corner profile has moved with their shot volume and in the same direction: 5.25 won and 4.50 conceded per match, against 4.42 and 5.13 last season — from conceding more than they won to winning more than they concede, consistent with 14.75 shots against 11.11. Ipswich’s has inverted completely: 5.70 won and 3.93 conceded in the Championship, against 3.50 and 5.50 now. That inversion is the single clearest measure on this card of what changing division has done to them, and it agrees with possession (56.09% to 43.92%) and shots (15.07 to 11.25). The divisional rate is 4.65 per team here and was 5.17 in the Championship. Both sets of figures are trustworthy; the problem is that only one of Ipswich’s describes this division.

D · Early-season risk notes

  • Ipswich’s entire 2025–26 record is from a different division, and one column of it does not exist at all. The 84 points, 80 goals, 37.0% clean-sheet rate and 5.70 corners were produced in the Championship against a 2.59-goal, 3.85-card divisional baseline. No 2025–26 xG exists for them in any public source, so the one measure that normally survives a change of level is missing. Every Championship figure in the strip is labelled as such for that reason.
  • Everton’s goals-for rate is identical across the two seasons and the coincidence is misleading. 1.24 per match last season, 1.25 this — but Ndiaye (£60m), McNeil (£22m) and Beto (£15m) all left, and Johnson, George and Röhl arrived, with Röhl and Nørgaard unavailable today. The shot and deep-completion columns have risen sharply while the goal count has not; four matches cannot say which of those is the truer signal.
  • Twelve signings is more turnover than four matches can describe. Ipswich’s predicted eleven contains seven summer arrivals including the goalkeeper and a centre-back, under a coach appointed on 23 June. No injury list for them is published anywhere reached, so the composition of the side is both more uncertain than usual and less reported than usual.
  • The discipline row rests on a referee pattern that is deep but specific. Attwell’s 25-match record and his four Everton matches are real and counted, but four matches is four matches, and the clubs’ own foul rates — 8.50 and 11.50 — point the other way. The row is rated highly because the evidence on both sides of the collision is unusually good, not because the outcome is settled.
  • Most sensitive: the Asian handicap and total goals — the first because Everton have drawn three of four and have a goal difference of +2, the second because the two clubs’ over-2.5 rates are 25.0% and 75.0% with the divisional line sitting exactly between them. Least sensitive: the discipline row and Ipswich’s side of BTTS — the first because it rests on 25 refereed matches plus four specific ones against this home club, the second because no clean sheet in four is corroborated by xG against, shots faced and deep completions conceded all at once.

E · Short market-type guide

  • The discipline family is more robust than the match result here because Attwell has 25 matches at 4.72 cards and 4.59 fouls per card plus four Everton matches averaging 6.25 cards, while the result depends on a promoted side twelve signings deep and a home side that sold £97m of its attack.
  • Ipswich’s defensive side of BTTS is more robust than a total on this match because no clean sheet in four is backed by 1.93 xG against, 16.25 shots faced and 8.00 deep completions conceded, whereas the two clubs’ over-2.5 rates (25.0% and 75.0%) straddle the divisional 53.7% and cancel out.
  • A first-half total is more robust than a first-half result because both clubs have timing rates for two seasons and they differ sharply — Everton 0.25 for and against, Ipswich 1.00 and 1.25 — while the result question inherits Everton’s three draws in four.
  • Everton’s corner rate is more robust than their goal rate because 5.25 won and 4.50 conceded moved in step with their shot volume rising from 11.11 to 14.75, whereas their goals-for figure is unchanged at 1.24–1.25 despite the entire forward line being sold.
  • The single fact that would most change which family I choose is a published Ipswich expected-goals figure for 2025–26. Without it there is no way to tell whether their Championship defensive record — 47 conceded in 46, a 37.0% clean-sheet rate — reflected a strong defence or a strong division position, and therefore no way to read the 2.50 they are conceding now as regression or as level. What would settle it is Understat or an equivalent extending coverage to the Championship, or a club-published season xG; neither exists in any source reached today.

A · Squad, promotion & transfer context

Hull City are promoted, through the play-offs rather than automatically, and are back in the top flight after a ten-year absence. They finished 6th in the Championship on 73 points — 21 wins, 10 draws, 15 defeats, 70 scored, 66 conceded — then beat Millwall over two legs and Middlesbrough in the final at Wembley, keeping three consecutive clean sheets through the run. Sixth place with a goal difference of +4 is the weakest league record of any side promoted from this division in either direction on this slate. Newcastle finished 12th in the Premier League on 49 points — 14 wins, 7 draws, 17 defeats, 53 scored, 55 conceded. The structural headline is that both clubs arrive almost unrecognisable, for opposite reasons. Newcastle sold roughly £236m of midfield and attack in one window and changed head coach in August; Hull made eighteen signings. Neither squad can be read off last season’s record, and for Hull the record is not even from this division.

  • Newcastle — turnover extreme and concentrated on selling; manager changed in early August 2026. Eddie Howe stepped down on 31 July 2026 and Matthias Jaissle was appointed on 5 August 2026 from Al-Ahli, where he had won consecutive Asian Champions League Elite titles, and before that Red Bull Salzburg — a fact carried from this board’s own card of 14 September (fx_0914_f4) and confirmed independently by the Premier League’s 2026/27 manager listing. Out, and this is the whole story: Sandro Tonali to Tottenham for an initial £92.5m rising to £100m; Bruno Guimarães to Arsenal for £75m; Anthony Gordon to Barcelona for £69.3m; Kieran Trippier left as a free agent. That is the entire central midfield and the leading wide attacker, for roughly £236m, and two of the three buying clubs’ sides play on this card — Tottenham at 13:30 and Arsenal at 16:00. In: Bazoumana Touré, reported at £42m and described as at the top of the club’s range; Ewen Jaouen from Reims, £18.5m; plus Sean Steur, Aladji Bamba and Lukas Hornicek, fees not published by any source reached. A club that banked £236m and spent something over £60m of it on five players with no Premier League record between them.
  • Hull City — promoted, eighteen signings, and the manager is the one thing that did not change. Sergej Jakirović was appointed in June 2025, took them up in his first English season, and signed a new two-year contract on 4 September 2026 running to summer 2028 with an option to 2029. He is therefore a promotion-winning coach retained and extended, not a new appointment — the opposite of Newcastle’s position. In — eighteen players, and the ones with published fees or clubs are: Nobel Mendy from Rayo Vallecano, €23.4m; Ilyas Ansah from Union Berlin, €17m; Iroegbunam from Everton, fee undisclosed but reported around £15m; Konstantinos Tzolakis, a goalkeeper, from Olympiacos; Mohamed-Ali Cho from Nice; and Reda Laalaoui from FUS Rabat, £400,000, the first signing of the window on a four-year deal. Three of those transfers connect directly to this board: Mendy comes from Rayo, who play at 14:00 today; Iroegbunam from Everton, who kick off at this same hour; and Ansah from Union Berlin, whose sale was documented on this board’s card of 18 September. Out: Ivor Pandur to Rangers, £6m — a goalkeeper, replaced by Tzolakis — Aidon Shehu to Panathinaikos, £2.5m, and Akin Famewo to Bolton for an undisclosed fee. Jakirović was still publicly expecting “three or four” more signings late in the window.
  • Suspensions / absences. No injury or suspension list for either club is published by any source reached — an unusual gap for a Premier League fixture and one this card will not fill. The one preview located notes only that “Newcastle’s back line is the obvious focus after conceding four times at Leeds”, naming Botman and Thiaw, which is commentary rather than team news. That same preview names Hull’s head coach as somebody other than Jakirović, which four independent sources — the club, the Premier League’s own manager listing, Anadolu Agency and beIN Sports — contradict, so the preview is treated as unreliable throughout and its predicted elevens are not used here.
  • Sample. Newcastle: six competitive matches, four in the league — 2–2 v Liverpool (23 August), 2–0 at Tottenham (29 August), 2–2 v Bournemouth (5 September), 1–4 at Leeds (14 September) — plus EFL Cup ties against West Bromwich Albion (26 August) and at Millwall (8 September). Twelfth on 5 points, 7 scored and 8 conceded. Hull: six competitive matches, four in the league — 2–0 v Manchester United (22 August), 1–0 at Coventry (29 August), 0–0 v Aston Villa (5 September), 2–2 at Chelsea (12 September) — plus EFL Cup ties at Stoke (25 August) and at Sunderland (8 September). Fifth on 8 points, unbeaten, with 5 scored and 2 conceded — the best defensive record of any club carded on this slate, from the side with the least of the ball.
  • Referee. Not published against this fixture, and this is a specific situation rather than a general absence. The Premier League released the officiating teams for matchweek 5 on 14 September — ten complete quartets with VAR — but its own release does not say which team takes which match. Three of the ten have since been matched by fixture-specific reporting: Sam Barrott to Tottenham–Aston Villa, Andy Madley to Brighton–Arsenal and Stuart Attwell to Everton–Ipswich, all carded elsewhere on this board. No source reached names an official against Newcastle–Hull. A search-engine summary did offer a full mapping, but it has already been shown wrong twice against fixture-specific sources on this same round, so it is not used. By the research spec that makes the Discipline row evidence-thin for this fixture regardless of what the clubs’ own rates say, and it is rated accordingly below. The seven unmatched officials for the round are England, Harrington, Gillett, Oliver, Herczeg, Jones and Bankes, whose 2025–26 card rates range from 3.10 per match (Oliver) to 4.43 (England) — a spread wide enough that not knowing which of them is here matters.
  • Not sourced. Hull’s 2025–26 expected goals, for or against — Understat does not cover the Championship and no substitute exists, so the xG row is blank on the away side for last season and is not inferred from their goal counts. The referee appointment, as above. Any injury or suspension list for either club. Fees for at least twelve of Hull’s eighteen arrivals and for three of Newcastle’s five, none of which is published by the sources reached. Newcastle’s total outlay is therefore not stated here, only the four fees that are on record. Everything else the spec asks for is published and was counted from the match record rather than read off a table.

B · Style & early-season reality

Newcastle are a side whose shape survived the summer and whose quality apparently did not. Possession has fallen from 52.56% to 47.10% and PPDA is almost unchanged at 10.41 against 9.97 — they press the same amount — but the output either side of that has collapsed. Shots are down from 12.95 to 9.75 per match and shots faced up from 12.37 to 19.00; expected goals have fallen from 1.60 to 1.04 and expected goals against risen from 1.51 to 2.05, which is the largest two-way swing of any club carded today. Corners tell the same story from the other end: 6.08 won and 4.92 conceded last season, 4.50 and 5.75 now. The mechanism is not mysterious and it is in section A rather than in the tactics — Tonali, Guimarães and Gordon accounted for the central midfield and the leading wide threat, and left for £236m between them. The one column that has risen is the disciplinary one: 14.25 fouls per match against 10.16, and 2.75 cards against 1.74, which is what a side that has lost its midfield control tends to do.

Hull are playing the most extreme version of a low block anywhere on this board, and it is working so far in a way their own chance numbers only partly support. 29.62% possession — the lowest figure on this slate by eleven points — against 45.02% in the Championship; PPDA 17.91, meaning they barely press at all; 2.25 deep completions per match against 8.50 conceded; 9.50 shots against 14.25 faced. They beat Manchester United 2–0 with 28.5% of the ball and 8 shots to 21. The results are a 75% clean-sheet rate and 0.50 conceded per match; the chances say 1.45 expected goals against, so they are conceding about a goal a game less than their opponents’ chances imply. The corner column is the clearest signature of the shape: 2.50 won and 6.75 conceded per match, the most lopsided corner deficit on this card. The evidence that this is a deliberate system rather than early-season luck is four matches of possession, pressing and shot data all agreeing, against a Championship season in which the same coach had 45% of the ball — so the shape is new even though the coach is not, and the one measure that would arbitrate, last season’s xG, does not exist for them.

C · Market-type suitability

1X2 / MoneylineDangerous
The table has the promoted side above the established one — Hull 5th on 8 points and unbeaten, Newcastle 12th on 5 — and neither position rests on evidence that transfers. Newcastle sold £236m of midfield and attack and changed coach five weeks before the season; their underlying numbers have moved further than any club carded today, from 1.60 xG to 1.04 and from 1.51 against to 2.05. Hull’s record is four matches at 29.6% possession following a 6th-place Championship season with a goal difference of +4. The divisional home-win rate is 34.1% against 42.6% last season, so even the base rate has shifted. Nothing here supports a result.
Asian handicap / DNBDangerous
Newcastle’s four margins are 0, +2, 0, −3; Hull’s are +2, +1, 0, 0. Six of the eight are a draw or a one-to-two goal result and the outlier is a 1–4 defeat at Leeds. A handicap also needs the two sides’ levels to be comparable in the same units, and here one side’s comparison set is the Championship. Newcastle’s home record last season was 1.89 scored and 1.58 conceded per match; the team that produced it has lost its entire central midfield. Hull’s away figures come from a division where they had 45% of the ball rather than 30%.
Totals (O/U goals)Split — two well-evidenced readings pointing opposite ways
Both halves are documented and they disagree completely. Newcastle’s four matches have produced 15 goals — 75.0% over 2.5, extending a 63.2% rate last season — with 1.04 xG for and 2.05 against behind it, and 19.00 shots faced per match. Hull’s have produced 7 — 25.0% over 2.5 — from 9.50 shots and 14.25 faced, at 29.6% possession. The divisional line of 53.7% sits between them. The complication worth naming is that Hull’s Championship rate was 61.2% over 2.5, so their low-scoring matches are a property of this season and this shape, not of the club — four matches old, on a style that is itself four matches old.
BTTSSplit — the home side is settled, the away side is not
Newcastle’s half is about as well evidenced as a four-match figure gets, because it agrees with a 38-match one: both teams have scored in 3 of 4 (75.0%) after 65.8% across the whole of last season, they have not failed to score in either sample, and they have kept one clean sheet in four on 2.05 xG against. Hull’s half is the opposite and rests on the gap this card keeps returning to: three clean sheets in four at 0.50 conceded, against 1.45 expected goals conceded. Their Championship clean-sheet rate was 28.6%, near that division’s 25.0% baseline, so 75% is not a standing property of this side. One side of this question has two seasons behind it and the other has four matches and a contradiction.
Team goalsSplit — asymmetric, and the asymmetry is about who left
Newcastle scored 53 last season at 1.39 per match on 1.60 xG, and are scoring 1.75 now on 1.04 xG — a rising goal count on a falling chance count, from an attack missing Gordon, Tonali and Guimarães. Of the two numbers, the xG is the one with the mechanism behind it: 9.75 shots and 3.25 on target per match, down from 12.95 and 4.63. Hull have scored 1.25 per match on 1.14 xG, which is internally consistent but comes from 9.50 shots and 2.25 deep completions — the lowest creation figures on this board. Neither club’s scoring rate has a usable history in this division in its current form.
Halves (1H result, 1H goals)OK — 1H goals well separated, 1H result not readable
The timing columns divide these sides more cleanly than the full-time ones and in a way the style read predicts. Newcastle have scored 0.50 first-half goals per match and conceded 1.25; Hull have scored 1.00 and conceded 0.25 — the promoted side is ahead on first-half goal difference and the established one is behind, against a divisional 1.27 per match. Both clubs ran near 0.74–0.82 for and 0.55–0.65 against in their respective divisions last season, so both figures have moved and they have moved in opposite directions. That is enough to read a first-half total. A first-half result needs a margin from two sides with a combined six draws or one-goal results in eight.
Discipline (cards, fouls)Dangerous — the official is not identifiable
No source reached names a referee for this fixture, and by the research spec a cards or fouls rating that cannot reference the official is incomplete. That is the whole rating and it holds despite the club evidence being unusually good. Hull take 3.25 cards per match, the highest rate of any club carded on this slate, on only 10.00 fouls — 3.08 fouls per card, which is a very heavy booking rate for a low-foul side and is what a deep block that defends its box tends to produce. Newcastle take 2.75 on 14.25 fouls, both figures sharply up from 1.74 and 10.16 last season. Against a divisional 3.93 cards and 11.57 fouls per team, two clubs trending upward is a real signal. But the seven officials still unmatched for this round ranged from 3.10 to 4.43 cards per match last season and from 4.59 to 7.39 fouls per card — a spread wider than the gap between the clubs themselves. Until one of them is named against this match, the largest input in the row is unknown.
CornersWell-suited — the most mechanised asymmetry on the card
Hull win 2.50 corners per match and concede 6.75 — the most lopsided corner deficit anywhere on this board, and it is corroborated by every other column describing the same shape: 29.62% possession, 9.50 shots against 14.25 faced, 2.25 deep completions against 8.50 conceded, PPDA 17.91. Their per-match corner counts read 1, 2, 3, 4 against 6, 6, 4, 11, so the pattern repeats rather than resting on one match. Newcastle have moved the other way — 4.50 won and 5.75 conceded against 6.08 and 4.92 last season — and that too tracks their shot and possession decline rather than sitting on top of it. The divisional rate is 4.65 per team. This is the one family here where four matches of data point the same way as the style evidence, the transfer ledger and the previous season all at once.

D · Early-season risk notes

  • Hull’s entire 2025–26 record is from a different division, and the xG column of it does not exist. The 73 points, 6th place, 28.6% clean-sheet rate and 61.2% over-2.5 rate were produced in the Championship against a 2.59-goal, 3.85-card baseline, over 49 matches including the play-offs. No public source publishes Championship expected goals, so the one measure that normally survives a change of division is missing, and this card does not estimate it.
  • Hull are conceding about a goal a game fewer than their opponents’ chances imply. Three clean sheets in four and 0.50 conceded per match, against 1.45 expected goals conceded and 14.25 shots faced. That gap is the single most load-bearing number on the away side, it is four matches old, and every rate that depends on it — the clean-sheet column, the BTTS figure, the 25.0% over-2.5 — inherits it.
  • Newcastle’s 2025–26 numbers describe a midfield that was sold entire. Tonali (£92.5m–£100m), Guimarães (£75m) and Gordon (£69.3m) all left, Trippier left free, and Eddie Howe stepped down on 31 July with Jaissle appointed on 5 August. Five replacements arrived with no Premier League record between them and published fees for only two of them. The 1.39 goals and 1.45 conceded per match in the strip belong to a different team.
  • The referee is appointed but not publicly matched to this fixture, which is a narrower problem than an unappointed official and a real one all the same. The league published ten officiating teams without a mapping; three have been matched elsewhere on this board by fixture-specific reporting; the remaining seven span 3.10 to 4.43 cards per match. Anything on this card that depends on the official — and the discipline row does entirely — is unresolved.
  • Most sensitive: the discipline row, which has no identified official at all, and the Asian handicap, which needs a margin from two clubs whose comparison seasons are in different divisions. Least sensitive: corners — Hull’s 2.50 won and 6.75 conceded repeats across all four matches and is corroborated by possession, shots, deep completions and PPDA simultaneously, and Newcastle’s decline from 6.08 to 4.50 tracks their own shot volume rather than standing apart from it.

E · Short market-type guide

  • Corners are more robust than the match result here because Hull’s 2.50-for and 6.75-against repeats in every one of their four matches and is explained by four independent columns — 29.6% possession, 9.50 shots, 2.25 deep completions, PPDA 17.91 — while the result depends on a Newcastle side that lost £236m of midfield and a coach in the same six weeks.
  • Newcastle’s side of BTTS is more robust than Hull’s because their 75% both-scored rate this season extends a 65.8% rate across 38 matches last season, whereas Hull’s 75% clean-sheet rate contradicts both their own Championship figure (28.6%) and their own expected goals against (1.45 per match).
  • A first-half total is more robust than a first-half result because both clubs have timing rates for two seasons and they now point opposite ways — Newcastle 0.50 for and 1.25 against, Hull 1.00 and 0.25 — while the result question inherits six draws or one-goal margins in eight matches.
  • Newcastle’s expected goals are more robust than their goal count because 1.04 xG per match is supported by 9.75 shots and 3.25 on target, both down sharply from 12.95 and 4.63, whereas the 1.75 goals they have actually scored runs ahead of every creation figure they have.
  • The single fact that would most change which family I choose is the identity of the referee. Hull take 3.25 cards per match on 10.00 fouls and Newcastle 2.75 on 14.25, both well up on last season — that is a documented collision waiting for its third input, and the seven officials still unmatched for this round range from 3.10 to 4.43 cards per match and 4.59 to 7.39 fouls per card. What would settle it is the Premier League publishing its matchweek-5 officials against fixtures rather than as an unordered list, or any club or press source naming the appointment for this match — neither exists in any source reached today.

A · Squad, promotion & transfer context

Neither club is promoted and neither is new to this division. Athletic Club finished 12th in 2025–26 on 45 points — 13 wins, 6 draws, 19 defeats, 43 scored, 58 conceded, a season in which they conceded eleven goals more than their expected-goals-against of 1.24 per match implied and kept a clean sheet in 15.8% of matches. Alavés finished 14th on 43 points — 11 wins, 10 draws, 17 defeats, 44 scored, 56 conceded, with a 13.2% clean-sheet rate. Those are the two weakest clean-sheet records in LaLiga last season and they belong to the two clubs in this fixture. The structural headline is that the club with the new head coach is the one that signed nobody. Athletic replaced Ernesto Valverde with Edin Terzić and then made no permanent signings at all, selling €6m of squad and finishing the window €10.5m up. Alavés kept Quique Sánchez Flores and spent a net €7m. One of these sides has a new idea and the old players; the other has the old idea and slightly different players.

  • Athletic Club — turnover low and entirely outgoing; manager changed, announced 5 May 2026. Ernesto Valverde confirmed in March 2026 that he would leave at the end of the season, ending his third spell after four years, with his contract expiring on 30 June 2026. Edin Terzić was announced as his successor on 5 May 2026 on a two-year deal to 30 June 2028 — the former Borussia Dortmund coach, German Cup winner in 2021 and a Champions League finalist in 2024, out of work since leaving Dortmund in June 2024. In — nothing. Athletic made no permanent signing in the summer of 2026. The squad additions are five players returning from loan: Julen Agirrezabala, Peio Canales, Álvaro Djaló, Hugo Rincón and Unai Vencedor. Out: Unai Gómez to Udinese, €4.5m — the player Udinese named as Arthur Atta’s replacement, and Udinese play on this card at 15:00 — Urko Izeta to Cádiz, €1.5m, Mikel Vesga free to Almería, Íñigo Lekue retired at the end of his contract, Andoni Gorosabel on loan to Espanyol, Adama Boiro on loan to West Ham, and Unai Vencedor’s contract rescinded to Burgos. Net €10.5m incoming. Contradiction reported, not resolved: one source lists Agirrezabala as returning from a loan at Valencia, another as having left for Racing Santander — who also play today; and the same source gives Terzić’s appointment as 5 May 2025 where five Spanish outlets give 5 May 2026. The 2026 date is used because five independent sources carry it and the 2025 one is a single transcription.
  • Deportivo Alavés — turnover low, one of the quietest windows in the division; manager unchanged. Quique Sánchez Flores remains head coach, no change this window — consistent with this board’s own card of 15 September, which recorded him as LaLiga’s manager of the month for August. In: Miguel Rodríguez, a forward, from Utrecht — the club’s largest outlay of the window; Nicolás Valentini from Fiorentina, €3m; and Mikel Rodríguez from Real Sociedad’s academy, signed as Jon Guridi’s replacement in midfield. Ville Koski was retained. Out: Víctor Parada, the club’s largest sale at up to €5m; Jon Guridi free at the end of his contract; and Asier Villalibre made permanent at Racing Santander — who play at 18:30 today. Net spend −€7m, and the Spanish press describes them as one of the least active clubs in the division this summer.
  • Suspensions / absences. No suspension is reported for either side. Athletic are missing two defenders: Daniel Vivian (muscular, out until October) and Unai Egiluz (anterior cruciate ligament, right knee, out until January). Alavés are missing three, and two of them are season-ending: Facundo Garcés (ACL, right knee, out until May), Mikel Rodríguez (ACL, right knee, out until May) and Aitor Mañas (cranioencephalic trauma). Mikel Rodríguez is one of the three players Alavés signed this summer, brought in specifically to replace Guridi, and he is out for the season — so a club that made three signings has effectively made two, and the midfield replacement is not among them. Both clubs are missing a centre-back from a defence that had the division’s worst clean-sheet record last season.
  • Sample. Athletic: five competitive matches, all league — 1–3 v Sevilla (22 August), 0–2 at Barcelona (27 August), 2–0 at Celta Vigo (30 August), 3–0 v Atlético Madrid (5 September), 1–1 v Elche (12 September). Eighth on 7 points, 7 scored and 6 conceded. Alavés: six competitive matches, all league — 3–0 v Getafe (15 August), 1–1 at Rayo Vallecano (20 August), 1–0 v Villarreal (28 August), 5–2 v Osasuna (6 September), 1–2 at Racing Santander (12 September), 0–1 v Valencia (15 September). Sixth on 10 points, 11 scored and 6 conceded. Alavés’ home record is the sharpest split on the card: 2.25 scored and 0.75 conceded across four at Mendizorroza, against 1.00 and 1.50 in two away.
  • Referee. Víctor García Verdura, confirmed by the matchday listing. Record, counted from every LaLiga match he refereed in the ESPN record: 2025–26, 19 matches, 3.58 cards per match on 24.05 fouls — 6.72 fouls per card — 0.16 penalties per match and 4 red cards. Against the division that makes him the most lenient regular official in LaLiga on both counts: 3.58 cards against a divisional 4.52, and 6.72 fouls per card against a divisional 5.58. His 0.16 penalties per match was the lowest rate of any official with more than fifteen appointments. 2026–27 so far, 3 matches, 3.00 cards on 23.00 fouls — 7.67 fouls per card — and 0.33 penalties, which extends the tendency. History with these clubs is unusually deep: six matches last season, four of them Alavés. Alavés–Atlético Madrid on 30 August 2025 (5 cards, 27 fouls, one penalty), Rayo–Alavés on 26 October (4), Alavés–Real Madrid on 14 December (4), Real Sociedad–Alavés on 11 April (7); and two Athletic matches, Rayo–Athletic on 28 February (5) and Athletic–Osasuna on 21 April (4 cards, one penalty). Those six averaged 4.83 cards — noticeably above his own 3.58 season rate, which is worth stating rather than smoothing away.
  • Not sourced. The reason Athletic have a league fixture outstanding — the division is at jornada 7 and they have played five; no source reached states why, and no explanation is invented here. Fees for Alavés’ two largest pieces of business: Miguel Rodríguez’s arrival from Utrecht is described as the club’s biggest outlay without a figure, and Víctor Parada’s sale is given only as “up to €5m”. Everything else the spec asks for is published by this division and was counted from the match record.

B · Style & early-season reality

Athletic under Terzić are creating far more and pressing far less, which is an unusual combination and a measurable one. Expected goals have risen from 1.46 to 1.96 per match and non-penalty xG from 1.35 to 1.96 — the largest attacking improvement of any club carded on this slate — while PPDA has moved from 10.10 to 13.54, meaning they allow opponents a third more passes before engaging. Possession is almost unchanged at 49.24% against 48.56%. The defensive side has gone the other way and the deep-completions column says how: 4.00 conceded per match last season, 7.60 now, with xG against up from 1.24 to 1.53, on a back line missing Vivian until October and Egiluz until January. Corners follow the same shape: 6.40 won and 5.60 conceded, against 5.76 and 3.87 last season — more won, and considerably more given up. The foul column is the one clear improvement: 10.60 per match against 13.68, well below a divisional 12.36. The evidence that this is Terzić’s side rather than Valverde’s is five matches and a change of pressing scheme, applied to a squad with not one new permanent player in it — which makes it a cleaner natural experiment than most, and a very small one.

Alavés have changed nothing and improved at both ends, which is the harder thing to explain and the easier thing to measure. Expected goals up from 1.41 to 1.77, expected goals against down from 1.39 to 1.02 — and non-penalty xG against down to 0.90, the lowest figure of any club carded today. The mechanism is in the pressing and shot rows: PPDA 10.51 against 10.38 — unchanged — but shots faced down from 12.47 to 10.17 and deep completions conceded from 6.05 to 6.33, essentially flat, while their own shots have risen from 12.18 to 14.17 and shots on target from 4.03 to 5.17. So the improvement is in the quality of what they concede rather than the volume, on a side that has lost two centre-backs to season-ending knee injuries and whose one midfield signing is among them. Possession has fallen from 48.03% to 43.95% and the foul rate from 14.92 to 13.67, both consistent with a side sitting a little deeper. Six matches, one coach, almost the same squad: of the two sides here, this is the one whose current numbers have the fewest confounders — and its clean-sheet rate last season was still the worst in the division.

C · Market-type suitability

1X2 / MoneylineOK — asymmetric, and the asymmetry is unusually clean
One side of this fixture is a genuine natural experiment and the other is a known quantity. Athletic have a new head coach and not one new permanent player, so anything that has changed in their numbers is attributable to Terzić rather than to personnel — and their xG has risen 0.50 per match while their xG against has risen 0.29. Alavés kept their coach and made three signings, one of whom is out until May, and have improved at both ends. Against that, both clubs finished 12th and 14th with the division’s two worst clean-sheet rates, and the sample is five and six matches. The divisional home-win rate of 45.0% against 48.9% last season has barely moved, which is the one stable input here.
Asian handicap / DNBDangerous
Athletic’s five margins are −2, −2, +2, +3, 0 — two heavy defeats, two comfortable wins and a draw, with nothing in between. Alavés’ six are +3, 0, +1, +3, −1, −1. Both sides produce three-goal results and one-goal results and almost nothing else, which is the distribution a handicap reads worst. Alavés’ venue split compounds it: 2.25 scored and 0.75 conceded per home match against 1.00 and 1.50 away, over four and two matches. Athletic’s own away record last season was 1.11 scored and 1.95 conceded, from a team with a different coach.
Totals (O/U goals)OK — both clubs below the divisional line, and both xG lines argue the other way
The rates agree with each other and disagree with the chance data, which is the honest state of this row. Athletic are at 40.0% over 2.5 and Alavés at 50.0%, both under a divisional 56.7%, and both were at 47.4% and 50.0% last season against a 50.0% baseline — so neither club has ever been a high-total side. But their combined expected goals this season are 1.96 and 1.77 per match, both above the divisional 1.64, and the division is running at 3.07 goals per match against 2.69 last season. Two stable club rates pointing low, two rising chance lines and a rising divisional baseline pointing high.
BTTSOK — last season is emphatic, this season contradicts it
The long-run evidence here is as strong as anything on the board and it is all in one direction: these two clubs had the two lowest clean-sheet rates in LaLiga last season — Athletic 15.8%, Alavés 13.2%, against a divisional 25.0% — and Alavés had both teams score in 63.2% of matches. Thirty-eight matches each. This season both have improved sharply: 40.0% and 33.3% clean sheets, and BTTS at 40.0% and 50.0% against a divisional 56.7%. The improvement is corroborated by chances on the Alavés side — 1.02 xG against, down from 1.39 — and contradicted on the Athletic side, whose xG against has risen to 1.53. A large old sample and a small new one saying opposite things is exactly the state that keeps this out of the top rating.
Team goalsWell-suited — both attacks are improving and the chance data says so
This is the family where the two sides agree and the mechanism is visible on both. Athletic have gone from 1.13 goals and 1.46 xG per match to 1.40 and 1.96; Alavés from 1.16 and 1.41 to 1.83 and 1.77. Both improvements are supported by shot volume — Athletic 14.00 shots against 13.74, Alavés 14.17 against 12.18 — and by shots on target, where Alavés have moved from 4.03 to 5.17. Crucially, both clubs are now scoring slightly under their own expected goals rather than over them (1.40 against 1.96; 1.83 against 1.77), so neither rate depends on finishing running hot. Failed-to-score rates have fallen from 34.2% and 26.3% to 20.0% and 16.7%. Five and six matches is the limit on this, but every column inside it points the same way.
Halves (1H result, 1H goals)OK — 1H goals well below the divisional line on both sides
Both clubs are unusually quiet before the break and both have been for two seasons, which makes this one of the steadier rows. Athletic have scored 0.40 first-half goals per match and conceded 0.40, against 0.47 and 0.58 last season; Alavés 0.67 and 0.17, against 0.53 and 0.50. The divisional rate is 1.43 per match, so a fixture between two sides whose combined first-half total runs at about 1.6 goals per match between them sits clearly under it, in both samples. Alavés’ 0.17 conceded before half-time — one goal in six matches — is the outlier and is four times better than their own last-season figure. A first-half result needs a margin inside 45 minutes and neither club supplies one.
Discipline (cards, fouls)Well-suited — three deep records that point the same way, with one caveat
García Verdura is the most lenient regular official in LaLiga: 3.58 cards per match over 19 matches last season against a divisional 4.52, on 6.72 fouls per card against 5.58, and 0.16 penalties per match — the lowest rate of any official with more than fifteen appointments. His 2026–27 line extends it at 3.00 cards and 7.67 fouls per card. Both clubs agree: Athletic take 2.20 cards and Alavés 2.33, against a divisional 4.48, and both were at 2.11 and 2.39 last season — and Athletic’s foul rate has fallen sharply from 13.68 to 10.60, the lowest in this fixture. Three independent records, two seasons deep on each, all pointing low. The caveat is specific and is not smoothed away: his six previous matches involving these two clubs produced 5, 4, 4, 5, 7 and 4 cards — an average of 4.83, well above his own 3.58.
CornersOK — Alavés stable, Athletic’s conceded figure has moved sharply
Alavés are as steady here as anywhere: 5.11 won and 5.08 conceded last season, 4.83 and 5.33 now, against a divisional 4.94 and 4.83 — a club that sits within half a corner of the divisional average at both ends in both seasons. Athletic have moved at one end only: 5.76 won and 3.87 conceded last season, 6.40 and 5.60 now — the corners won have risen with their shot volume and expected goals, which is coherent, but the corners conceded have risen by 1.73 per match, tracking their deep completions conceded from 4.00 to 7.60. Both of those Athletic movements are five matches old and both are explained by the same change in pressing scheme, which is the reason this is rated OK rather than higher.

D · Early-season risk notes

  • Athletic’s 2025–26 numbers were produced by a different head coach and are the one set on this card where the squad is genuinely the same. Terzić was announced on 5 May 2026 and made no permanent signing; the five additions are loan returns. That makes the five-match sample unusually interpretable — every change is coaching rather than personnel — and unusually small. A 0.50-goal rise in expected goals per match over five matches is one good afternoon away from looking different.
  • Both defences are missing a centre-back and both were the worst in the division last season. Athletic are without Vivian until October and Egiluz until January; Alavés have lost Garcés and Mikel Rodríguez to anterior cruciate ligament injuries until May, one of them a summer signing brought in to replace Guridi. The clean-sheet rates in the strip — 15.8% and 13.2% last season, 40.0% and 33.3% now — describe units that are neither what they were nor at full strength.
  • Alavés’ expected goals against have fallen 0.37 per match with no change of coach and almost no change of squad, which is the sort of improvement that most often reverts. It is corroborated by shots faced falling from 12.47 to 10.17, but their deep completions conceded are essentially flat at 6.33 against 6.05 — so the volume of opposition entries into their box has not changed, only what has come of it.
  • Athletic’s outstanding fixture is an unexplained gap in the record. The division has reached jornada 7 and they have played five matches. Every Athletic rate on this card is therefore over five matches where Alavés’ is over six, and no source reached says why the fixture is outstanding or when it will be played.
  • The division has moved further than either club. LaLiga is running at 3.07 goals per match and 56.7% over 2.5 this season against 2.69 and 50.0% last, on 60 matches against 380. Both clubs sit below the new divisional line on totals and BTTS while their own expected goals sit above it — the disagreement in the totals row is really a disagreement between two baselines.
  • Most sensitive: the Asian handicap and BTTS — the first because both clubs produce three-goal results and one-goal results with nothing between, the second because a 38-match record saying both sides always concede is contradicted by a five- and six-match record saying they no longer do. Least sensitive: the discipline row and team goals — the first because a 19-match referee record and two two-season club card rates all point low together, the second because both attacks have improved on shots, shots on target and expected goals simultaneously and neither is outscoring its chances.

E · Short market-type guide

  • Team goals are more robust than total goals here because each club’s attacking improvement is corroborated by its own shots, shots on target and expected goals — Athletic 1.46 to 1.96 xG, Alavés 1.41 to 1.77 — whereas the total depends on reconciling two club over-2.5 rates below the divisional line with two xG lines above it.
  • The discipline row is more robust than BTTS here because García Verdura’s 19 matches at 3.58 cards and 0.16 penalties sit alongside two club card rates that have been near 2.2 for two seasons, while the BTTS question has a 38-match record and a six-match record saying opposite things.
  • A first-half total is more robust than a first-half result because both clubs have been well under the divisional first-half rate in both seasons — Athletic 0.47 then 0.40 for, Alavés 0.53 then 0.67 — while no margin inside 45 minutes is readable from five and six matches.
  • Alavés’ corner rate is more robust than Athletic’s because theirs has sat within half a corner of the divisional average at both ends across 38 matches and 6, whereas Athletic’s conceded figure has risen 1.73 per match in five matches under a new pressing scheme.
  • The single fact that would most change which family I choose is whether Athletic’s defensive numbers under Terzić are the scheme or the absences. Their expected goals against have risen from 1.24 to 1.53 and deep completions conceded from 4.00 to 7.60 — but Vivian has been out throughout and Egiluz is gone until January, so the higher line and the missing centre-backs are confounded. What would settle it is five matches with both fit, which cannot happen before January; short of that, a published per-match defensive breakdown separating Athletic’s matches with and without Vivian would do it, and no source reached publishes one.

A · Squad, promotion & transfer context

Racing Santander are promoted, and not narrowly: they won LaLiga 2 outright with 82 points from 42 matches — 25 wins, 7 draws, 10 defeats, 90 scored, 61 conceded. Ninety goals is 2.14 per match against a second-division baseline of 2.63 per match for both sides combined, and they failed to score in only 9.5% of matches. They are back in the top flight after a long absence. Celta Vigo finished 6th in LaLiga on 54 points — 14 wins, 12 draws, 12 defeats, 53 scored, 48 conceded — and qualified for Europe. The structural headline is that the established side is the one in trouble: Celta have no win in six, three goals scored, and the lowest expected goals per match of any club on this board at 0.90, while the promoted champions sit three points above them having scored eleven. Neither club changed head coach. Both squads changed heavily around them, and Racing’s comparison season is not from this division at all.

  • Celta Vigo — turnover high, deliberately so, and weighted to departures; manager unchanged. Claudio Giráldez remains head coach in his third full season, having stayed despite offers elsewhere. The club’s stated plan for the window was ten to twelve departures against five or six arrivals, with the priorities named publicly as a defender, a winger and a striker. Out: Manuel Fernández Arroyo to Cádiz, Franco Cervi free, and four loans — Carlos Domínguez to Real Oviedo, Unai Núñez to Espanyol, Manu Sánchez and Hugo Sotelo to Levante. In: Altay Bayındır on loan from Manchester United, 7 August, and Abdoulaye Faye on loan from Bayer Leverkusen, 3 August — two loans rather than transfers. Javi Galán also arrived free from Osasuna, a move documented from the other side on this board’s Osasuna card at 14:00 today. The goalkeeping position was openly unsettled through the window, with Andrei Radu named as the likely first choice ahead of Iván Villar while the club pursued Bayındır. A club that planned to lose twice as many players as it gained, and filled the gaps with loans.
  • Racing Santander — promoted as champions, and they bought a spine; manager unchanged. José Alberto López remains head coach, the coach who won the division. In: Julen Agirrezabala from Athletic Club, €4.5m — the club’s largest signing of the window, a goalkeeper, and he is expected to start; Iván Martín; Pablo Ramón and Asier Villalibre, both made permanent after playing key parts in the promotion — Villalibre’s permanent transfer is from Alavés, who play at 16:15 today; Pedro Felipe and Facundo González, two South Americans from Juventus; and Sergio Canales, returning to the club. Out: Suleiman Camara to Slovakia, €1m — the largest sale, plus Mario García, Javier Castro and Samuel Calera. Net spend −€6.9m. Contradiction resolved here: this board’s Athletic card at 16:15 reported one source listing Agirrezabala as returning from a loan at Valencia and another as leaving for Racing; the Racing ledger settles it as a €4.5m permanent transfer to Racing, and the two accounts reconcile as a loan return followed by a sale.
  • Suspensions / absences. No suspension is reported for either side. Celta: no unavailable player is listed by any source reached — notable on a side that has not won in six and has just been beaten heavily, because it removes the easiest explanation for the form. Racing: Andrés Martín is out with a knee injury, the only absence named. Both clubs are therefore close to full strength, which matters for how the numbers below should be read: the rates are not being depressed by an absence list.
  • Sample. Celta: seven competitive matches, six in the league — 0–0 at Valencia (22 August), 1–2 v Osasuna (27 August), 0–2 v Athletic Club (30 August), 0–0 at Real Sociedad (3 September), 1–1 at Getafe (7 September), 1–1 v Málaga (13 September) — plus a Europa League tie at Omonia Nicosia on 16 September. Eighteenth on 4 points: no wins, four draws, two defeats, three goals scored in six matches. Racing: six competitive matches, all league — 2–2 v Villarreal (16 August), 0–1 at Getafe (23 August), 3–2 v Elche (28 August), 2–3 at Rayo Vallecano (5 September), 2–1 v Alavés (12 September), 2–7 at Barcelona (16 September). Tenth on 7 points, 11 scored and 16 conceded. Five of their six matches have gone over 2.5 and five of six have seen both teams score.
  • Referee. Ricardo de Burgos Bengoetxea, confirmed. Record, counted from every LaLiga match he refereed: 2025–26, 19 matches, 3.79 cards per match on 22.63 fouls — 5.97 fouls per card — and 0.47 penalties per match, one of the highest penalty rates in the division, with 2 red cards. 2026–27 so far, 3 matches, 5.00 cards on 25.00 fouls — 5.00 fouls per card — and 0.67 penalties, with one red. Both seasons put him under the divisional card rate (4.52 last season, 4.48 this) on cards while sitting clearly above it on penalties. History: two Celta matches last season, and both produced penalties — Celta–Valencia on 3 January 2026 gave 4 cards, 19 fouls and two penalties, and Celta–Elche on 3 May gave 3 cards, 29 fouls and one penalty. Three penalties in two matches involving this home club. No Racing match at all: he refereed no LaLiga 2 fixture last season, so there is no record of him with the away side.
  • Not sourced. Racing’s 2025–26 expected goals, for or against — Understat does not cover LaLiga 2 and nobody else publishes it, so the xG row is blank on the away side for last season and is not inferred from their 90 goals. Fees for most of Racing’s arrivals other than Agirrezabala, and for Celta’s departures; both windows are reported by name rather than by price in the sources reached. One source gives the kickoff as 13:30 and the rest as 18:30; the ESPN fixture record has 16:30 UTC, which is 18:30 in Vigo, and that is what is used. No source characterises de Burgos’ reading of contact in the box in words; his 0.47 penalties per match is counted, not described. Everything else the spec asks for is published in both divisions and was counted from the match record.

B · Style & early-season reality

Celta have stopped creating, and the collapse is in the chance data rather than only in the results. Expected goals have fallen from 1.37 to 0.90 per match — the lowest figure of any club carded on this slate — shots on target from 4.03 to 2.83, and goals from 1.39 to 0.50. They still have the ball: possession is up from 50.46% to 54.23%. What they no longer do is get into the box or press. PPDA has moved from 12.88 to 14.59 and, more tellingly, opponents’ PPDA against them from 12.91 to 15.18 — both sides press less, so this is a slower, flatter version of the same side. The one column that has improved sharply is the defensive one, and it is worth stating because it cuts against the league position: deep completions conceded have halved from 6.26 to 3.00 per match, the best figure on this board, and xG against has fallen from 1.48 to 1.41. Their away record is the evidence: 0.33 scored and 0.33 conceded across three. This is a side that has become much harder to score against and has almost stopped scoring — four draws in six, three of them by a single goal or none.

Racing are playing exactly as a promoted champion tends to: the same intent, applied at a level where it is punished. In LaLiga 2 they had 52.39% of the ball, took 14.21 shots against 13.02 faced, and won 5.71 corners against 4.79. In LaLiga they have 46.25%, take 10.17 shots against 17.33 faced, and win 4.50 corners against 10.17. The corner column is the single most extreme number anywhere on this board — 10.17 conceded per match, against a divisional 4.94 — and it is not one result: their per-match counts read 10, 8, 10, 9, 6 and 18, so even excluding the 2–7 at Barcelona they concede around 8.6 a game. Their pressing has intensified rather than dropped (PPDA 9.98, more aggressive than Celta’s 14.59) and the cost is in the same rows: 9.83 deep completions conceded per match and 2.29 expected goals against. At the other end they have kept scoring — 1.83 per match against 2.14 in the second tier, failing to score once in six — which is the part of the promotion record that has travelled. The evidence that this is the level rather than a bad start is six matches of shot, corner and deep-completion data all moving the same way, against a season whose xG nobody published.

C · Market-type suitability

1X2 / MoneylineDangerous
The promoted side is above the established one and neither position rests on evidence that transfers. Celta finished 6th on 54 points and are 18th without a win, with 0.90 expected goals per match; Racing won the second division with 90 goals and are conceding 2.67 per game at 2.29 xG against. One club’s comparison season is from a different division and the other’s describes a team that is not producing anything like the same numbers. The divisional home-win rate of 45.0% is close to last season’s 48.9%, which is the only stable input in the row. Six matches each, pointing in contradictory directions.
Asian handicap / DNBDangerous
Celta’s six margins are 0, −1, −2, 0, 0, 0 — four draws and no win, a goal difference of −3 across six matches, and a home record of 0.67 scored and 1.67 conceded against 0.33 and 0.33 away. A side that draws two-thirds of its matches has no margin to read. Racing’s are 0, −1, +1, −1, +1, −5 — five results inside one goal and one rout. Two distributions that are both tightly clustered with one extreme tail, from clubs whose comparison seasons are in different divisions.
Totals (O/U goals)Split — the widest disagreement on this board
Celta are at 16.7% over 2.5 and Racing at 83.3%: the two most extreme club rates on the entire slate, in the same fixture. Both are internally consistent. Celta’s six matches have produced 9 goals, with 0.90 xG for and 1.41 against; Racing’s have produced 27, with 1.52 and 2.29. The divisional line of 56.7% sits between them, and the division is running 0.38 goals per match above its own 2025–26 rate. Racing’s second-division rate was 71.4% against a 50.2% baseline, so a high-total tendency is at least a standing property on their side; Celta’s 44.7% last season was below the line too. Neither club is behaving anomalously against its own history — they simply have opposite histories.
BTTSSplit — Racing well evidenced, Celta’s attack is the unknown
Racing’s half is documented from every direction: both teams have scored in 5 of 6, no clean sheet in six, 2.67 conceded per match on 2.29 xG against, 17.33 shots faced and 9.83 deep completions conceded — and their second-division BTTS rate was 73.8% against a 56.0% baseline, so this is continuous with who they were rather than new. Celta’s half is the weak input and it is weak on the scoring side rather than the conceding one: they have failed to score in 50.0% of matches against 15.8% last season, on 0.90 xG and 2.83 shots on target per match. Their clean-sheet rate of 33.3% is up from 26.3% and backed by the best deep-completions-conceded figure on this board.
Team goalsSplit — one side’s rate is well evidenced, the other’s is collapsing
Racing have scored 1.83 per match on 1.52 xG, after 2.14 per match in the second tier with a 9.5% failed-to-score rate. Two levels, the same behaviour, slightly reduced — that is about as good as a promoted side’s attacking evidence gets, even without last season’s xG. Celta’s is the opposite: 0.50 goals per match against 1.39 last season, on expected goals of 0.90 against 1.37, and 2.83 shots on target against 4.03. The chance data confirms the decline rather than contradicting it, which means the 0.50 is not simply poor finishing. Both halves have a mechanism; only one has a stable level.
Halves (1H result, 1H goals)OK — 1H goals sharply separated, 1H result not readable
The first-half columns divide these sides more cleanly than almost any pair on this board. Celta have scored 0.33 first-half goals per match and conceded 0.50 — two scored in six matches. Racing have scored 1.50 and conceded 1.50, so their matches produce three first-half goals between them. Against a divisional 1.43 per match, one club sits at roughly half the baseline and the other at double it. Both figures have moved from last season in the same direction as their full-time numbers (Celta 0.50 and 0.55; Racing 0.86 and 0.67 in LaLiga 2, against a 1.17 baseline there). A first-half result is a different question and Celta’s four draws in six make it unreadable.
Discipline (cards, fouls)OK — deep referee record, but the penalty rate is the live number
De Burgos has 19 matches of last season at 3.79 cards and 5.97 fouls per card — under the divisional 4.52 cards, close to the divisional 5.58 leash — and his 2026–27 line reads 5.00 cards on 5.00 fouls per card over three matches, a sharper reading than his season figure. The clubs pull apart: Celta take 1.83 cards on 10.33 fouls, the lowest pairing in this fixture; Racing take 2.33 on 14.83 fouls, the highest foul rate of any club carded today, and Racing came from a division running at 5.31 cards per match against LaLiga’s 4.48. The number that stands out is not cards but penalties: de Burgos averaged 0.47 per match last season and 0.67 this, and both of his previous Celta matches produced one — three penalties in two. That is a documented tendency rather than an anecdote, and it is the part of this row with the most behind it.
CornersWell-suited — the most extreme and best-corroborated figure on this board
Racing concede 10.17 corners per match. That is the highest figure anywhere on this slate, more than double the divisional 4.94, and it repeats rather than resting on the 2–7 at Barcelona: their per-match counts conceded read 10, 8, 10, 9, 6 and 18, so even discarding the outlier they give up about 8.6 a game. Every other column agrees — 17.33 shots faced, 9.83 deep completions conceded, 46.25% possession, and a PPDA of 9.98 that says they press high and get played through — and it is a change of level rather than of behaviour, since in LaLiga 2 they won 5.71 and conceded 4.79. Celta corroborate from the other side: 3.33 won and 5.00 conceded, against 3.79 and 4.68 last season, a club that has been below the divisional corner rate at both ends for two seasons. The direction here is as well established as anything on the board; only the level is six matches old.

D · Early-season risk notes

  • Racing’s entire 2025–26 record is from a different division and its xG column does not exist. The 82 points, 90 goals, 71.4% over-2.5 rate and 2.93 cards per match were produced in LaLiga 2, a division running at 2.63 goals and 5.31 cards per match — a higher card rate than LaLiga’s. No public source publishes second-division expected goals, so the measure that would normally survive the step up is missing, and nothing here estimates it from their goal count.
  • Celta’s league position and their defensive numbers tell opposite stories, and both are six matches old. Eighteenth with no win, but 3.00 deep completions conceded per match — the best figure on this board — and xG against down to 1.41 from 1.48. Four of their six matches were draws, three of those 0–0 or 1–1. The problem is entirely at the other end: 0.90 xG and 2.83 shots on target per match. Reading them as a bad team rather than a non-scoring one would misread every row above.
  • No absence explains Celta’s form. No source reached lists an unavailable Celta player. That removes the usual caveat and replaces it with a harder one: whatever has changed is structural or is noise, and six matches cannot separate them. Their window was deliberately ten-to-twelve out and five-to-six in, filled with two loans, so a squad-composition explanation is available but not demonstrated.
  • Racing’s corner and shot deficits include one result that distorts every average. The 2–7 at Barcelona on 16 September contributed 18 corners conceded and 30 shots faced. It cannot be excluded and should not be treated as typical; the per-match figures with and without it are given in the corners row above precisely so the difference is visible.
  • Most sensitive: the Asian handicap and 1X2 — the first because Celta have drawn four of six with a goal difference of −3, the second because the two clubs’ comparison seasons are in different divisions and the table has them the wrong way round. Least sensitive: corners — Racing’s 10.17 conceded repeats in every one of six matches and is corroborated by shots faced, deep completions, possession and PPDA at once, and Celta’s sub-divisional rate at both ends has held for two seasons.

E · Short market-type guide

  • Corners are more robust than the match result here because Racing’s 10.17 conceded per match repeats across all six fixtures and is explained by four independent columns — 17.33 shots faced, 9.83 deep completions conceded, 46.3% possession, PPDA 9.98 — while the result question sets a 54-point side that cannot score against a promoted champion whose last season has no xG at all.
  • Racing’s side of BTTS is more robust than Celta’s because their 83.3% both-scored rate continues a 73.8% rate from the division below and is backed by 2.29 xG against, whereas Celta’s 50% failed-to-score rate is a six-match figure against 15.8% across the whole of last season.
  • A first-half total is more robust than a first-half result because the two clubs sit at roughly half and double the divisional first-half rate respectively — Celta 0.33 for and 0.50 against, Racing 1.50 and 1.50 — while Celta’s four draws in six make any margin inside 45 minutes unreadable.
  • The penalty element of the discipline row is more robust than the card element because de Burgos has averaged 0.47 penalties per match across 19 matches and 0.67 across three this season, and awarded three in his two previous Celta matches — whereas his card rate (3.79, then 5.00) has moved sharply between the two seasons and the two clubs sit on opposite sides of the divisional foul rate.
  • The single fact that would most change which family I choose is whether Celta’s 0.90 expected goals is the squad or the sample. They planned twelve departures and made two loan signings, they have no absentees, and their shots on target have fallen from 4.03 to 2.83 — but six matches with four draws is exactly the size at which a scoring drought and a scoring problem look identical. What would settle it is a published per-90 breakdown of their chance creation with and without the players who left, or simply five more matches; neither exists today, and the totals row in particular rests on which reading is right.

England · Premier League · Matchweek 5

Nottingham Forest – Coventry City

18:30 CEST · The City Ground, Nottingham · the Championship champions, 97 goals last season and none in four since, against a side whose expected goals both ways have swung further under a July appointment than any club on this board

Played this season
Forest 5 competitive (4 Premier League, 1 EFL Cup) · Coventry 6 competitive (4 Premier League, 2 EFL Cup) · four league matches each. Coventry’s 25/26 figures are Championship figures and are read against the Championship baseline, not the Premier League one
