A · Squad, promotion & transfer context

Coventry City are promoted as Championship champions, and by a distance: 95 points from 46 matches — 28 wins, 11 draws, 7 defeats, 97 scored, 45 conceded. That is 2.11 goals per match in a division whose two sides between them average 2.59, a 37.0% clean-sheet rate against a 25.0% baseline, and it is their first season in the top flight in twenty-five years. Nottingham Forest finished 16th in the Premier League on 44 points — 11 wins, 11 draws, 16 defeats, 48 scored, 51 conceded. The structural headline is what has happened since. Coventry have scored no goals in four Premier League matches and conceded ten; Forest have changed head coach and their expected goals have swung by 1.12 per match across the two ends — the largest two-way improvement of any club carded on this slate — for five points. Two clubs whose current results and current chance data disagree, in opposite directions.

  • Nottingham Forest — turnover deliberately low; manager changed in July 2026. Oliver Glasner joined as head coach in July 2026 from Crystal Palace, where he won the FA Cup and the Conference League — confirmed by the Premier League’s own 2026/27 manager listing. In — a short list, by design: Xaver Schlager in midfield, Ousmane Diomande at centre-back and Steven Benda in goal, with a fourth deal taking Gustavo Sá on loan to sister club Olympiacos. Glasner had asked for about four signings and the club reports getting five over the line, with the stated emphasis on streamlining the squad rather than expanding it — the opposite approach to both promoted clubs on this board. Forest pursued a goalkeeper and a wing-back late and reportedly missed at least one target. No fee is published for any of the three arrivals in any source reached. Diomande and Schlager are both in the expected eleven, so two of the three additions are starters.
  • Coventry City — promoted, and they spent like it; manager unchanged. Frank Lampard remains head coach, appointed in November 2024, the coach who won the division. In — eleven signings totalling more than £110m: Caleb Yirenkyi from Nordsjælland, £23m; Carl Rushworth from Brighton, £22m — a goalkeeper, signed permanently from a club that plays on this same card at 16:00; Sidiki Cherif from Fenerbahçe, £21m; Ethan Pinnock permanently from Brentford; and Dan Bentley free from Wolves, a second goalkeeper. Out — five: headlined by Jahnoah Markelo to Shabab Al Ahli, £4.4m, with goalkeeper Oliver Dovin loaned to Leyton Orient. Eleven in and five out, for a net outlay well over £100m, by a club whose entire squad had just scored 97 goals in the division below.
  • Suspensions / absences. Forest are missing two centre-backs and may be missing a third: Nikola Milenković is ruled out with a hamstring, Nicolò Savona is out with a knee injury after a setback, and Jair Cunha has returned to training with his availability uncertain. That matters more than usual because Forest’s improvement this season is defensive as much as attacking — expected goals against down from 1.70 to 1.08. Coventry have a suspension: Taiwo Awoniyi is unavailable, and Aurele Amenda is likely out with a calf injury picked up after twelve minutes of the midweek EFL Cup defeat to Aston Villa. Both clubs are therefore missing a starter from the unit their numbers most depend on.
  • Sample. Forest: five competitive matches, four in the league — 0–1 v Leeds (22 August), 2–2 at Liverpool (29 August), 0–0 v Tottenham (5 September), 2–1 at Aston Villa (12 September) — plus an EFL Cup tie against Leeds on 25 August. Eleventh on 5 points, 4 scored and 4 conceded. The win at Villa Park was Glasner’s first in charge. Forest have not scored at home this season: 0.00 goals per match across two, against 2.00 in two away. Coventry: six competitive matches, four in the league — 0–3 at Arsenal (21 August), 0–1 v Hull (29 August), 0–1 at Manchester City (5 September), 0–5 v Brighton (13 September) — plus EFL Cup ties at Plymouth (25 August) and against Aston Villa (16 September). Twentieth on 0 points, 0 goals scored and 10 conceded: four defeats, four blanks. Three of the four league opponents already appear on this board.
  • Referee. Jarred Gillett, confirmed. Record, counted from every match he refereed in the ESPN record: Premier League 2025–26, 22 matches, 3.64 cards per match on 19.77 fouls — 5.44 fouls per card, close to the divisional 5.60 — with 0.27 penalties per match and 1 red card. 2026–27 so far, 3 matches, 4.33 cards on 22.67 fouls — 5.23 fouls per card — and no penalties. He also took two Championship matches last season, at 3.50 cards and 4.86 fouls per card with 0.50 penalties, so his tolerance is documented across two divisions. Against the Premier League he books slightly under the divisional rate (3.64 against 3.86) on the lowest foul count of any official with more than twenty appointments — a middling leash on a quiet whistle. History: one Forest match last season, Nottingham Forest–Leeds United on 9 November 2025, which produced 3 cards, 21 fouls and a penalty. No Coventry match appears in his record in either division.
  • Not sourced. Coventry’s 2025–26 expected goals, for or against — no public source publishes Championship xG, so the away side’s row is blank for last season and is not inferred from their 97 goals. Fees for all three of Forest’s permanent signings, none of which is published by any source reached; Forest’s outlay is therefore not stated. Fees for six of Coventry’s eleven arrivals and four of their five departures. The exact date of Glasner’s appointment — the Premier League’s listing gives July 2026 without a day, and no source reached is more precise. No source characterises Gillett’s reading of contact in the box in words; his 0.27 penalties per match is counted, not described. Everything else the spec asks for is published in both divisions and was counted from the match record.

B · Style & early-season reality

Forest under Glasner are a different team in the chance data and almost the same team in the table, which is the single most interesting tension on this card. Expected goals have risen from 1.29 to 1.79 per match and expected goals against have fallen from 1.70 to 1.08 — a two-way swing of 1.12 goals per match, the largest of any club carded on this slate. The mechanism is visible: PPDA down from 13.48 to 10.99, deep completions up from 5.53 to 6.75 and conceded down from 7.74 to 4.25, shots up from 12.53 to 14.50 and shots faced down from 13.32 to 10.75. They press harder, concede less territory and create more. The cost is in the discipline column and it is steep: fouls per match up from 10.71 to 15.75, the highest rate of any club on this board, and cards from 1.61 to 2.75 — a high press bought with fouls. Two things have not followed: possession is down to 43.42% from 46.75%, and the goals have not arrived at home, where they have scored none in two matches. Their corner rate has halved, from 5.24 won to 2.75, which sits oddly beside the rise in shots and deep completions and is the one column that does not fit.

Coventry are the clearest illustration on this board of what a change of division does to a dominant second-tier side. In the Championship they had 55.24% of the ball, took 16.33 shots against 11.63 faced, hit 5.57 on target and scored 2.11 per match. In the Premier League they have 39.30%, take 10.25 against 15.75 faced, hit 3.25 on target and have scored none. Every one of those pairs has moved against them and the pressing figure explains why they cannot get it back: PPDA 19.85, the most passive figure on this board by a wide margin, against opponents pressing them at 9.99. They are being pressed twice as hard as they press. The defensive collapse is the sharper half: 0.98 conceded per match and a 37.0% clean-sheet rate in the Championship, against 2.50 conceded and none in four here, on 1.82 expected goals against — and that is with £22m spent on a goalkeeper and Pinnock signed permanently. The one column that has barely moved is corners — 5.33 won and 4.24 conceded then, 4.50 and 4.25 now — which makes it the only Championship figure on this card that has survived the step up.

C · Market-type suitability

1X2 / MoneylineOK — asymmetric, and both sides’ evidence is unusually legible
Unusually for this board, both halves of the question have something solid behind them and they point the same way. Forest’s underlying numbers have improved at both ends under a July appointment — 1.79 xG for and 1.08 against, against 1.29 and 1.70 last season — while their points total has not caught up. Coventry’s have collapsed on every measure that transfers between divisions: possession, shots, shots on target, goals and clean sheets, with 0 points and 0 goals from four. The reservations are real: four matches each, Forest missing two centre-backs, Coventry’s comparison season in a different division, and a divisional home-win rate down to 34.1% from 42.6%. But this is the rare row where the direction is corroborated rather than assumed.
Asian handicap / DNBDangerous
A handicap needs a margin and Forest’s split makes theirs unreadable: 0.00 goals scored per home match across two, against 2.00 per away match, with four league margins of −1, 0, 0, +1. Coventry’s are −3, −1, −1, −5 — two narrow defeats and two routs, with no result of theirs yet containing a goal of their own. A side with zero goals in four has no scoring distribution to place a line against, and the two heavy defeats came against Arsenal and Brighton, both of whom are also on this card and both of whom are playing well.
Totals (O/U goals)OK — both clubs at 50%, and the components disagree
The headline rates are identical and uninformative — both clubs are at exactly 50.0% over 2.5, against a divisional 53.7% — but they are built from opposite parts. Forest’s four matches produced 8 goals in a 0–1, a 2–2, a 0–0 and a 2–1; Coventry’s produced 10, all of them their opponents’. Coventry’s matches go over when they are beaten heavily and under when they are beaten narrowly, which is a distribution driven entirely by one side of the pitch. The xG lines add a little: 1.79 and 1.07 for, 1.08 and 1.82 against, which sum close to the divisional 2.85 per match. Coventry’s Championship 63.0% over 2.5 against a 50.4% baseline does not transfer.
BTTSWell-suited — the away side’s record is complete and mechanically explained
This is the best-evidenced family here and it rests on the most extreme figure on the card. Coventry have failed to score in all four Premier League matches — 100% failed-to-score, 0.0% both-scored, no clean sheets — against a divisional 29.3%. Unlike most four-match extremes this one has a full mechanism behind it: shots down from 16.33 to 10.25, shots on target from 5.57 to 3.25, possession from 55.24% to 39.30%, expected goals at 1.07, and a PPDA of 19.85 that says they cannot win the ball high. The honest caveats are that they scored 97 goals last season with a 15.2% blank rate, and that Forest have themselves not scored at home this season and kept a clean sheet in only one of four. Two clubs that struggle to score, one of which has not managed it at all.
Team goalsSplit — Forest’s creation is real, their conversion is not established
Forest are creating 1.79 expected goals per match and scoring 1.00, with 14.50 shots and 3.50 on target — a side generating well above its own 1.29 from last season and converting well below it. Whether that is a finishing problem or four matches is exactly what cannot be settled here, and the home split (no goals in two) sharpens the question. Coventry’s side needs less discussion and offers less: zero goals from 1.07 expected, 3.25 shots on target per match, and a 2.11-per-match Championship record that describes a different level. Both team-goal questions have a chance figure behind them; neither has a conversion rate worth the name.
Halves (1H result, 1H goals)OK — both first halves are nearly empty, and both figures are extreme
The first-half column here is quieter than anywhere else on the board. Forest have scored one first-half goal in four matches (0.25 per match) and conceded none at all — 0.00 against, the only club carded today with a blank first-half defensive column. Coventry have scored none and conceded 1.00. Against a divisional 1.27 per match, the two clubs between them have produced one first-half goal in eight team-appearances. Both figures have moved sharply from last season (Forest 0.50 and 0.63; Coventry 1.00 and 0.37 in the Championship), which is what keeps this at OK rather than higher — but the direction is consistent with the style data on both sides. A first-half result is unreadable from one goal in eight.
Discipline (cards, fouls)OK — referee well documented, and one club has moved sharply
Gillett is documented across 22 Premier League matches at 3.64 cards and 5.44 fouls per card, with 0.27 penalties — a middling leash, slightly under the divisional card rate, on the lowest foul count of any official with more than twenty appointments — plus two Championship matches at 3.50 cards and 4.86 fouls per card. His 2026–27 line runs a little sharper at 4.33 cards. The live number is Forest’s: fouls up from 10.71 to 15.75 per match and cards from 1.61 to 2.75, the highest foul rate of any club on this board, and it is mechanically tied to Glasner’s press — PPDA down from 13.48 to 10.99. Coventry have gone the other way: 1.25 cards on 12.50 fouls, and they came from a division running at 3.85 cards per match against the Premier League’s 3.93. One club’s rate is rising for a structural reason, the other’s is not.
CornersSplit — Coventry’s rate survived the step up, Forest’s halved
These two move in opposite directions and only one of them fits its own style data. Coventry won 5.33 corners and conceded 4.24 in the Championship, and win 4.50 and concede 4.25 now — almost unchanged, and the only Championship figure on this card that has survived promotion, which is notable given their possession fell by sixteen points. Forest have gone from 5.24 won and 4.82 conceded to 2.75 and 5.25 — a halving at one end that sits awkwardly beside shots rising from 12.53 to 14.50 and deep completions from 5.53 to 6.75. When a club’s corner count falls while its shot count rises, one of the two is telling the wrong story, and four matches cannot say which. The divisional rate is 4.65 per team.

D · Early-season risk notes

  • Coventry’s whole 2025–26 record is from a different division and its xG column does not exist. Ninety-five points, 97 goals, a 37.0% clean-sheet rate and 63.0% over 2.5 came against a Championship baseline of 2.59 goals and 3.85 cards per match. No public source publishes Championship expected goals, so nothing here converts their 97 goals into a level, and the 0.00 they are now scoring is measured against 1.07 xG rather than against anything from last season.
  • Forest’s improvement is measured over four matches and two of its architects are unavailable. Expected goals against down from 1.70 to 1.08 and deep completions conceded from 7.74 to 4.25 is a real defensive shift, but Milenković and Savona are out and Cunha is doubtful, so the back line that produced it is not the back line that will play. Glasner arrived in July with three signings and no published fees.
  • Forest’s results and chances disagree by more than any club on this board. A 1.12-goal two-way swing in expected goals has produced one win in four and no goals at home. Either the chances will convert or the swing is a four-match artefact of playing Leeds, Liverpool, Tottenham and Aston Villa — three of whom are 17th, 19th or newly promoted this morning. Two of Forest’s four opponents so far also appear on this card, both in the bottom three.
  • Coventry’s four opponents are the reason to be careful about their zero. Arsenal (top, four wins), Manchester City, Brighton (13 goals in four) and Hull. Three of those four are on this board and two are among its best-performing sides. A 100% failed-to-score rate against that set is not the same fact as a 100% rate against a normal four.
  • Most sensitive: the Asian handicap and team goals — the first because Forest have scored nothing at home and everything away across two matches each, the second because Forest are creating 1.79 xG and scoring 1.00 while Coventry are creating 1.07 and scoring nothing. Least sensitive: BTTS and the first-half total — the first because Coventry’s blank record is corroborated by possession, shots, shots on target, xG and PPDA at once, the second because the two clubs have produced one first-half goal between them in eight team-appearances against a divisional 1.27 per match.

E · Short market-type guide

  • BTTS is more robust than total goals here because Coventry’s 100% failed-to-score rate is explained by five independent columns — 10.25 shots, 3.25 on target, 39.3% possession, 1.07 xG, PPDA 19.85 — whereas both clubs sit at exactly 50.0% over 2.5 and their matches reach that number by completely different routes.
  • Forest’s expected goals are more robust than their goal count because 1.79 xG per match is supported by shots rising to 14.50 and deep completions to 6.75, while the 1.00 they have scored includes two home matches in which they scored nothing at all.
  • A first-half total is more robust than a first-half result because one first-half goal in eight team-appearances, against a divisional 1.27 per match, is a clear reading in one direction, whereas no margin inside 45 minutes exists on either side.
  • Coventry’s corner rate is more robust than any other figure they have because it is the single Championship number that survived promotion — 5.33 won and 4.24 conceded then, 4.50 and 4.25 now — while their possession fell sixteen points and their shots fell by six a game.
  • The single fact that would most change which family I choose is whether Forest’s defensive improvement belongs to Glasner or to Milenković and Savona. Expected goals against have fallen from 1.70 to 1.08 and deep completions conceded from 7.74 to 4.25, but both first-choice centre-backs are out today and a third is doubtful, so the scheme and the personnel are confounded in exactly the four matches that produced the figure. What would settle it is a published per-match defensive split for Forest with and without those two — no source reached publishes one, and until it exists the 1X2 and BTTS rows both rest on a defence that has not been tested without them.

A · Squad, promotion & transfer context

  • Not sourced, and not pursued at this tier. Reduced-depth card: three searches per club, sections B and E omitted. Fields the tier did not pursue: fees for most of Stuttgart’s eleven arrivals and twelve departures, and for Dortmund’s window beyond Adeyemi; the full loan ledger on both sides; the style read beyond the pressing, shot and corner figures quoted in section C. Fields pursued and still empty: no injury or suspension list for either club is published by any source reached — the club channel that would carry it returned a navigation page rather than the preview. That is a real gap on a fixture where one side’s defensive numbers are the whole story. Fields pursued and contradictory: Chema Andrés’ fee. The German press gives €22m as Stuttgart’s largest sale of the summer; Brighton’s own confirmed-transfer ledger, used on this board’s 16:00 card, gives £12.9m for the same player from the same club. Those do not reconcile at any plausible exchange rate and neither is preferred here.
  • VfB Stuttgart — turnover very high by volume, concentrated and modest by value; manager unchanged. Sebastian Hoeneß remains head coach, in charge since 3 April 2023 and now in his fourth season, with a contract to 2028. Eleven arrivals and twelve departures, but only three transfers carried a notable fee, with the German press summarising the window as three signings for about €55m. Midfield: out — Chema Andrés, a 21-year-old defensive midfielder and the club’s largest sale (€22m on one source, £12.9m on another); in — El Khannouss and Gríscha Prömel free from Hoffenheim, named as the direct replacements. Prömel, at 31, is described as the only experienced signing of the window, brought in as a midfield leader. Elsewhere: Pejčinović and Sauer replace the departing Milošević, sold to Portugal after a Werder Bremen loan, and Jovanović, sold to Italy; Funk and Seimen replace Alexander Nübel in goal — Nübel’s subsequent sale by Bayern to Beşiktaş for €6.25m was documented on this board’s card of 18 September. A young squad rebuilt around the edges rather than reinforced.
  • Borussia Dortmund — turnover low; manager unchanged. Niko Kovač remains head coach, in charge since 2 February 2025 on a contract to summer 2027, publicly confirmed by sporting director Lars Ricken as staying for 2026–27. Out, the headline: Karim Adeyemi to Barcelona, reported at $25m with a 35% sell-on clause, plus Salih Özcan after several years at the club. In: Joey Veerman from PSV Eindhoven, a permanent midfield signing, and Takato Yamamoto, 18, from Gamba Osaka on a one-year loan. No fee is published for Veerman by any source reached. Two clubs on this slate sold their leading wide attacker to Barcelona this summer — Dortmund and Newcastle — and Barcelona themselves play at 21:00 tonight.
  • Suspensions / absences. None is published for either club by any source reached, and that absence is itself the finding here. One club channel headline indicates Dortmund will name “three new players” in the starting eleven against Stuttgart, but the article body was not retrievable, so what that change is cannot be stated. Anything on this card that depends on which back line Stuttgart field — and the defensive rows depend on it entirely — carries that gap.
  • Sample. Stuttgart: five competitive matches, three in the league — 1–5 at Bayern München (28 August), 4–1 v 1. FC Köln (4 September), 1–2 at Hoffenheim (12 September) — plus a DFB-Pokal tie at Hansa Rostock (21 August) and a Champions League tie against Viking FK (9 September). Fourteenth on 3 points, 6 scored and 8 conceded, with all three league matches going over 2.5 and both teams scoring in all three. Dortmund: six competitive matches, three in the league — 2–0 v Hamburger SV (29 August), 3–2 at Hoffenheim (5 September), 3–0 v Paderborn (12 September) — plus the German Super Cup against Bayern (22 August), a DFB-Pokal tie at HEBC Hamburg (1 September) and a Champions League tie against Villarreal (8 September). Third on 9 points from three wins, 8 scored and 2 conceded.

Record, counted from every Bundesliga match he refereed in the ESPN record: 2025–26, 14 matches, 3.29 cards per match on 19.86 fouls — 6.04 fouls per card, against a divisional 5.46, a longer leash than average — with 0.36 penalties per match and 4 red cards in fourteen appointments. 2026–27 so far, one match, 3.00 cards on 18.00 fouls, 6.04 fouls per card, no penalty — the same tendency. Booking below the divisional rate (3.29 against 3.82) while sending off four times in fourteen matches is a genuine split and both halves are counted. History: three matches involving these clubs last season, and they were not alike — VfB Stuttgart–Heidenheim on 5 October 2025 gave 2 cards on 23 fouls, FC Augsburg–Borussia Dortmund on 31 October gave 5 on 30, and VfB Stuttgart–Bayern München on 6 December gave 7 cards on 17 fouls and a penalty — that last one at 2.4 fouls per card, four times sharper than his season figure.

C · Market-type suitability

1X2 / MoneylineDangerous
Three league matches each is the thinnest sample on this slate and the two clubs’ records within it point in opposite directions to their full seasons. Stuttgart finished 4th on 62 points and are 14th on 3; Dortmund finished 2nd on 73 and are 3rd on 9. Neither changed coach and neither made a signing large enough to explain a change of level, so the honest reading is that three matches is telling us very little that 34 did not. The divisional home-win rate of 53.6% against 43.8% last season is itself computed over 28 matches. Nothing here supports a result.
Asian handicap / DNBDangerous
Stuttgart’s three margins are −4, +3 and −1; Dortmund’s are +2, +1 and +3. Stuttgart’s home and away split across three matches is 4.00 scored and 1.00 conceded in their single home match against 1.00 and 3.50 in two away — one match per venue is not a split, it is two data points. The 1–5 at Bayern alone carries much of their 2.67 conceded per match, and it cannot be excluded. A margin read off six results, one of which is a four-goal defeat at the champions, is a margin read off variance.
Totals (O/U goals)Well-suited — every input large and pointing the same way
This is the one family here where the sample size problem is solved by the division rather than by the clubs. The Bundesliga is running at 3.96 goals per match and 82.1% over 2.5 — the most extreme divisional figures on this board — against 3.24 and 63.7% across the whole of last season. Both clubs were above the old baseline too: Stuttgart at 73.5% over 2.5 across 34 matches, Dortmund at 55.9%, and Stuttgart’s three this season have all gone over. The xG columns corroborate rather than contradict: Stuttgart 2.48 for and 3.30 against, Dortmund 2.16 and 1.19, against a divisional 1.98 per team. The honest caveat is that the 82.1% rests on 28 matches and its own 25/26 value is eighteen points lower.
BTTSSplit — the home side is at 100%, the away side keeps clean sheets
The two halves are as far apart as this family gets. Stuttgart have had both teams score in 3 of 3, with no clean sheet and no blank, conceding 2.67 per match on 3.30 expected goals against — the worst defensive chance figure of any club carded on this slate. Dortmund have kept clean sheets in 2 of 3, conceding 0.67 on 1.19 xG against, extending a 44.1% clean-sheet rate across 34 matches last season. The scoring half is settled on both sides — neither club has failed to score in a league match this season, and their blank rates last season were 8.8% and 5.9%, the two lowest on this board. So one team will very likely score; whether both do rests on a Dortmund defence that has been the better one in both samples.
Team goalsWell-suited — four rates across two seasons that barely move
Both clubs have scoring records that survived the summer almost untouched, which almost nothing else on this card can claim. Stuttgart scored 2.09 per match last season and 2.00 this; Dortmund 2.06 and 2.67. The chance data agrees on both: Stuttgart 2.01 xG then 2.48 now, Dortmund 1.99 then 2.16, with shots on target at 6.09 and 5.67 for Stuttgart and 4.79 and 6.00 for Dortmund. Failed-to-score rates of 8.8% and 5.9% last season have become 0.0% and 0.0%. Neither side is outscoring its expected goals, so neither rate depends on finishing running hot. Three matches is the limit, but here the three agree with the thirty-four.
Halves (1H result, 1H goals)OK — last season is deep, this season contradicts it
The two-season picture is unusually good and the current one cuts against it. Last season Stuttgart scored 0.97 first-half goals per match and conceded 0.68; Dortmund scored 0.94 and conceded 0.32 — 34 matches each, both well-documented. This season Stuttgart have scored 0.33 and conceded 0.67, and Dortmund 1.00 and 0.33, against a divisional 1.43 per match. So Dortmund’s figures have held almost exactly and Stuttgart’s attacking one has collapsed to a third of its previous value over three matches. That asymmetry is what a first-half total has to price, and it is the reason this is rated OK rather than higher. A first-half result needs a margin inside 45 minutes from a three-match sample.
Discipline (cards, fouls)OK — everything points low, and the referee’s history here does not
Stieler books below the divisional rate on a long leash: 3.29 cards per match and 6.04 fouls per card over 14 matches, against a divisional 3.82 and 5.46, with the same figures again in his single 2026–27 appointment. Both clubs agree: Stuttgart take 1.33 cards on 10.00 fouls and Dortmund 2.00 on 10.33, against a divisional 3.25 cards and 11.21 fouls per team, and both were at 1.91 last season. Three records, two of them 34 matches deep, all pointing under the line. Two things hold the rating down and both are counted: Stieler showed 4 red cards in those 14 matches, and his one previous Stuttgart match against a major opponent — Stuttgart–Bayern on 6 December 2025 — produced 7 cards on 17 fouls, at 2.4 fouls per card.
CornersSplit — Dortmund stable, Stuttgart’s conceded figure has jumped
Dortmund have barely moved: 5.35 won and 4.21 conceded per match last season, 4.33 and 4.67 now, both close to a divisional 5.71 and 4.88 in the two seasons. Stuttgart have moved at both ends and sharply at one: 5.68 won and 4.29 conceded last season, 6.67 and 7.33 now. The 7.33 conceded is the second-highest figure on this board and it tracks the rest of their defensive collapse rather than standing apart from it — 19.67 shots faced per match against 12.68 last season, 10.33 deep completions conceded against 6.35, and expected goals against up from 1.57 to 3.30. The direction is corroborated four ways; the level rests on three matches, one of them at Bayern.

D · Early-season risk notes

  • Three league matches each is the thinnest sample anywhere on this slate, and one of Stuttgart’s three is a 1–5 at Bayern München which alone carries much of their 2.67 conceded, 3.30 expected goals against, 19.67 shots faced and 7.33 corners conceded. It cannot be excluded and should not be treated as typical. Their single home match produced a 4–1 win.
  • The division has moved further than either club, and further than any division on this board. The Bundesliga is running at 3.96 goals per match and 82.1% over 2.5 against 3.24 and 63.7% across the whole of 2025–26 — a gap of 0.72 goals a game — on 28 matches. Every row above that leans on the 26/27 divisional line is leaning on about a fifth of a season, and the totals row leans on it heavily.
  • Neither club changed coach or made a signing large enough to explain a change of level, which cuts both ways. It means last season’s 34-match records are unusually applicable — Hoeneß is in his fourth season and Kovač his second full one — and it means Stuttgart’s defensive numbers have no structural explanation at all. A 1.73-goal rise in expected goals against with the same coach and no defensive sale is either three matches of noise or something no source reached has identified.
  • No injury or suspension list exists for either side in any source reached. On a card whose defensive rows are the entire story, not knowing which back line Stuttgart field is a material gap, and one club channel indicates Dortmund will make three changes to their starting eleven without the detail being retrievable.
  • Most sensitive: the Asian handicap and 1X2 — both need a margin from three matches per club, and Stuttgart’s three contain a four-goal defeat and a three-goal win. Least sensitive: total goals and team goals — the first because the divisional rate, both clubs’ 34-match rates, both clubs’ three-match rates and both xG lines all point the same way, the second because four scoring rates across two seasons sit between 2.00 and 2.67 with the expected goals agreeing and neither club overperforming them.

Italy · Serie A · Giornata 5

Venezia – Lazio

A · Squad, promotion & transfer context

Venezia are promoted as Serie B champions and they won the division outright: 82 points from 38 matches — 24 wins, 10 draws, 4 defeats, 77 scored, 31 conceded, a 42.1% clean-sheet rate against a 27.3% divisional baseline. Four defeats in a season. Lazio finished 9th in Serie A on 54 points — 14 wins, 12 draws, 12 defeats, 41 scored, 40 conceded — and were the lowest-scoring watchable side in the division: 31.6% of their matches went over 2.5 against a divisional 45.8%, they failed to score in 44.7% of them, and both teams scored in only 28.9%. The structural headline is that both clubs have changed and in the same direction. Venezia spent about €44m of a €20m-plus sale on a squad rebuilt for the top flight and kept the coach who took them up; Lazio replaced Maurizio Sarri with Gennaro Gattuso and rebuilt the defence and the forward line around him. Lazio’s numbers have already moved sharply. Venezia’s have moved too, and in the wrong direction.

  • Venezia — promoted, turnover very high, and it was funded by one sale; manager unchanged. Giovanni Stroppa stayed after promotion and was renewed to 2029, and will start from a 3–5–2. Out, the headline: Doumbia to Sporting for more than €20m at the end of May, the sale that financed everything else; plus Michael Svoboda to Brighton, £4.3m — a transfer documented from the other side on this board’s 16:00 card — Nicolussi Caviglia, Bjarkason, Sidibé and Venturi. In — about €44m, over half of it on two forwards: Akor Adams and Albion Rrahmani together account for €23.5m; Simon Sohm from Fiorentina reinforces a midfield that also gained Busio, Toma Bašić and Kike Pérez. Bašić arrives from Lazio — a direct transfer between the two clubs in this fixture. Other arrivals include Bella-Kotchap, Helgason, Rrahmani, Furtado, Montipò, Juan Jesus, Mazzocchi and Correia. Sporting director Filippo Antonelli is credited with one of the most ambitious windows in the club’s recent history. A squad described by the Italian press as completely renewed, under a coach who is not.
  • Lazio — turnover high at both ends of the pitch; manager changed in the summer. Maurizio Sarri’s contract was resolved by mutual consent on 26 May 2026 — he had already been replaced by Igor Tudor during the season — and Gennaro Gattuso is the new head coach. Defence: in — Danilho Doekhi free from Union Berlin, whose departure was documented on this board’s card of 18 September, Josip Šutalo from Ajax and Alfonso Pedraza from Villarreal; out — Mario Gila and Elseid Hysaj. Goalkeeper: out — Ivan Provedel. Midfield: in — Davide Frattesi from Internazionale, described by the Italian press as the highlight of the window, plus Galassi from Real Madrid Castilla; out — Toma Bašić to Venezia. Attack: in — Andrea Pinamonti and Albert Guðmundsson, both from Fiorentina; out — Pedro, Boulaye Dia on loan to Rennes with an obligation to buy worth €8.5m plus bonuses, and Ratkov on loan to Levante with a €14m option. The window is reported as closing at a zero balance.
  • Suspensions / absences. No injury or suspension list for either club is published by any source reached. The club and press pages that carried the referee appointment did not carry team news, and no preview located supplies one. That is a material gap on a fixture where Venezia’s entire XI is new and Lazio have changed both centre-backs and both forwards; nothing is invented to fill it.
  • Sample. Venezia: six competitive matches, four in the league — 0–2 v Lecce (23 August), 0–2 at AC Milan (28 August), 2–3 at Frosinone (6 September), 2–4 v Fiorentina (11 September) — plus Coppa Italia ties against Modena (15 August) and at Udinese (2 September). Twentieth on 0 points: four defeats, 4 scored and 11 conceded, no clean sheet. Lazio: five competitive matches, four in the league — 1–0 at Bologna (24 August), 1–0 v Genoa (30 August), 2–1 at Udinese (7 September), 2–2 v AC Milan (12 September) — plus a Coppa Italia tie against Mantova (16 August). Fourth on 10 points, unbeaten, 6 scored and 3 conceded. Both of Venezia’s league defeats at home came by two goals or more.
  • Referee. Simone Sozza of the Seregno section, confirmed, with Bercigli and Zingarelli on the lines, Bonacina fourth official, Pezzuto on VAR and Piccinini assisting. Record, counted from every match he refereed in the ESPN record: Serie A 2025–26, 17 matches, 3.76 cards per match on 25.47 fouls — 6.77 fouls per card, against a divisional 6.74, almost exactly average — with 0.47 penalties per match and, the number that stands out, 7 red cards in 17 matches. That is the highest red-card rate of any official on this board by a distance. 2026–27 so far, one match, 3.00 cards on 14.00 fouls at 4.67 fouls per card, no penalty. He also took five Serie B matches last season at 6.60 cards on 31.00 fouls — 4.70 fouls per card — a much sharper reading in the division below, though that division itself ran at 4.52 cards against Serie A’s 3.77. History: two Lazio matches last season, both large — Lazio–Roma on 21 September 2025 gave 4 cards on 36 fouls, and Lazio–Fiorentina on 7 January 2026 gave 8 cards on 26 fouls and two penalties. No Venezia match appears in his record in either division.
  • Not sourced. Venezia’s 2025–26 expected goals, for or against — Understat does not cover Serie B and no substitute exists, so the away column of the xG row is blank for last season and is not inferred from their 77 goals. Any injury or suspension list for either club. Individual fees for most of Venezia’s fifteen-odd arrivals beyond the €23.5m attributed to Adams and Rrahmani jointly, and for Lazio’s arrivals other than the loan-and-obligation structures on the two departures. The exact date of Gattuso’s appointment — Sarri’s resolution is dated 26 May 2026, but no source reached dates the successor. No source characterises Sozza’s reading of contact in the box in words; his 0.47 penalties per match is counted, not described.

B · Style & early-season reality

Venezia are the strangest set of numbers on this board: a side bottom of Serie A with no points that dominates the ball and presses harder than anyone. Possession 59.20% — the second-highest figure of any club carded today, and from a team with zero points — and a PPDA of 7.32, the most aggressive press on this entire slate, meaning they engage inside eight opposition passes. That is continuous with the side that won Serie B at 63.97% possession, so the intent has survived the step up. What has not survived is everything downstream of it. Shots have fallen from 17.21 to 13.25 per match and shots faced risen from 10.08 to 15.00; deep completions run at 4.50 against 8.50 conceded; expected goals against sit at 2.65, the second-worst figure on this board. The defensive record is the sharpest contrast anywhere on the card: 0.82 conceded per match and a 42.1% clean-sheet rate in Serie B, against 2.75 conceded and no clean sheet in four here. A high line and a heavy press, applied at a level where the opposition plays through it — and the foul rate has fallen from 15.37 to 11.00, which says they are not even getting close enough to foul.

Lazio have changed more than their coach and the chance data says so before the results do. Expected goals have risen from 1.22 to 1.82 per match and deep completions from 4.53 to 8.25 — nearly doubled — while PPDA has fallen from 16.66 to 12.68, so a side that sat off last season now presses. Under Sarri and then Tudor they were the most goal-shy team in the division: 41 scored in 38, a 44.7% blank rate, 31.6% of matches over 2.5. Under Gattuso, through four matches, they have scored in every one, at 1.50 per match, and conceded 0.75 on 1.44 expected goals against. The forward line doing it is new — Pinamonti and Guðmundsson both from Fiorentina, with Dia and Pedro gone — and so is the midfield behind it, with Frattesi in from Internazionale. The one column that has moved against them is discipline: fouls up from 10.58 to 15.25 per match, the joint-highest rate on this board, which is the ordinary cost of pressing more. The evidence that this is Gattuso’s side rather than Sarri’s is four matches, a 0.60-goal rise in expected goals, a near-doubling of deep completions and four new starters — not a season.

C · Market-type suitability

1X2 / MoneylineDangerous
Both clubs are unreadable for opposite reasons. Venezia won Serie B with 82 points and four defeats, and have lost four from four in Serie A with a completely rebuilt squad — their comparison season is in a different division and its xG does not exist. Lazio changed head coach and both centre-backs and both forwards, and their four-match record (10 points, unbeaten) contradicts a 38-match record in which they scored 41 goals. The divisional home-win rate of 43.9% is close to last season’s 38.9%, which is the only stable input. Four matches on each side, neither continuous with what came before.
Asian handicap / DNBDangerous
Venezia’s four margins are −2, −2, −1 and −2; Lazio’s are +1, +1, +1 and 0. Every Lazio result has been decided by exactly one goal or drawn, and every Venezia result has been a defeat — two of the tightest and most one-directional distributions on this board, from a four-match sample each. Lazio’s away record last season was 0.74 scored and 0.79 conceded per match, from a side that has since changed coach, both forwards and both centre-backs. Venezia’s home record in Serie B was 2.32 scored and 0.63 conceded; in Serie A it is 1.00 and 3.00.
Totals (O/U goals)Split — identical current rates built from opposite histories
Both clubs sit at exactly 50.0% over 2.5 this season, under a divisional 58.5%, and the agreement is superficial. Lazio arrive from a season at 31.6% over 2.5 — the lowest figure of any club on this board by fourteen points — against a divisional 45.8%, and their matches this season have produced 9 goals in four. Venezia arrive from 57.9% in Serie B against a 48.2% baseline, and their four Serie A matches have produced 15 goals — though eleven of those were conceded. The xG lines sum to 3.62 per match between them, above the divisional 3.02, driven almost entirely by Venezia’s 2.65 against. A division running 0.59 goals per match above its own completed season completes the disagreement.
BTTSSplit — Venezia cannot keep a clean sheet, Lazio could not score
The two halves are both well documented and they pull hard against each other. Venezia have no clean sheet in four, concede 2.75 per match on 2.65 expected goals against, face 15.00 shots and concede 8.50 deep completions — a defensive record with a full mechanism behind it. But they have also failed to score in 50.0% of their matches, on 1.80 xG. Lazio’s 28.9% both-scored rate last season was the lowest on this board and came with a 44.7% blank rate; this season they have scored in all four and kept two clean sheets. So each club supplies one half of a BTTS and denies the other, and in both cases the current four matches contradict the 38 behind them.
Team goalsSplit — Lazio’s rise is evidenced, Venezia’s fall is a change of level
Lazio’s attacking improvement is the better-evidenced half and it is corroborated rather than asserted: 1.50 goals per match against 1.08 last season, on expected goals of 1.82 against 1.22, with deep completions up from 4.53 to 8.25 and a new forward line. They are scoring below their chances rather than above them, which is the useful direction. Venezia have scored 1.00 per match on 1.80 xG — also underperforming — after 2.03 per match in Serie B with a 10.5% blank rate. Their 77-goal season came at 17.21 shots per match against the 13.25 they manage now, so the drop has a mechanism, but nothing converts a Serie B scoring rate into a Serie A one and no xG exists to bridge it.
Halves (1H result, 1H goals)OK — one striking figure, on a four-match base
The first-half columns are unusually informative here. Lazio have not conceded a first-half goal all season — 0.00 against in four matches — while scoring 0.75, against 0.42 and 0.50 last season. Venezia have scored 0.25 and conceded 1.00, against 1.00 and 0.32 in Serie B. So both clubs’ first-half figures have moved sharply and in opposite directions from their own previous seasons, and the divisional rate is 1.20 per match against Serie B’s 1.13. A first-half total therefore has two clear and differently-sourced inputs; a first-half result does not, because Lazio’s four results are three one-goal wins and a draw.
Discipline (cards, fouls)OK — deep referee record, and the red-card rate is the live number
Sozza is almost exactly average on cards and leash — 3.76 per match and 6.77 fouls per card over 17 Serie A matches, against a divisional 3.77 and 6.74 — but he showed 7 red cards in those 17, the highest rate of any official on this board, and 0.47 penalties per match. His two Lazio matches produced 4 and 8 cards, the second with two penalties. The clubs pull apart on fouls and converge on cards: Lazio commit 15.25 fouls per match, the joint-highest rate on this board and up from 10.58, while Venezia commit 11.00, down from 15.37 in Serie B; card rates are 2.00 and 2.50 against a divisional 3.12. Lazio’s foul rate rising by nearly five per match under a new coach who presses more is a structural reading, not form. The rating is held at OK because Sozza’s single 2026–27 appointment ran at 3.00 cards on 14.00 fouls — the lightest match of his in the record.
CornersOK — both low, and Venezia’s is oddly low for a side with 59% of the ball
Both clubs sit below the divisional 4.77 per team at one end or both, in both seasons. Lazio won 3.82 and conceded 4.21 last season and win 5.00 and concede 4.25 now — a rise that tracks their deep completions doubling from 4.53 to 8.25 and is therefore coherent. Venezia won 4.61 and conceded 4.55 in Serie B and win 3.00 and concede 3.50 now, which is the row that does not fit: a side with 59.20% possession and 13.25 shots per match winning three corners a game is unusual, and it suggests their possession is being held in areas that do not produce them. The figures are small at both ends, which limits how much the row can carry, but the mechanism behind Lazio’s is visible and Venezia’s is a genuine anomaly worth naming.

D · Early-season risk notes

  • Venezia’s entire 2025–26 record is from Serie B and its xG column does not exist. Eighty-two points, 77 goals, a 42.1% clean-sheet rate and four defeats came against a division running at 2.54 goals and 4.52 cards per match — a higher card rate than Serie A’s 3.12. No public source publishes Serie B expected goals, so nothing here converts that season into a level, and the squad that produced it has been rebuilt with roughly €44m.
  • Lazio’s 2025–26 numbers describe a team under a coach who has gone and a forward line that has been replaced. The 31.6% over-2.5 rate, the 28.9% BTTS and the 44.7% blank rate were produced under Sarri and then Tudor, with Dia and Pedro in attack. Gattuso arrived in the summer, Pinamonti and Guðmundsson came from Fiorentina and Frattesi from Internazionale. Every one of those three figures is the lowest of its kind on this board and none of them describes the current side.
  • Venezia’s possession and pressing figures say one thing and their results say another, and both are only four matches old. 59.20% of the ball and a PPDA of 7.32 — the most aggressive on this slate — with zero points, 2.75 conceded and 2.65 expected goals against. Either the approach is producing chances that have not converted, or it is a high line being punished; four matches, two of them against Milan and Fiorentina, cannot separate those.
  • No team news exists for either club in any source reached. Venezia’s XI is substantially new and Lazio have changed both centre-backs and both forwards, so the identity of the starting elevens carries more weight here than on most cards, and it is unavailable.
  • Most sensitive: the Asian handicap and 1X2 — Lazio have won three one-goal matches and drawn one, Venezia have lost four, and both clubs’ comparison seasons belong to different teams or different divisions. Least sensitive: the discipline row’s foul component and Venezia’s conceding side of BTTS — the first because Lazio’s rise from 10.58 to 15.25 fouls tracks a documented change in pressing, the second because no clean sheet in four is corroborated by 2.65 xG against, 15.00 shots faced and 8.50 deep completions conceded at once.

E · Short market-type guide

  • Venezia’s conceding side of BTTS is more robust than the match result here because no clean sheet in four is explained by four independent columns — 2.75 conceded, 2.65 xG against, 15.00 shots faced, 8.50 deep completions conceded — while the result question rests on a Serie B season with no xG and a Lazio side four matches into a new coach.
  • Lazio’s expected goals are more robust than their goal count because 1.82 xG per match is supported by deep completions nearly doubling from 4.53 to 8.25 and a new forward line in Pinamonti and Guðmundsson, whereas the 1.50 they have scored comes from four matches decided by a single goal each.
  • The foul component of the discipline row is more robust than the card component because Lazio’s rise from 10.58 to 15.25 fouls per match has a stated mechanism in Gattuso’s pressing — PPDA from 16.66 to 12.68 — while Sozza’s own card rate reads 3.76 across 17 matches, 6.60 in five Serie B matches and 3.00 in his only match this season.
  • A first-half total is more robust than a first-half result because Lazio have conceded no first-half goal in four and Venezia have conceded 1.00 per match, two clear and opposite readings, while three one-goal Lazio wins supply no margin inside 45 minutes.
  • The single fact that would most change which family I choose is whether Venezia’s 59% possession is producing chances or just territory. They have 1.80 expected goals per match, more than Lazio managed all last season, and no points; they also win only 3.00 corners a game from that possession, which is the figure that does not fit. What would settle it is a published breakdown of where their possession is held — final-third entries or touches in the box per match — and no source reached publishes one for Serie A at club level. Until it exists, the totals and team-goals rows both rest on whether their 1.80 xG is real creation or inflated by chasing four losing scorelines.

France · Ligue 1 · Journée 5

Angers SCO – ESTAC Troyes

A · Squad, promotion & transfer context

Troyes are promoted as Ligue 2 champions — 67 points from 34 matches, 20 wins, 7 draws, 7 defeats, 60 scored, 33 conceded — and are back in the top flight after three years away. Angers finished 13th in Ligue 1 on 36 points — 9 wins, 9 draws, 16 defeats, and just 29 goals scored, 0.85 per match, with 29.4% of their matches going over 2.5 against a divisional 52.6%. That is the lowest scoring rate of any club on this board. The structural headline is that the promoted club kept its promotion-winning coach and sold most of what won it, while the established club changed coach and has transformed at the only end that was broken. Angers’ expected goals have risen from 0.98 to 1.50 a game and their corners from 3.56 to 9.00; Troyes have banked more than €34m in departures and their arrivals column is close to empty.

  • Angers SCO — manager changed this summer, and the change reaches into two other fixtures on this card. Alexandre Dujeux left, a departure the French press called a surprise, and signed for FC Lorient — who play at this same kickoff tonight. Stéphane Gilli, 52, was appointed on a contract to 2028. He had been without a club since leaving Paris FC in February 2026, the club he had promoted to Ligue 1 a year earlier, and who also play on this slate. So the Angers bench connects to two other fixtures on this board in opposite directions: the coach who left is in the away dugout at Le Mans, and the coach who arrived built the Paris FC side playing at 17:15. No transfer fee for any Angers arrival or departure is published in the sources reached at this depth, and the club’s window is described only in general terms — several important players expected to leave, with recruitment shaped by whoever took the job.
  • ESTAC Troyes — promoted, and the window was overwhelmingly outgoing; manager unchanged. Stéphane Dumont remains head coach, appointed on 12 August 2024, the coach who won the division. Out, and this is the bulk of the window: Mathys Detourbet, a 19-year-old winger, to Manchester City for about €25m; Martin Adeline to Hamburg, €4m; Sankhoun Diawara to AC Milan, €2m; Patrick Beach to Melbourne, €1.5m; Amar Fatah to Lecce, €1.4m; Nicolas Lemaître, the goalkeeper, to Stade Rennais at the end of his contract; Thierno Baldé free to Nancy and Mounaïm El Idrissy free to Raja Casablanca. Out on loan: Hugo Picard to Columbus, Jérémy Le Douaron to Palermo, Thimothé Nkada to Standard Liège and Elijah Odede to Lommel. Loan returns out: Júnior Díaz to Brest, Yasser Larouci to Kifisia, Hugo Gambor to Gent and Tawfik Bentayeb — the Ligue 2 top scorer last season — back to Union Touarga. Roughly €34m of published outgoing fees, a goalkeeper gone, the division’s leading scorer gone, and no arrival with a published fee. Contradiction reported, not resolved: one source lists Karim Dermane, Patrick Beach and Thimothé Nkada as arrivals; the detailed club-by-club table lists all three as departures, with Nkada on loan to Standard Liège rather than from. The detailed table is internally consistent and the summary is not, but neither is preferred here.
  • Suspensions / absences. No injury or suspension list for either club is published by any source reached. That is the second Ligue 1 fixture on this slate where the team news simply is not available, and it matters here because Troyes’ squad has lost its goalkeeper, its leading scorer and five players for fees since May.
  • Sample. Angers: four competitive matches, all league — 0–2 v Lille (23 August), 3–1 at AJ Auxerre (29 August), 1–2 v Stade Rennais (6 September), 0–0 at Le Havre (12 September). Eleventh on 4 points, 4 scored and 5 conceded. Their home and away records are inverted from last season: 0.50 scored and 2.00 conceded per home match, against 1.50 and 0.50 away. Troyes: four competitive matches, all league — 0–0 v Paris FC (22 August), 2–1 at Lorient (29 August), 2–6 v Strasbourg (6 September), 0–2 at Lille (13 September). Twelfth on 4 points, 4 scored and 9 conceded. The 2–6 at home to Strasbourg is the single most distorting result on this card — they had 27 shots and won 11 corners to nil in it and lost by four. Both clubs have exactly four points from four matches.
  • Referee. Azzedine Souifi, confirmed, with Brice Parinet Le Tellier and Florent Marmion on the lines, Samir Zolota fourth official, and Pierre Gaillouste and Julien Schmitt on VAR. He is effectively a Ligue 2 official taking a Ligue 1 fixture: the ESPN record contains exactly one top-flight match for him, and it was Lorient–Troyes on 29 August 2026 — this same away club — which produced no cards at all, 20 fouls and a penalty. He has no Ligue 1 record for 2025–26 whatsoever. What does exist is the division below: Ligue 2 2025–26, 16 matches, 4.00 cards per match on 32.38 fouls — 8.09 fouls per card, a very long leash — with 0.38 penalties per match and 4 red cards; and Ligue 2 2026–27, 3 matches, 5.00 cards on 37.00 fouls, 7.40 fouls per card. For comparison, Ligue 1 ran at 3.91 cards and 6.17 fouls per card last season and Ligue 2 at 4.14 cards. By the research spec an official newly arrived in a division with almost no record there is itself a reason discipline markets carry more variance, and that is carried into section C. History: one match with Troyes, none with Angers.

B · Style & early-season reality

Angers have changed more than any Ligue 1 club on this board and the change is concentrated in the two columns that were worst. Under Gilli their expected goals have risen from 0.98 to 1.50 per match and their shots from 9.29 to 15.75 — both up by more than half — while corners won have gone from 3.56 to 9.00, the highest figure of any club carded on this slate, against a divisional 4.84. The pressing data explains where it comes from: PPDA has fallen from 18.48 to 13.24, so a side that last season stood off almost entirely now engages, and possession is up from 41.92% to 48.67%. The defensive side has improved too but less dramatically — expected goals against from 1.77 to 1.59, deep completions conceded from 7.21 to 7.50, essentially flat. Two things have not followed the chances. The goals: 1.00 per match against 1.50 xG, with only 3.00 shots on target, so they are creating volume rather than quality. And the venue split has inverted: 0.50 scored and 2.00 conceded at home against 1.50 and 0.50 away, where last season they were 1.06/1.18 at home and 0.65/1.65 away.

Troyes are playing a deep, disciplined, very low-contact game and conceding almost nothing in dead-ball terms while being beaten in open play. Possession 38.85% against 51.76% in Ligue 2; PPDA 17.11, the second most passive on this board, against opponents pressing them at 8.32. The two numbers that stand out are both league extremes and both point the same way: 0.75 cards per match — three in four matches, the lowest of any club on this board against a divisional 2.95 — and 1.50 corners conceded per match, also the lowest, against a divisional 4.84. A side that does not foul (11.25, under the divisional 11.74, and down from 13.26 in Ligue 2) and does not concede corners is being hurt another way: expected goals against 2.03 per match and 9.50 deep completions conceded, against 0.97 goals conceded and a 35.3% clean-sheet rate in the division below. Their own attacking figures have held up better than the defensive ones — 13.75 shots against 13.76 in Ligue 2 — but the conversion has collapsed, 2.75 shots on target now against 5.09 then. The evidence that this is the level rather than a bad month is four matches of possession, pressing and shot-on-target data all moving together, against a promotion season whose xG nobody published.

C · Market-type suitability

1X2 / MoneylineDangerous
Both clubs have four points from four matches and neither record means what it looks like. Angers changed head coach and their underlying numbers have moved further than any Ligue 1 club here — xG from 0.98 to 1.50, shots from 9.29 to 15.75 — on four matches. Troyes are promoted, sold roughly €34m of players including their goalkeeper and the division’s top scorer, and their comparison season is in Ligue 2 with no xG. The divisional home-win rate is the most extreme input of all: 29.7% this season against 46.1% last, with 35.1% draws — so the base rate a home side is read against has itself collapsed, over 37 matches.
Asian handicap / DNBDangerous
Angers’ four margins are −2, +2, −1 and 0; Troyes’ are 0, +1, −4 and −2. A handicap needs a central estimate and Troyes’ distribution has a four-goal tail in a four-match sample — the 2–6 against Strasbourg alone carries most of their 2.25 conceded and much of their 2.03 expected goals against. Angers’ venue split makes their side no better: they have scored 0.50 and conceded 2.00 per home match this season having run at 1.06 and 1.18 at home last season. Two goal differences of −1 and −5 from four matches each, with a 35.1% divisional draw rate behind them.
Totals (O/U goals)OK — identical current rates, and the histories disagree sharply
Both clubs sit at exactly 50.0% over 2.5 this season, just under a divisional 54.1%, and their matches have produced 9 and 13 goals respectively. The histories pull apart hard: Angers went over 2.5 in only 29.4% of 34 matches last season — the lowest rate of any club on this board, against a divisional 52.6% — scoring 29 goals all year. Troyes went over in 55.9% of a Ligue 2 season running at 47.4%. The xG lines sum to 2.76 and 3.62 in the two directions, straddling the divisional 2.76 per match. Ligue 1 is also the one division on this board scoring below its own 2025–26 rate, at 2.76 against 2.82, which at least means the baseline here is not moving the way the others are.
BTTSOK — both at 50%, and both blank halves are well evidenced
The symmetry here is real rather than coincidental: both clubs have had both teams score in 2 of 4 and both have failed to score in 2 of 4, against a divisional 56.8% and 27.0%. Each side’s inability to score has a mechanism: Angers manage 3.00 shots on target per match from 15.75 shots, and Troyes 2.75 from 13.75 — the two lowest on-target rates relative to volume on this board. Angers’ 35.3% blank rate last season and Troyes’ 20.6% in Ligue 2 sit either side of the current 50%. The conceding half is where they differ: Troyes are at 2.03 expected goals against and Angers at 1.59.
Team goalsSplit — Angers’ creation is evidenced, their conversion is not
Angers are the more interesting half. They have gone from 0.85 goals and 0.98 expected goals per match to 1.00 and 1.50, with shots up from 9.29 to 15.75 and corners from 3.56 to 9.00 — a genuine increase in volume under a new coach — but shots on target have barely moved, 3.26 to 3.00, so the extra shots are not extra chances on goal. Troyes have scored 1.00 per match on 1.26 xG after 1.76 in Ligue 2, with shots on target down from 5.09 to 2.75. Both clubs are creating volume and converting at the lowest on-target rates on this board, which is a coherent shared finding and a four-match one.
Halves (1H result, 1H goals)OK — Angers’ first-half record has changed markedly
The timing columns carry a real fact on the home side. Angers scored 0.26 first-half goals per match last season — one goal every four matches, the lowest figure on this board by a distance — and conceded 0.71. This season they score 0.50 and concede 1.25. Troyes sit at 0.75 and 0.75, against 0.68 and 0.53 in Ligue 2. Against a divisional 1.22 per match, the fixture’s combined first-half rate sits close to the line rather than under it, which is a change from what Angers’ history would suggest. A first-half result is unreadable: four matches each, with Angers conceding more before the break than they score.
Discipline (cards, fouls)Dangerous — the official has no record at this level
The club side of this row is exceptionally clean and the referee side is nearly empty, and the spec is explicit that a discipline rating which cannot rest on the official is incomplete. Troyes take 0.75 cards per match — three in four matches, the lowest rate on this board against a divisional 2.95 — and Angers 1.50, both on foul rates at or below the divisional 11.74. Both were also well under their divisional card rates last season. Against that: Souifi has exactly one Ligue 1 match on record, which produced zero cards, and no 2025–26 top-flight season at all. His Ligue 2 record is real but describes a different division — 4.00 cards on 8.09 fouls per card across 16 matches, a much longer leash than Ligue 1’s 6.17 — and a referee new to a level is a source of variance in his own right. Everything about the clubs points low; the largest single input is unmeasured.
CornersWell-suited — two league-extreme figures, each with a visible mechanism
This fixture contains both ends of the corner distribution on this board. Angers win 9.00 corners per match, the highest figure of any club carded today, against 3.56 last season and a divisional 4.84 — and it is corroborated rather than isolated, moving in step with shots rising from 9.29 to 15.75, possession from 41.92% to 48.67% and PPDA from 18.48 to 13.24. Their per-match counts read 8, 10, 12 and 6, so it repeats. Troyes concede 1.50 corners per match, the lowest figure on this board, against 3.71 in Ligue 2; their counts conceded read 2, 2, 0 and 2 — including conceding none in a match they lost 2–6, which is the clearest possible sign that this is a property of how they defend rather than of how well. One side generates corners at twice the divisional rate and the other concedes them at a third of it, over four matches each, with the style data agreeing on both.

D · Early-season risk notes

  • Troyes’ entire 2025–26 record is from Ligue 2 and its xG column does not exist. Sixty-seven points, 60 goals, a 35.3% clean-sheet rate and 2.24 cards per match came in a division running at 2.53 goals and 4.14 cards. No public source publishes Ligue 2 expected goals, and the squad that produced that season has since lost its goalkeeper to Rennes, its leading scorer to a loan return and five players for roughly €34m.
  • Angers’ transformation is four matches old and measured against a very low base. Going from 0.98 to 1.50 expected goals per match is a large relative move, but the starting point was the worst attacking record on this board — 29 goals in 34 matches. The shots-on-target column has not moved at all (3.26 to 3.00), so the improvement is in volume rather than in quality, and the coach responsible arrived this summer.
  • One result distorts most of Troyes’ defensive numbers. The 2–6 at home to Strasbourg on 6 September contributed four of their nine goals conceded and much of the 2.03 expected goals against. It cannot be excluded, and it is also the match in which they took 27 shots and won 11 corners to nil — so it distorts the attacking and corner columns in the opposite direction at the same time.
  • The referee has no meaningful record in this division. One Ligue 1 match, zero cards in it, and no 2025–26 top-flight season. His 16-match Ligue 2 record at 8.09 fouls per card describes a division that books more often and whistles more often than this one. That is why the discipline row is rated dangerous despite both clubs having the cleanest card rates on the board.
  • Ligue 1’s home-win rate is the most extreme divisional figure on this slate. 29.7% against 46.1% last season, with draws at 35.1%, over 37 matches. It is reported as an observed rate rather than a trend, but every row above that touches the result or the margin is being read against a baseline that has moved by sixteen points.
  • Most sensitive: the discipline row, which has no usable referee input, and the Asian handicap, which has a four-goal tail inside a four-match sample. Least sensitive: corners — Angers’ 9.00 won repeats across all four matches and tracks their shots, possession and pressing together, and Troyes’ 1.50 conceded repeats across all four including the match they lost by four.

E · Short market-type guide

  • Corners are more robust than the match result here because Angers’ 9.00 won and Troyes’ 1.50 conceded each repeat in every one of four matches and each moves in step with three other columns — shots, possession and PPDA on one side, possession and deep completions on the other — while the result rests on a new coach with four matches and a promoted side whose comparison season has no xG.
  • Angers’ shot volume is more robust than their goal rate because 15.75 shots and 9.00 corners per match are corroborated by possession and pressing data, whereas their 3.00 shots on target is essentially unchanged from last season’s 3.26 and their goals have risen only from 0.85 to 1.00.
  • BTTS is more robust than a first-half result here because both clubs’ blank halves have the same documented mechanism — the two lowest on-target conversion rates on this board — while four matches each and a 35.1% divisional draw rate leave no readable margin inside 45 minutes.
  • The clubs’ card rates are more robust than the discipline row as a whole because Troyes’ 0.75 and Angers’ 1.50 per match each continue a below-divisional rate from last season in their own divisions, while the official has one match at this level and zero cards in it.
  • The single fact that would most change which family I choose is a Ligue 1 card record for Azzedine Souifi. Both clubs are the cleanest on this board and a lenient official would compound that, while his Ligue 2 line of 4.00 cards per match sits above Ligue 1’s own 3.91 — the two readings point opposite ways and one match at this level cannot separate them. What would settle it is four or five more Ligue 1 appointments, or a published French federation breakdown of his record by competition; neither exists in any source reached today, and until one does the discipline row is the weakest on this card despite having the strongest club evidence.

A · Squad, promotion & transfer context

Le Mans are promoted, as Ligue 2 runners-up on 62 points — 16 wins, 14 draws, 4 defeats, 50 scored, 31 conceded — and are back in the top flight after seventeen years away, the longest absence of any promoted club on this slate. Promotion was secured on 10 May 2026 with a 2–0 win over Bastia. Their Ligue 2 season was built on defence: a 44.1% clean-sheet rate against a 29.6% divisional baseline, 0.91 conceded per match, and only 35.3% of matches going over 2.5 against a 47.4% baseline. Lorient finished 9th in Ligue 1 on 45 points — 11 wins, 12 draws, 11 defeats, 48 scored, 51 conceded. The structural headline is a coaching swap that runs straight through this board: Lorient’s new head coach is Alexandre Dujeux, appointed on 9 June 2026, who left Angers — the club playing at this same kickoff on the other Ligue 1 card, under the man who replaced him. Le Mans, by contrast, kept and extended the coach who took them up.

  • Le Mans FC — promoted, continuity at the top, and a modest window; manager unchanged and extended. Patrick Videira was extended after promotion: in two seasons he has taken the club from the National up to Ligue 1, finishing second in Ligue 2. Two coaches, Alexis Baudet and Johan Liébus, joined his staff; the goalkeeping coach Sébastien Moncé was not retained for the Ligue 1 season. Off the field the club changed president — Thierry Gomez announced his departure on 16 June and was replaced by Pedro Oliveira — and the DNCG cleared the club to go up. In: Billal Brahimi, an Algeria international, from Santos FC on 6 July, and Louis Mafouta after a strong Ligue 2 season. Retained: Antoine Rabillard extended, and Erwan Colas extended to 2029. No fee is published for either arrival in any source reached, and the incoming list is the shortest of any promoted club on this board — against Ipswich’s twelve, Coventry’s eleven and Hull’s eighteen.
  • FC Lorient — turnover high by volume, described as continuity rather than revolution; manager changed 9 June 2026. Alexandre Dujeux was appointed on 9 June 2026 on a two-year deal with an option for a third. He had been Angers’ coach since March 2023, taking them up in 2024 and keeping them up twice; Stéphane Gilli replaced him at Angers, and both clubs kick off at 20:45 tonight. In — seven: Trevan Sanusi from Newcastle, Bamo Meïté from Marseille, Dermane Karim from Lommel — the club Troyes sold him to, which ties this fixture to the other Ligue 1 card — Abdoulaye Faye from Bayer Leverkusen, Isak Jensen on loan from AZ Alkmaar, and Nosa Obaretin from Napoli with a €3.5m option. Out — fourteen: Joel Mvuka to Celtic, Formose Mendy to Watford, Gédéon Kalulu to Aris Limassol, Sirine Doucouré to TOP Oss and Dembo Sylla to Red Star. Contradiction reported, not resolved: an Abdoulaye Faye is listed here as joining Lorient from Bayer Leverkusen, and this board’s 18:30 card has an Abdoulaye Faye joining Celta Vigo on loan from Bayer Leverkusen on 3 August 2026. Those may be two players or one; no source reached distinguishes them.
  • Suspensions / absences. No injury or suspension list for either club is published by any source reached. That is the third Ligue 1 fixture on this slate with no available team news, and it is the general state of the division on this board rather than a quirk of this match.
  • Sample. Le Mans: four competitive matches, all league — 2–2 v Brest (22 August), 2–3 at Stade Rennais (30 August), 1–1 at Nice (5 September), 2–2 v Lens (13 September). Fourteenth on 3 points from three draws and one defeat — no wins, but also no match in which they failed to score and none in which they kept a clean sheet: both teams have scored in all four. Lorient: four competitive matches, all league — 0–0 at Nice (22 August), 1–2 v Troyes (29 August), 1–0 at Lens (5 September), 2–2 v Toulouse (12 September). Ninth on 5 points, 4 scored and 4 conceded. Their away record is two matches without conceding at all: 0.50 scored and 0.00 conceded, against 1.50 and 2.00 at home. Three of the eight matches between them have been draws with goals.
  • Referee. Geoffrey Kubler, confirmed, with Aurélien Drouet and Hicham Zakrani on the lines, Gabriel Henry fourth official, and Jérémie Pignard and Ludovic Zmyslony on VAR. Like the official appointed to the other Ligue 1 fixture on this board, he is a Ligue 2 referee with one top-flight match to his name: the ESPN record contains exactly one Ligue 1 appointment for him — Angers–Stade Rennais on 6 September 2026, which produced 2 cards on 25 fouls, 12.5 fouls per card and no penalty — and no 2025–26 Ligue 1 record at all. His Ligue 2 record is substantial and points the other way: 2025–26, 16 matches, 5.31 cards per match on 26.62 fouls — 5.01 fouls per card, a short leash — with 0.38 penalties per match and 6 red cards; and 2026–27, 2 matches at 4.00 cards. Ligue 1 ran at 3.91 cards and 6.17 fouls per card last season; Ligue 2 at 4.14, so his 5.31 was above even the second division’s own rate. History: two Le Mans matches last season, both in Ligue 2 and both heavy — Pau–Le Mans on 21 November 2025 gave 6 cards on 25 fouls and a penalty, and Le Mans–Dunkerque on 26 January 2026 gave 6 cards on 29 fouls. No Lorient match appears in his record. By the research spec an official with almost no record at this level is itself a source of variance, and that is carried into section C.
