B · Style & early-season reality

Le Mans have inverted the profile that got them promoted, and the inversion is total. In Ligue 2 they conceded 0.91 goals per match, kept a clean sheet in 44.1% of matches and saw only 35.3% go over 2.5 — a defensive side in a division averaging 2.53 goals. In Ligue 1 they concede 2.00, have kept no clean sheet in four, and 75% of their matches have gone over 2.5 with both teams scoring in every one. The shot data explains it: 11.18 shots against 9.26 faced in Ligue 2, against 10.25 against 18.00 faced now, and the corner column says the same thing more sharply — 5.41 won and 4.41 conceded then, 3.00 and 8.00 now, the third-heaviest corner deficit on this board. Their expected goals against sit at 2.35 per match. What has survived is the attacking end and the discipline: 1.75 goals per match against 1.47 in Ligue 2, on 1.71 expected goals, with shots on target at 4.50 against 4.41 — almost unchanged — and the foul rate down from 16.88 to 14.25. This is a side still scoring at its promotion rate while being taken apart at the other end.

Lorient under Dujeux are playing the quietest football on this board and the numbers are almost eerily low. 7.75 fouls per match — the lowest rate of any club carded on this slate, against a divisional 11.74 and their own 9.59 last season — and 0.50 cards per match, two in four matches. They are not pressing to produce that: PPDA 18.02, against 17.74 last season, essentially unchanged and the most passive figure here bar one. Possession is up marginally at 47.73% against 45.36%, and the chance numbers have drifted down at both ends: expected goals from 1.44 to 1.18, expected goals against from 1.56 to 1.30, and non-penalty xG against down to 1.11. So the new coach has made them slightly harder to play through and slightly less dangerous, which is consistent with two clean sheets in four and 1.00 goal per match. The corner column is the one that has moved against them: 3.53 won and 5.38 conceded last season, 2.25 and 6.25 now, and they won no corners at all in the win at Lens. The evidence that this is Dujeux’s side rather than last season’s is four matches, a foul rate down by nearly two per game and a 0.26-goal fall in expected goals against — not a season.

C · Market-type suitability

1X2 / MoneylineDangerous
Le Mans are promoted after seventeen years away with two published signings and a comparison season in Ligue 2 that has no xG; Lorient changed head coach on 9 June and have four matches under him. Neither club has won more than once in four — Le Mans have not won at all. And the base rate is the most extreme on this board: Ligue 1 is running at a 29.7% home-win rate against 46.1% last season, with 35.1% draws over 37 matches, so the divisional prior a home side is read against has itself moved by sixteen points. Nothing here supports a result.
Asian handicap / DNBDangerous
Le Mans’ four margins are 0, −1, 0 and 0 — three draws and a one-goal defeat, a goal difference of −1 across four matches. Lorient’s are 0, −1, +1 and 0. Six of the eight results are draws or one-goal margins, and a side that has drawn three of four supplies no margin at all. Lorient’s venue split is the other problem: 1.50 scored and 2.00 conceded per home match against 0.50 and 0.00 away, over two matches each. With Ligue 1’s draw rate at 35.1%, this is the least readable margin question on the board.
Totals (O/U goals)Split — Le Mans’ matches are loud, and that contradicts their own history
The two halves disagree and one of them disagrees with itself. Le Mans’ four matches have produced 15 goals — 75.0% over 2.5, and every match has finished 2–2, 2–3, 1–1 or 2–2 — from a side that went over in only 35.3% of a Ligue 2 season. Their xG line of 1.71 for and 2.35 against supports the current reading rather than the old one. Lorient sit at 50.0% over 2.5 on 1.18 and 1.30 xG, against 44.1% last season. Ligue 1 is the only division on this board running below its own 2025–26 goals rate (2.76 against 2.82), so the divisional baseline is the one stable input and it points slightly down.
BTTSWell-suited — the home side’s record is complete and mechanically supported
This is the best-evidenced family here. Le Mans have had both teams score in 4 of 4, with no clean sheet and no blank — 0.0% on both columns against a divisional 27.0% — and both halves of that have a mechanism. Conceding: 2.00 goals and 2.35 expected goals against per match, 18.00 shots faced and 8.00 corners conceded, up from 9.26 shots faced in Ligue 2. Scoring: 1.75 goals on 1.71 xG with 4.50 shots on target, essentially unchanged from their promotion season’s 4.41 — so the attack has travelled even though the defence has not. Lorient are the softer input at 50.0%, but they have failed to score in only one of four and conceded in two. The caveat is that Le Mans kept a clean sheet in 44.1% of matches last season, so this is the division’s doing, not the club’s.
Team goalsSplit — Le Mans’ scoring has travelled, Lorient’s has drifted down
Le Mans are the better-evidenced half and unusually so for a promoted club: 1.75 goals per match against 1.47 in Ligue 2, on 1.71 expected goals, with shots on target at 4.50 against 4.41 — the one column on their card that has not moved at all between divisions. Lorient have scored 1.00 per match on 1.18 xG, down from 1.41 and 1.44 last season, with shots on target at 3.50 against 3.94. Neither club is outscoring its expected goals, which means both rates rest on chance creation rather than on finishing. The limit is four matches each and, on the Le Mans side, a promotion season with no xG to anchor it.
Halves (1H result, 1H goals)OK — Le Mans’ first-half rate has quadrupled at one end
The timing columns carry a real change. Le Mans conceded 0.29 first-half goals per match in Ligue 2 — one every three and a half matches — and concede 1.25 now, more than four times as many, while their own first-half scoring has risen from 0.74 to 1.25. Lorient sit at 0.50 and 0.50, close to last season’s 0.59 and 0.50 and among the quietest first halves here. Against a divisional 1.22 per match, this fixture’s combined first-half rate is driven almost entirely by the home side, and by a figure four matches old that contradicts 34. A first-half result is unreadable from three draws in four.
Discipline (cards, fouls)Dangerous — the official has no record at this level, and his lower-division record points the opposite way
The clubs could hardly be further apart and the referee evidence is nearly absent, which is exactly the state the spec calls incomplete. Lorient commit 7.75 fouls and take 0.50 cards per match — both the lowest figures of any club carded on this slate, against a divisional 11.74 and 2.95. Le Mans commit 14.25 and take 2.00, down from 16.88 fouls in Ligue 2 but still the higher of the two by a distance. Against that: Kubler has one Ligue 1 match on record, which gave 2 cards on 25 fouls — 12.5 fouls per card, the most lenient single reading anywhere in this data — and no 2025–26 top-flight season at all. His Ligue 2 record says the opposite: 5.31 cards per match at 5.01 fouls per card over 16 matches, above even Ligue 2’s own 4.14, and his two previous Le Mans matches both produced 6 cards. One match at 12.5 fouls per card and sixteen at 5.01 cannot both be the right prior.
CornersOK — both concede heavily, and one figure is a clean division effect
Both clubs are well below the divisional 4.84 at one end and above it at the other. Le Mans have gone from winning 5.41 and conceding 4.41 in Ligue 2 to winning 3.00 and conceding 8.00 — a swing of nearly six corners per match across the two columns, and it tracks their shots faced rising from 9.26 to 18.00, so the mechanism is the change of level rather than a change of approach. Their counts conceded read 10, 8, 5 and 9, so it repeats. Lorient win 2.25 and concede 6.25, against 3.53 and 5.38 last season — they won no corners at all in one match — which is a continuation of an existing below-average profile rather than a break from it. Two sides that concede more corners than they win, in both seasons and in both divisions.

D · Early-season risk notes

  • Le Mans’ entire 2025–26 record is from Ligue 2 and its xG column does not exist. Sixty-two points, a 44.1% clean-sheet rate and a 35.3% over-2.5 rate came in a division running at 2.53 goals and 4.14 cards per match. No public source publishes Ligue 2 expected goals, so there is nothing to anchor their defensive collapse against — the 0.91 conceded then and the 2.00 conceded now cannot be compared on chances, only on goals.
  • Every figure on the Le Mans side is a four-match figure and three of the four were draws. 2–2, 2–3, 1–1, 2–2 — a set of results that produces a 100% BTTS rate, a 75% over-2.5 rate, a 0% clean-sheet rate and a 0% blank rate simultaneously. Those four rates are not four pieces of evidence; they are one four-match pattern seen from four angles, and a single 1–0 would move all of them.
  • The referee evidence contradicts itself and there is not enough of it either way. One Ligue 1 match at 12.5 fouls per card; sixteen Ligue 2 matches at 5.01 and 5.31 cards per game, with six red cards; two previous Le Mans matches at 6 cards each. A referee new to a division is a source of variance in his own right, and here the two available priors point in opposite directions. The same is true of the other Ligue 1 fixture carded on this board, which has a different official in the identical situation.
  • Lorient’s numbers are four matches into a new coach and they are low in every direction. 7.75 fouls, 0.50 cards, 1.18 expected goals, 1.30 against, 2.25 corners won — a very quiet set of readings. Two clean sheets in four came in the two away matches, where they have conceded nothing at all; at home they have conceded 2.00 per match. That split is two matches per venue.
  • Ligue 1’s home-win rate is the most extreme divisional figure on this slate — 29.7% against 46.1% last season, with 35.1% draws over 37 matches. On a fixture where the home side has drawn three of four and the away side two of four, that baseline is doing more work than usual and is itself a fifth of a season old.
  • Most sensitive: the discipline row, whose largest input has one match of relevant record pointing the opposite way to sixteen matches of lower-division record, and the Asian handicap, with six draws or one-goal results in eight. Least sensitive: BTTS and Le Mans’ team-goal rate — the first because both halves of their 4-from-4 are supported by shots, corners and expected goals at both ends, the second because their shots on target have not moved between divisions at all.

E · Short market-type guide

  • BTTS is more robust than the match result here because Le Mans’ 4-from-4 has a mechanism at each end — 18.00 shots faced and 2.35 xG against for the conceding half, 4.50 shots on target and 1.71 xG for the scoring half — while the result question sets a promoted side with no wins against a club four matches into a new coach, under a 29.7% divisional home-win rate.
  • Le Mans’ scoring rate is more robust than their conceding rate because shots on target have barely moved between divisions (4.41 to 4.50) and their goals have actually risen, whereas the 2.00 they concede has no Ligue 2 expected-goals figure to be compared against and rests on four matches.
  • Lorient’s foul rate is more robust than the discipline row as a whole because 7.75 fouls per match continues a below-divisional 9.59 from last season in the same division, while the referee’s own two priors — one Ligue 1 match at 12.5 fouls per card and sixteen Ligue 2 matches at 5.01 — cannot both hold.
  • A first-half total is more robust than a first-half result because Le Mans’ first-half figures have moved by a factor of four at one end and Lorient’s have not moved at all, which is two distinguishable inputs, whereas three draws in four leave no margin inside 45 minutes.
  • The single fact that would most change which family I choose is a Ligue 1 card record for Geoffrey Kubler. His one top-flight match ran at 12.5 fouls per card — twice the divisional 6.17 — while his sixteen Ligue 2 matches ran at 5.01 with six red cards, and his two previous Le Mans matches gave 6 cards each. What would settle it is four or five more Ligue 1 appointments, or a published French federation breakdown of his record by competition; neither exists in any source reached today. Until one does, a fixture containing the lowest foul rate on this board has an unmeasurable discipline row — and the same is true of the other Ligue 1 card here, which is a statement about the division’s refereeing data rather than about either match.

France · Ligue 1 · Journée 5

Olympique Lyonnais – Stade Rennais

A · Squad, promotion & transfer context

Neither club is promoted and neither is new to this division. Lyon finished 4th in 2025–26 on 60 points — 18 wins, 6 draws, 10 defeats, 53 scored, 40 conceded — and reached the last 16 of the Europa League before losing to Celta Vigo. Rennes finished 6th on 59 points — 17 wins, 8 draws, 9 defeats, 59 scored, 50 conceded. Both are unbeaten this season and sit third and fourth. The structural headline is that both squads were rebuilt in the same two places — the forward line and the defensive line — and both changes are already visible in the chance data, pointing opposite ways. Lyon sold €74.6m of attackers and replaced them with a €1m transfer and two loans, and are creating exactly as much as last season while conceding far less. Rennes sold a centre-back for £60m and rebuilt the forward line, and have gone from 1.60 expected goals per match to 2.31 while their corners conceded have nearly doubled.

  • Lyon — turnover high on the flanks and at centre-forward; manager unchanged. Paulo Fonseca continues as head coach. Attack — the headline: out — Malick Fofana to Sunderland, €35m (1 September), Afonso Moreira to Bayer Leverkusen, €33.6m, Martín Satriano to Getafe €6m, with Endrick’s and Roman Yaremchuk’s loans ended; in — Keito Nakamura from Reims, €1m plus €19m in bonuses (2 September), Loïs Openda on loan from Juventus (€3.5m) and Julien Duranville from Borussia Dortmund, €5m. Midfield: in — Mads Bidstrup from Red Bull Salzburg, €10.5m, and Kaïl Boudache from Nice, €5m; out on loan — Orel Mangala to Getafe, Mahamadou Diawara to Red Star. Defence: in — Felix Bacher from Estoril €4.3m, Noham Kamara from Paris Saint-Germain €4.1m, Mohamed Ouédraogo from Altach €2.2m, Zachary Athekame on loan from Milan; out — Duje Čaleta-Car to Sassuolo, fee undisclosed, and Ainsley Maitland-Niles to Everton, €3.5m. Goalkeeper: Matt Turner returned from loan and was loaned back to New England Revolution; Dominik Greif and Rémy Descamps remain. Roughly €74.6m of attacking sales against €1m of attacking transfer fees plus two loans.
  • Stade Rennais — turnover high at centre-back and centre-forward; manager changed last season and has since been extended. Habib Beye left on 9 February 2026; Sébastien Tambouret was caretaker until Franck Haise was appointed on 18 February 2026, and Haise’s contract has since been extended by a season — so this is his first full campaign. Centre-back: out — Jérémy Jacquet to Liverpool, £60m, Lilian Brassier to Eintracht Frankfurt (1 September), Leo Østigård’s loan to Genoa made permanent, Mikayil Faye loaned to Nürnberg; in — Charlie Cresswell from Toulouse (4 August) and Gonçalo Oliveira from Benfica (€3.5m on Benfica’s ledger). Attack: in — Boulaye Dia from Lazio (1 September), Eliezer Mayenda from Sunderland, Issa Soumaré free from Le Havre; out — Breel Embolo to Atlanta United, Albert Grønbæk to Hamburger SV. Defensive midfield: Jordan James to Wolves; Seko Fofana loaned to Porto; Glen Kamara loaned to QPR; Adrien Thomasson free from Lens. Goalkeeper: Nicolas Lemaître free from Troyes; Brice Samba remains. Four of Rennes’ moves involve clubs kicking off at 20:45 tonight: Cresswell from Toulouse and Soumaré from Le Havre, who play each other; Dia from Lazio; and Lemaître from Troyes.
  • Suspensions / absences. No injury or suspension is reported for either club in any source reached, and that was also the case when both were carded on this board three days ago — no Lyon injury was reported before their Europa League tie at Anderlecht on 16 September, and no Rennes absentee before their tie at Sturm Graz. One caution carries forward from that research: the only Rennes probable eleven reached then named Przemysław Frankowski, whose loan ended in June, so probable elevens for this club have already proved unreliable and none is relied on here.
  • Sample. Lyon: nine competitive matches, four in the league — 2–0 at Toulouse (22 August), 1–1 v Le Havre (29 August), 3–1 v AJ Auxerre (4 September), 0–0 at Paris FC (12 September) — plus four Champions League qualifiers (Sparta Prague and Fenerbahçe home and away, losing the tie) and a Europa League league-phase tie at Anderlecht on 16 September. Fourth on 8 points, unbeaten, 6 scored and 2 conceded, with no goal conceded in two away matches. Rennes: five competitive matches, four in the league — 2–2 v Paris Saint-Germain (23 August), 3–2 v Le Mans (30 August), 2–1 at Angers (6 September), 1–0 v Marseille (11 September) — plus a Europa League tie at Sturm Graz on 16 September. Third on 10 points, unbeaten, 8 scored and 5 conceded, having scored in every match. Two of Rennes’ four league opponents, Le Mans and Angers, kick off at 20:45 tonight.
  • Referee. Mathieu Vernice, confirmed, with Steven Torregrossa and Florian Goncalves de Araujo on the lines, Ruddy Buquet as fourth official and Eric Wattellier and Cédric Dos Santos on VAR. Record, counted from every match he refereed in the ESPN record: Ligue 1 2025–26, 15 matches, 4.33 cards per match on 27.53 fouls — 6.35 fouls per card, against a divisional 6.17 — with 0.40 penalties per match and 4 red cards. He books above the divisional rate (4.33 against 3.91) on the highest foul count of any regular Ligue 1 official. 2026–27 so far, 2 matches, 2.00 cards on 26.00 fouls — 13.0 fouls per card, far more lenient — but 3 penalties in those 2 matches, a rate of 1.50 per match against his own 0.40 last season. He also took three Ligue 2 matches last season at 6.00 cards and 1.00 penalties per match. History with these clubs is the deepest of any referee on this board: five matches last season, three of them Lyon’s and all three heavy — Lille–Lyon on 28 September 2025 gave 8 cards on 44 fouls, Lorient–Lyon on 7 December gave 5 on 33, and Nantes–Lyon on 7 February gave 6 on 24: an average of 6.33 cards. His two Rennes matches were quieter but both gave penalties — Nantes–Rennes on 20 September 2025, 4 cards and two penalties, and Rennes–Angers on 11 April, 2 cards and one.
  • Not sourced. Fees for most Rennes arrivals and for the Brassier, Embolo and James sales, none published in the sources reached; the Čaleta-Car fee. Any injury or suspension list for either club. Understat’s Rennes log was flagged as disagreeing with the published results when these clubs were carded on 16 September; it now reconciles — four matches, 8 goals, 2.31 expected goals — and is used here with that history stated. No source characterises Vernice’s reading of contact in the box in words, which matters more than usual given his 1.50 penalties per match this season; the figure is counted, not described.

B · Style & early-season reality

Lyon are almost exactly the side they were, with one change: they have stopped conceding. Possession 54.12% against 54.61%, PPDA 10.99 against 10.68, expected goals 1.59 against 1.62 — three figures essentially unmoved across a summer in which €74.6m of attackers left. What has moved is the defensive column: expected goals against down from 1.41 to 1.19, goals conceded from 1.18 to 0.50, with deep completions conceded flat at 5.50 against 5.12 — so the improvement is in the quality of what they allow rather than the volume. Their deep completions for have risen from 7.38 to 8.75, which is the clearest sign the attack still functions without Fofana and Moreira. The away record is the striking number: two matches, nothing conceded, 1.00 scored. The caution is fixture-shaped rather than statistical: this is their ninth competitive match in six weeks, including four Champions League qualifiers and a Europa League tie in Brussels three days ago — the heaviest schedule of any club on this board, against opponents who have played five.

Rennes have changed more, and the direction is attacking. Expected goals have risen from 1.60 to 2.31 per match and non-penalty xG from 1.47 to 2.12; shots from 14.15 to 17.00, shots on target from 4.97 to 5.75, deep completions from 6.41 to 8.50. That is a side creating substantially more in Haise’s first full season, with Dia, Mayenda and Soumaré added to the forward line. The defensive side has improved on chances but not on territory: expected goals against down from 1.77 to 1.63, yet corners conceded up from 4.62 to 8.25 per match, the third-highest figure on this board, and possession down from 53.53% to 49.77%. The corner column is where the rebuilt centre-back line shows — Jacquet gone for £60m, Brassier gone, Østigård made permanent elsewhere, Cresswell and Oliveira in. Their per-match corners conceded read 10, 6, 12 and 5, so it is a pattern rather than one match, and the 10 came against Paris Saint-Germain with 30.5% of the ball.

C · Market-type suitability

1X2 / MoneylineOK — both sides well documented, but the schedule is unmeasured
This is one of the better-evidenced result questions on the board. Both clubs finished 4th and 6th on 60 and 59 points, both kept or settled their coach, both are unbeaten, and both have chance data agreeing with their results: Lyon 1.59 xG and 1.19 against, Rennes 2.31 and 1.63. What is not evidenced is fatigue. Lyon are playing their ninth competitive match since early August, including four Champions League qualifiers and a Europa League tie in Brussels three days ago; Rennes have played five. The divisional home-win rate of 29.7% against 46.1% last season is the other live input, and it rests on 37 matches.
Asian handicap / DNBDangerous
Lyon’s four margins are +2, 0, +2, 0; Rennes’ are 0, +1, +1, +1. Seven of the eight results are a draw or a one-to-two-goal win and neither club has lost — a distribution with no downside tail at all, which is exactly what makes a margin unreadable. Lyon’s venue split compounds it: 2.00 scored and 1.00 conceded at home against 1.00 and 0.00 away, two matches each. Ligue 1’s 35.1% draw rate is the highest of the five divisions on this board.
Totals (O/U goals)Split — two clubs at opposite ends, both with a season behind them
The disagreement here is unusually well grounded because both clubs have 34-match records pointing the same way as their current four. Rennes are at 75.0% over 2.5 this season after 70.6% last, against a divisional 52.6% — a standing property rather than a run — with 2.31 xG for and 1.63 against. Lyon are at 25.0% after 55.9%, with 1.59 and 1.19. One club reliably plays high-scoring matches; the other has just stopped. The divisional line is 54.1%, and Ligue 1 is the only division on this board scoring below its own 2025–26 rate (2.76 against 2.82), which tilts the tie-break slightly toward Lyon’s reading.
BTTSOK — Rennes always score, Lyon have stopped conceding
Each club supplies one clean half of the question and they conflict. Rennes have scored in all four matches and failed to score in only 17.6% of 34 last season, while creating 2.31 xG per match — the second-highest figure on this board after Barcelona. Lyon have kept clean sheets in 2 of 4 at 1.19 xG against, extending a 44.1% clean-sheet rate across 34 matches, and have conceded nothing in two away matches. Both readings are two seasons deep, which is rare here. What holds the rating is Lyon’s own attacking half: they have failed to score in 25.0% of matches this season against 17.6% last.
Team goalsWell-suited — Rennes’ creation is corroborated four ways
Rennes are the best-evidenced attacking side on this card and the improvement is mechanical rather than finishing-driven. Expected goals 1.60 to 2.31, shots 14.15 to 17.00, shots on target 4.97 to 5.75, deep completions 6.41 to 8.50 — four independent columns moving together, under a coach in his first full season with three new forwards. They are scoring 2.00 per match against 2.31 expected, so the rate is not running hot. Lyon’s side is quieter and equally stable: 1.50 goals on 1.59 xG against 1.56 and 1.62 last season, with the attacking personnel almost entirely replaced and the output unchanged — which is itself the finding.
Halves (1H result, 1H goals)OK — Rennes’ first-half rate has nearly doubled
The timing columns separate these sides clearly. Rennes have scored 1.50 first-half goals per match against 0.79 last season and conceded 0.50 against 0.56. Lyon have scored 0.50 against 0.79 and conceded 0.25 against 0.44, so both of their first-half figures have fallen while both of Rennes’ attacking ones have risen. Against a divisional 1.22 per match, the fixture’s combined first-half rate sits above the line and is driven almost entirely by the away side. Four matches each is the limit, and Rennes’ 1.50 includes a 2–2 with Paris Saint-Germain. A first-half result needs a margin from two unbeaten sides and there is none.
Discipline (cards, fouls)OK — strong referee history with the home club, everything else pointing low
Vernice is the live input and his record here is the deepest on this board: 15 Ligue 1 matches at 4.33 cards and 27.53 fouls, above the divisional 3.91 cards — and specifically three Lyon matches producing 8, 5 and 6 cards, an average of 6.33. Against that, both clubs are among the cleanest in the division: Lyon take 1.25 cards on 11.5 fouls and Rennes 1.75 on 13.0, against a divisional 2.95 cards and 11.74 fouls, and both sat at 1.76–1.91 last season. The complication is his own current form: 2.00 cards at 13.0 fouls per card across two matches this season, the opposite of his 2025–26 line. A referee whose two priors contradict, meeting two low-card clubs he has a heavy history with, is informative in both directions — which is why this sits at OK rather than higher or lower.
CornersWell-suited — Rennes’ conceded figure repeats and has a named cause
Rennes concede 8.25 corners per match, against 4.62 last season and a divisional 4.84 — and the per-match counts read 10, 6, 12 and 5, so it repeats across all four rather than resting on the Paris Saint-Germain match. The cause is named in section A rather than assumed: the centre-back line that conceded 4.62 was dismantled — Jacquet to Liverpool for £60m, Brassier to Frankfurt, Østigård made permanent at Genoa — and rebuilt around Cresswell and Oliveira, with possession down from 53.53% to 49.77%. Lyon are the control: 5.91 won and 4.97 conceded last season, 5.50 and 5.00 now, a club that has barely moved at either end across a summer of heavy attacking turnover. One side stable across two seasons, one moved sharply with a stated mechanism.

D · Early-season risk notes

  • Lyon are on their ninth competitive match in six weeks and Rennes their fifth. Four Champions League qualifiers, a Europa League tie in Brussels on 16 September, and four league matches. Nothing in the rate data above accounts for that, and it is the largest unmeasured variable on this card — particularly for a side whose defensive figures (0.50 conceded, 1.19 xG against) depend on sustained organisation.
  • Lyon’s attacking output is unchanged while the attackers are not. Fofana (€35m) and Moreira (€33.6m) left; Nakamura arrived for €1m plus bonuses and Openda on loan. Expected goals moved only from 1.62 to 1.59 across that. Either the system carries the output or four matches is too short to show the change, and both readings are live.
  • Rennes’ corner and defensive numbers describe a back line assembled in August. A £60m centre-back sold, two more gone, two arrivals. The 8.25 corners conceded is four matches deep and corroborated by possession, but the unit producing it has played together for a month.
  • The referee’s two priors point in opposite directions. Fifteen matches last season at 4.33 cards and 6.35 fouls per card; two this season at 2.00 cards and 13.0 fouls per card, with three penalties between them. His penalty rate has moved from 0.40 to 1.50 per match on a two-match sample, and the discipline row and any penalty-dependent reading both inherit that unresolved split.
  • Ligue 1’s home-win rate is the most extreme divisional figure on this slate — 29.7% against 46.1% last season, with 35.1% draws over 37 matches — and this fixture sets two unbeaten sides against each other underneath it.
  • Most sensitive: the Asian handicap and 1X2 — neither club has lost, seven of eight results are inside two goals, and Lyon’s schedule is unaccounted for. Least sensitive: corners and Rennes’ team-goal rate — the first because Rennes’ 8.25 conceded repeats across all four matches with a named cause while Lyon’s rate is unchanged across two seasons, the second because four independent creation columns moved together and the goals are running below the chances.

E · Short market-type guide

  • Corners are more robust than the match result here because Rennes’ 8.25 conceded repeats in all four matches and traces to a named £60m centre-back sale, while the result depends on a Lyon side playing its ninth match in six weeks with no published fatigue or injury data.
  • Rennes’ team goals are more robust than the total because their creation rise is corroborated by shots, shots on target, deep completions and expected goals simultaneously, whereas the total pits their 75% over-2.5 rate against Lyon’s 25% with the divisional line sitting between them.
  • Lyon’s clean-sheet record is more robust than their scoring record because 44.1% across 34 matches and 50.0% across four agree, with expected goals against falling from 1.41 to 1.19, whereas their attacking output is unchanged despite €68.6m of forwards being replaced by a €1m signing and a loan.
  • A first-half total is more robust than a first-half result because Rennes at 1.50 first-half goals and Lyon at 0.50 are two clearly separated inputs, while two unbeaten sides supply no margin inside 45 minutes.
  • The single fact that would most change which family I choose is Vernice’s real penalty rate. He has given three penalties in two matches this season against 0.40 per match across fifteen last season, and two of his five previous matches involving these clubs also produced penalties — so a spot-kick is a live and badly-measured input on a card where both sides are otherwise low-card and low-foul. What would settle it is another five or six Ligue 1 appointments this season, or a published French federation breakdown of penalties awarded per official; neither exists in any source reached today.

A · Squad, promotion & transfer context

Neither club is promoted and neither is new to this division. Toulouse finished 10th in 2025–26 on 44 points — 12 wins, 8 draws, 13 defeats, 47 scored, 46 conceded. Le Havre finished 14th on 35 points — 7 wins, 14 draws, 13 defeats, 32 scored, 44 conceded, with a 41.2% failed-to-score rate and only 32.4% of matches going over 2.5 against a divisional 52.6%. Both sit in the bottom four on two points, and neither has won. The structural headline is on the home side and it is a contradiction: Toulouse changed coach in May and are now creating 1.94 expected goals per match against 1.37 last season — the largest attacking improvement of any Ligue 1 club on this board — while sitting 17th with one goal per game and no clean sheet. Le Havre have changed almost nothing and are scoring even less than the 0.94 per match that nearly relegated them.

  • Toulouse — turnover high and funded by one sale; manager changed, announced 21 May 2026. Carles Martínez Novell, in charge since summer 2023, announced at the end of April that he would leave without extending a contract that ran to 30 June. Jens Berthel Askou, 43, was announced on 21 May 2026, arriving from Motherwell, who had finished 4th in Scotland; he had previously worked in Denmark, the Faroe Islands, Sweden and the Czech Republic. Out, the headline: Dayann Methalie, the club’s largest sale at €28m; Emersonn to Ipswich Town for £24m plus £2.6m — documented from the buying side on this board’s 16:00 card — Charlie Cresswell to Stade Rennais on 4 August, who play at this same kickoff tonight; plus Ibrahim Cissoko and Edhy Zuliani. In: Thomas Jørgensen, €7.5m — the largest arrival — with Niko Sigur, Casper Tengstedt, Sion Oppong and Christ Tapé. Two of the three departures with published destinations went to clubs on this card. The French press, which described Toulouse as very active in the previous window, expected more continuity this time; the ledger shows the opposite at the top end.
  • Le Havre — turnover low and almost entirely outgoing; manager unchanged in this window. Didier Digard is head coach. In — three: Junior Mwanga, €1.5m, the largest arrival of the window, and Timothée Pembélé. Out — nine: Mory Diaw, the goalkeeper, Fôdé Doucouré, Godson Kyeremeh and Mokrane Bentoumi, plus two departures documented from the other side elsewhere on this board — Issa Soumaré free to Stade Rennais, who play at 20:45 tonight, and Arouna Sanganté free to Sevilla, who play at 21:00. Three in and nine out, with the largest fee in either direction €1.5m, on a squad that scored 32 goals last season. No fee is published for any Le Havre departure in the sources reached at this depth, so the balance of the window cannot be stated.
  • Suspensions / absences. No injury or suspension list for either club is published by any source reached. That is the fourth Ligue 1 fixture on this slate with no available team news, and at this point it is a property of the division’s coverage rather than of any one match — Angers–Troyes, Le Mans–Lorient and Lyon–Rennes are all in the same position on this board.
  • Sample. Toulouse: four competitive matches, all league — 0–2 v Lyon (22 August), 2–2 at Brest (29 August), 0–1 v Lille (3 September), 2–2 at Lorient (12 September). Seventeenth on 2 points from two draws and two defeats, 4 scored and 7 conceded, with no clean sheet in four and no goal scored at home in two. Le Havre: four competitive matches, all league — 0–1 v Monaco (23 August), 1–1 at Lyon (29 August), 1–2 v Brest (5 September), 0–0 v Angers (12 September). Sixteenth on 2 points from two draws and two defeats, 2 scored and 4 conceded — the lowest scoring rate of any club on this board at 0.50 per match. Three of Toulouse’s four opponents and two of Le Havre’s appear elsewhere on this slate.
  • Referee. Hakim Ben El Hadj Salem, confirmed, with Gwenaël Pasqualotti and Huseyin Ocak on the lines, Robin Chapapria fourth official and Bastien Dechepy and Mathieu Grosbost on VAR. Record, counted from every match he refereed in the ESPN record: Ligue 1 2025–26, 10 matches, 3.50 cards per match on 22.10 fouls — 6.31 fouls per card, close to the divisional 6.17 — with 3 red cards and, the figure that stands out, 0.60 penalties per match, against a divisional norm nearer 0.40. 2026–27 so far, 2 matches, 5.00 cards on 25.00 fouls — 5.00 fouls per card, a sharper reading than last season — and no penalty. He also took one Ligue 2 match last season. History with these clubs is unusually deep for a ten-match record: four of his ten involved one of them, and three produced penalties — Auxerre–Le Havre on 26 October 2025 (5 cards, 33 fouls), Lorient–Toulouse on 9 November (4 cards, 24 fouls, two penalties), Le Havre–Angers on 4 January 2026 (2 cards, 23 fouls) and Toulouse–Lille on 12 April (3 cards, 25 fouls, one penalty). He has also already taken two matches involving clubs on tonight’s card this season, at Angers and at Le Mans.
  • Not sourced. Fees for every Le Havre departure, so the club’s net window cannot be stated; fees for four of Toulouse’s five arrivals and for Cissoko, Zuliani and Cresswell. Any injury or suspension list for either club. Whether Didier Digard’s tenure began this summer or earlier — he is named as head coach for 2026–27 by the sources reached, but none gives an appointment date, so no change is asserted and none is ruled out. One Toulouse league match from 2025–26 is missing from ESPN’s record — their season reads 33 matches where the division played 34 — so every Toulouse 25/26 rate here is over 33 matches and is labelled as such rather than adjusted. No source characterises the referee’s reading of contact in the box in words; his 0.60 penalties per match is counted, not described.

B · Style & early-season reality

Toulouse under Askou are a different team in every column except the one that decides matches. Expected goals have risen from 1.37 to 1.94 per match and deep completions from 6.24 to 9.75 — more than half as many again — while PPDA has fallen from 12.81 to 10.43, so they press harder and get into the box far more often. The most dramatic shift is possession: 42.22% last season, 52.35% now, a ten-point jump, from a side that has just sold its most valuable player for €28m and its centre-forward to Ipswich. What has not followed is the scoreline: 1.00 goal per match against 1.42 last season, on 4.50 shots on target against 4.30. The defensive column has gone backwards in results while holding in chances — 1.75 conceded per match against 1.39, but expected goals against of 1.53 against 1.31, and no clean sheet in four. The foul rate is the one unambiguous cost of the new approach: 16.75 per match, the highest of any club on this board, against 14.36 last season and a divisional 11.74, while their card rate has halved from 2.48 to 1.25 — two figures moving hard in opposite directions.

Le Havre are the same side as last season, only quieter at both ends. Expected goals 1.10 against 1.14, non-penalty xG 0.91 against 1.00, possession 48.73% against 46.84% — essentially unmoved — and the goals have fallen from 0.94 to 0.50 per match. The one genuine change is in the press: PPDA 9.61 against 10.75, so they engage earlier, and opponents press them at 8.93 against 9.26. The cost shows in the defensive chance column: expected goals against up from 1.64 to 1.86, the higher of the two sides here, with deep completions conceded down from 7.65 to 6.50 — fewer entries, better ones. Their shot volume has risen sharply, from 10.94 to 15.00 per match, without the expected goals following it, which says the extra shots are coming from further out. The single most telling number on their side is the first-half column: no first-half goal at all in four matches, against 0.41 per match last season, on a side that failed to score in 41.2% of a full season.

C · Market-type suitability

1X2 / MoneylineDangerous
Two sides on two points from four, neither with a win, and the readings of them disagree. Toulouse changed coach in May and their underlying numbers say they are much improved — 1.94 expected goals against 1.37, possession up ten points — while their results say 17th. Le Havre’s numbers say they are what they were, and what they were is 14th with 35 points. The divisional home-win rate is the most extreme input on the board: 29.7% against 46.1% last season, with 35.1% draws, and Toulouse have not scored at home this season in two attempts. Four matches, two contradictory readings, and a base rate that has moved sixteen points.
Asian handicap / DNBDangerous
Toulouse’s four margins are −2, 0, −1, 0; Le Havre’s are −1, 0, −1, 0. Between them, eight results containing four draws and four defeats, none by more than two goals, and no win at all. That is a tightly clustered distribution with no upper tail on either side, which leaves nothing for a margin to attach to. Toulouse’s venue split is the sharpest detail: 0.00 scored and 1.50 conceded per home match against 2.00 and 2.00 away, two matches each. Ligue 1’s 35.1% draw rate is the highest of the five divisions here and both clubs have drawn half their matches.
Totals (O/U goals)OK — both clubs low, and one chance line disagrees
The club evidence points one way and is two seasons deep on the away side. Le Havre are at 25.0% over 2.5 this season after 32.4% last, against a divisional 52.6% — the second-lowest rate on this board — and their matches have produced 6 goals in four. Toulouse are at 50.0% after 54.5%. The complication is Toulouse’s expected goals: 1.94 for and 1.53 against, summing to 3.47 per match, well above the divisional 2.76, so the chance data on the home side argues for more goals than the scorelines have delivered. Ligue 1 is the only division on this board running below its own 2025–26 rate, which supports the low reading.
BTTSOK — both at 50%, and both blank halves are well documented
The symmetry is real: both clubs have had both teams score in 2 of 4 and both have failed to score in 2 of 4, against a divisional 56.8% and 27.0%. Each side’s scoring problem has a history rather than being a four-match artefact — Le Havre failed to score in 41.2% of 34 matches last season, the worst rate on this board, and Toulouse in 30.3% of 33. The conceding halves differ and favour the same conclusion: Toulouse have kept no clean sheet in four (0.0%, against 27.3% last season) while Le Havre have kept one, and Le Havre’s 1.86 expected goals against is the higher of the two.
Team goalsSplit — Toulouse’s creation is real, their conversion is not
Toulouse are the interesting half and the gap is large. They create 1.94 expected goals per match — up from 1.37, and the third-highest figure on this board — from 15.00 shots and 9.75 deep completions, and score 1.00. That is close to a goal a game of underperformance across four matches, and it is what the whole card turns on. Le Havre’s side needs less discussion: 0.50 goals per match on 1.10 expected goals, 15.00 shots but only 4.50 on target, after a season at 0.94 and 1.14. Both clubs are scoring below their chances, both by a margin, and neither has a conversion rate worth projecting from four matches.
Halves (1H result, 1H goals)Well-suited — the quietest first-half pair on the board, in both seasons
This is the best-evidenced family here because the current figures extend long ones rather than contradicting them. Le Havre have not scored a first-half goal all season — 0.00 in four matches — after 0.41 per match across 34 last season, and concede 0.50 after 0.68. Toulouse score 0.25 after 0.61 and concede 0.75 after 0.67. Against a divisional 1.22 per match, these two clubs have produced one first-half goal between them in eight team-appearances, and both were below the divisional line last season too. Four separate figures, two seasons each, all pointing the same way — which is more agreement than any other row on this card can show. A first-half result remains unreadable: four draws in eight.
Discipline (cards, fouls)OK — a real foul signal, a real referee record, and they conflict on cards
The foul evidence is strong and one-directional: Toulouse commit 16.75 fouls per match, the highest rate of any club on this board, up from 14.36 and against a divisional 11.74; Le Havre commit 14.75, up from 13.71. Both are well above the line and both have risen. The card evidence contradicts it: Toulouse take only 1.25 cards per match, down from 2.48, and Le Havre 2.00, against a divisional 2.95. The referee sits between the two readings: 3.50 cards on 6.31 fouls per card across ten matches last season, but 5.00 cards at 5.00 fouls per card across two this season — and his four previous matches with these clubs gave 5, 4, 2 and 3. The live number is penalties: 0.60 per match last season, with two in one Toulouse match and one in another.
CornersOK — the most stable pair of rates on the board, and therefore the least informative
Both clubs sit within three-quarters of a corner of the divisional 4.84 at both ends, in both seasons. Toulouse won 4.52 and conceded 5.12 last season, and win 4.25 and concede 4.50 now. Le Havre won 4.26 and conceded 4.82, and win 5.25 and concede 5.00. Four rates, eight figures, none of them more than 0.99 from the divisional line. That stability is worth something — it survived a change of coach on one side and nine departures on the other — but it means the row carries no asymmetry to read. Worth noting against the style data: Toulouse’s deep completions rose from 6.24 to 9.75 without their corner count moving at all, which suggests the extra territory is coming centrally rather than down the flanks.

D · Early-season risk notes

  • Toulouse’s results and their chance data disagree by about a goal a game, and four matches cannot settle which is right. They create 1.94 expected goals and score 1.00; they concede 1.53 expected and ship 1.75. A new coach appointed on 21 May, a ten-point rise in possession and a 56% rise in deep completions all say the process has changed; 17th place and no win say it has not yet mattered.
  • One Toulouse league match from 2025–26 is missing from the public record. Their season reads 33 matches where the division played 34, so every 25/26 Toulouse rate above — the 1.42 goals, the 54.5% over 2.5, the 2.48 cards — is computed over 33 and labelled that way. It is a small distortion but it is real and it is not adjusted for here.
  • Le Havre sold or lost nine players and replaced them with three, and no fee is published for any departure. The largest arrival cost €1.5m. Their numbers have barely moved, which is the expected result of a quiet window — but two of the departures, Soumaré and Sanganté, went free to clubs playing tonight, and a squad that scored 32 goals last season is now scoring at half that rate.
  • The referee’s penalty rate is the outlier and his card rate is unstable. Ten matches last season at 3.50 cards and 0.60 penalties; two this season at 5.00 cards and none. Three of his four previous matches involving these clubs produced a penalty, including two in one. On a card where the home side commits 16.75 fouls per match, that is the input most likely to decide the discipline row and it is measured over ten matches.
  • Ligue 1’s home-win rate has collapsed to 29.7% from 46.1%, with 35.1% draws, over 37 matches. Both clubs have drawn half their matches, neither has won, and the home side has not scored at home. Every result-shaped row above is read against a baseline that has moved sixteen points in one direction.
  • Most sensitive: the Asian handicap and 1X2 — eight results between them, four draws and four defeats, no win, and Toulouse’s process and results pointing opposite ways. Least sensitive: the first-half total — four figures across two seasons each, all below the divisional line, with one first-half goal between the two clubs in eight team-appearances this season.

E · Short market-type guide

  • A first-half total is more robust than anything else on this card because both clubs were under the divisional first-half rate across full seasons (Toulouse 0.61, Le Havre 0.41 against 1.22) and are further under it now, with Le Havre yet to score a first-half goal at all.
  • Toulouse’s expected goals are more robust than their goal count because 1.94 per match is supported by 15.00 shots, 9.75 deep completions and a ten-point rise in possession, whereas the 1.00 they have scored comes from four matches in which they have not scored at home at all.
  • The foul side of the discipline row is more robust than the card side because Toulouse at 16.75 and Le Havre at 14.75 are both above the divisional 11.74 and both up on last season, while Toulouse’s card rate has halved to 1.25 and the referee’s own two seasons read 3.50 and 5.00.
  • BTTS is more robust than the total because both clubs’ failed-to-score rates have long histories — Le Havre 41.2% across 34 matches, Toulouse 30.3% across 33 — whereas the total has to reconcile two low club rates with Toulouse’s 3.47 combined expected goals per match.
  • The single fact that would most change which family I choose is whether Toulouse’s 1.94 expected goals is the coach or the fixture list. Their four opponents were Lyon, Brest, Lille and Lorient, and three of those sit in the top half; a side can accumulate shots and deep completions while chasing. What would settle it is a published home-and-away or in-game-state split of their chance creation under Askou — no source reached publishes one for Ligue 1 at club level — and until it exists the totals and team-goals rows both rest on whether that 1.94 describes a better team or a team that has been behind.

A · Squad, promotion & transfer context

Neither club is promoted and neither is new to this division. Barcelona won the 2025–26 title with 94 points — 31 wins, 1 draw, 6 defeats, 95 scored, 36 conceded, and a failed-to-score rate of 2.6%, meaning they blanked once in 38 matches. Sevilla finished 13th on 43 points — 12 wins, 7 draws, 19 defeats, 46 scored, 60 conceded, with a 15.8% clean-sheet rate. The structural headline is that the champions have got substantially better: Barcelona are scoring 4.67 goals per match this season against 2.50 last, on 4.31 expected goals against 2.62 — the largest attacking figures anywhere on this board by a distance — having replaced Lewandowski, Ferran Torres and Rashford with Anthony Gordon and Karim Adeyemi, both bought from clubs that played earlier on this same card. Sevilla, meanwhile, are 5th on 13 points having changed coach in March and rebuilt around eleven arrivals, most of them free or on loan.

  • Sevilla — turnover very high in both directions, and the coach changed mid-last-season; eleven in, thirteen out. Matías Almeyda was dismissed on 23 March 2026 after a 2–0 defeat to Valencia, with the club 15th on 31 points and three above the relegation zone; Luis García Plaza replaced him and this is his first full season. Out, the headline: Akor Adams to Venezia, €16m — Venezia kick off at 20:45 tonight, and this board’s card on them records €23.5m spent on Adams and Rrahmani together — Juanlu Sánchez to Bournemouth, €11m, Joaquín Oso to Strasbourg, €10m, Djibril Sow to Genoa €4m, Tanguy Nianzou to Lille; with Gudelj, Alexis Sánchez, Azpilicueta (retired), Januzaj and Nyland all leaving at contract expiry and Maupay and Batista Mendy’s loans ending. In — eleven, and the pattern is free transfers and loans: Robbie Ure from Sirius, €6.5m; Giorgi Kochorashvili from Sporting CP, €4m; Fran González from Real Madrid Castilla, €3m; Julio Díaz from Atlético B, €1m; free — Arouna Sanganté from Le Havre, who play at 20:45 tonight, Juan Iglesias from Getafe, and Jon Guridi from Alavés, whose departure at contract end is recorded on this board’s 16:15 card; on loan — Odysseas Vlachodimos from Newcastle, the first-choice goalkeeper, Youssouf Fofana from Milan, Felix Correia from Lille and Lucas Stassin from Saint-Étienne (€2.5m). Three of Sevilla’s eleven arrivals and one of their sales involve clubs playing on this card.
  • Barcelona — turnover high at both ends of the squad; manager unchanged. Hansi Flick continues as head coach. In: Anthony Gordon from Newcastle United, €70m fixed plus €10m in variables — recorded from the selling side on this board’s 16:00 card, where Newcastle’s €236m of sales is the structural story — Karim Adeyemi from Borussia Dortmund on a contract to 2031, also documented from the other side on this board’s 18:30 card, and Jesse Bisiwu from Club Brugge, contract to 2031. Out: Robert Lewandowski at the end of his contract, Marc-André ter Stegen, Ferran Torres, Marcus Rashford, Ansu Fati to Monaco, €11m, Iñaki Peña to Greece €3m, and Ronald Araújo on loan to Liverpool. Two of Barcelona’s three signings were bought from clubs that played earlier today — Newcastle at 16:00 and Dortmund at 18:30 — which is the clearest single illustration of the selling pattern running through this whole board. Discarded: one source asserts an outcome for the 2026–27 title; the season is six matches old and the claim is not usable, so it is not carried.
  • Suspensions / absences. No suspension is reported for either side. Barcelona: Joan García is the one concern — he came off at half-time of the last match with knee discomfort, though the staff do not expect a serious absence. Sevilla: Rubén Vargas and Lucas Stassin are listed as injured, with Arouna Sanganté a doubt. Contradiction reported, not resolved: the same source that lists Stassin as injured names him in its own probable eleven, so the Sevilla forward line cannot be stated with confidence — which matters, because Stassin and Sanganté are two of the eleven summer arrivals and Vargas a third.
  • Sample. Sevilla: six competitive matches, all league — 2–1 v Rayo Vallecano (15 August), 3–1 at Athletic Club (22 August), 1–3 v Atlético Madrid (29 August), 1–1 at Espanyol (6 September), 1–0 v Valencia (11 September), 1–0 at Deportivo (16 September). Fifth on 13 points from four wins, 9 scored and 6 conceded, and they have scored in every match. Barcelona: seven competitive matches, six in the league — 5–0 at Elche (23 August), 2–0 v Athletic Club (27 August), 5–2 v Rayo Vallecano (31 August), 5–0 at Valencia (6 September), 4–2 at Levante (13 September), 7–2 v Racing Santander (16 September) — plus a Champions League tie against Feyenoord on 9 September. First on 18 points from six wins: 28 scored and 6 conceded. Three of Barcelona’s six league opponents were carded on this board today — Rayo at 14:00, Athletic at 16:15 and Racing Santander at 18:30.
  • Referee. Juan Martínez Munuera, confirmed, with Diego Barbero and Miguel Martínez on the lines, José David Martínez fourth official, Raúl Martín González on VAR and Javier Iglesias Villanueva assisting. The Valencian official has been international since 2015 and this is his fourth LaLiga match of 2026–27. Record, counted from every LaLiga match he refereed in the ESPN record: 2025–26, 18 matches, 4.33 cards per match on 28.44 fouls — 6.56 fouls per card, a longer leash than the divisional 5.58 — with 0.33 penalties per match and 2 red cards. 2026–27 so far, 3 matches, 3.00 cards on 22.67 fouls, 7.56 fouls per card, and 0.67 penalties. He books just under the divisional rate (4.33 against 4.52) while whistling considerably more. History: three matches last season involving these clubs, and the relevant one is exact — he refereed the reverse fixture, Barcelona–Sevilla on 15 March 2026, which produced just 1 card on 19 fouls but two penalties. He also took Barcelona–Real Oviedo on 25 January (3 cards, 29 fouls) and Sevilla–Real Sociedad on 4 May (3 cards, 30 fouls). The Spanish press notes this is his first match involving either club this season.
  • Not sourced. Fees for Barcelona’s Bisiwu signing and for the Lewandowski, ter Stegen, Ferran Torres and Rashford departures, none published in the sources reached, so Barcelona’s net window cannot be stated. Fees for four of Sevilla’s eleven arrivals. Whether Lucas Stassin is available, the two accounts being irreconcilable. No source characterises Martínez Munuera’s reading of contact in the box in words — notable given he awarded two penalties in the reverse fixture — so his 0.33 and 0.67 penalty rates are counted, not described. Everything else the spec asks for is published by this division and was counted from the match record.

B · Style & early-season reality

Barcelona are producing the most extreme attacking numbers on this board and they are not a small-sample illusion — every column has moved in the same direction from an already-dominant baseline. Expected goals 4.31 per match against 2.62 last season; non-penalty xG 3.69; shots 21.00 against 18.21; shots on target 8.50 against 6.76; deep completions 15.33 against 11.53. Possession is unchanged at 68.48% against 68.84% and the press is unchanged at a PPDA of 7.01 against 7.09 — so this is the same system doing more damage inside it, with Gordon and Adeyemi in place of Lewandowski, Ferran Torres and Rashford. The defensive side has drifted slightly the other way: 1.00 conceded per match against 0.95, and expected goals against 1.17 against 1.33. The corner column is the clearest single measure of territorial control on this card: 8.17 won and 2.83 conceded, against a divisional 4.94, and against Racing Santander three days ago they won 18 corners to nil. The one figure that should temper all of this is the opposition: three of their six league opponents are the clubs carded on this board at 14:00, 16:15 and 18:30, two of whom sit 11th and 10th.

Sevilla are a pressing, foul-heavy side that has improved defensively and stopped creating. Expected goals 1.11 per match, and non-penalty expected goals just 0.86 — so roughly a quarter of what they generate comes from the spot — against 1.04 and 0.92 last season. The defensive improvement is the real change and it is substantial: expected goals against down from 1.61 to 1.16, goals conceded from 1.58 to 1.00, and a clean-sheet rate up from 15.8% to 33.3%, on a back line that lost Nianzou and Juanlu Sánchez and gained Juan Iglesias free and Vlachodimos on loan in goal. They press hard and are pressed hard: PPDA 8.90 with opponents at 7.49, both among the most aggressive pairs on this board, and possession has fallen from 50.98% to 43.83%. The cost is the discipline column, where they lead the board: 3.50 cards per match against 2.89 last season, on 15.17 fouls against a divisional 12.36. Their first-half attacking record is the most extreme on their side of the card — one first-half goal in six matches, 0.17 per match, against a divisional 1.43.

C · Market-type suitability

1X2 / MoneylineOK — the best-documented side on the board, against a real caveat
Barcelona are the most thoroughly evidenced team anywhere on this page: a 94-point title season, six wins from six, 28 goals, and chance data that has improved on every column from that baseline — 4.31 expected goals, 21.00 shots, 15.33 deep completions, possession and PPDA unchanged. Six league matches is also the deepest current sample here. The caveat is specific and checkable: three of those six opponents are on this board today — Rayo, Athletic and Racing Santander — and Racing shipped seven. Sevilla’s own side is readable too: 5th on 13 points with a genuinely improved defence (1.16 xG against, down from 1.61). The divisional home-win rate of 45.0% is close to last season’s 48.9%, the steadiest baseline on this board.
Asian handicap / DNBDangerous
Barcelona’s six margins are +5, +2, +3, +5, +2, +5 — an average of +3.67 with nothing below +2 — and Sevilla’s are +1, +2, −2, 0, +1, +1. A side whose smallest winning margin all season is two goals has a distribution with no lower tail, which is as hard to place a line against as one with no upper tail. The 28 goals came against opposition that includes two clubs sitting 10th and 11th this morning and a promoted side that conceded seven. Sevilla’s home record is 1.33 scored and 1.33 conceded per match across three. Six matches of extreme margins against a mixed fixture list is not a margin estimate.
Totals (O/U goals)Well-suited — every input large, and they agree
This is the best-evidenced family on the card because the sample is six matches rather than four and both clubs have full seasons behind them. Barcelona have gone over 2.5 in 83.3% of matches this season after 73.7% across 38 last, with 4.31 expected goals for and 1.17 against; their matches have produced 34 goals in six. Sevilla sit at 50.0% after 55.3%, with 1.11 and 1.16. The divisional line has moved the same way — LaLiga is at 3.07 goals per match and 56.7% over 2.5 this season against 2.69 and 50.0% last — so the club rates, the chance data and the baseline all point up together, which almost nothing else on this board can claim. The honest caveat stays the fixture list behind Barcelona’s 28 goals.
BTTSOK — both always score, and one rarely concedes
The scoring half is settled about as firmly as this board allows: neither club has failed to score in a league match this season — 0.0% each — and Barcelona blanked once in 38 matches last season, a 2.6% rate, the lowest figure anywhere on this page. Sevilla have scored in all six and in 73.7% of last season. The conceding half is where they part: Barcelona have kept three clean sheets in six at 1.17 expected goals against, while Sevilla have kept two at 1.16 — the two defensive chance figures are almost identical, which is a genuinely surprising result and the reason this is not rated lower. Sevilla’s 15.8% clean-sheet rate last season is the figure that has moved most.
Team goalsWell-suited on the away side — the largest and best-corroborated figure on the board
Barcelona are scoring 4.67 goals per match on 4.31 expected goals, which is the rarest combination on this page: an extreme rate that its own chance data almost entirely accounts for, rather than one running ahead of it. The supporting columns all moved together from a 95-goal season — shots 18.21 to 21.00, shots on target 6.76 to 8.50, deep completions 11.53 to 15.33 — with possession and pressing unchanged, so the mechanism is the same system with different forwards. Sevilla’s side is the weak half and the penalty split is why: 1.11 expected goals but only 0.86 non-penalty, so about a quarter of their creation is from the spot, and they score 1.50 per match on it.
Halves (1H result, 1H goals)Well-suited — the widest first-half gap on the board, both sides two seasons deep
This is the sharpest split on the page and both halves of it have history. Barcelona score 2.17 first-half goals per match — the highest figure on this board by more than a goal — after 1.16 across 38 matches last season, and concede 0.33 after 0.53. Sevilla score 0.17, one first-half goal in six matches, after 0.50 last season, and concede 0.67. Against a divisional 1.43 per match, one club is at 150% of the baseline before the break and the other at 12%. Both figures extend last season’s direction rather than contradicting it, and Barcelona’s is measured over six matches rather than four. A first-half result inherits the same margin problem as the full-time one.
Discipline (cards, fouls)Well-suited — a wide club gap, a deep referee record, and a specific precedent
Three inputs, all substantial, and they agree on the shape if not the level. Sevilla take 3.50 cards per match — the highest rate of any club on this board — on 15.17 fouls, against 2.89 and 14.76 last season. Barcelona take 1.33 on 9.33 fouls, against 1.61 and 9.47, both the lowest figures here. That is a 2.17-card gap between the two sides, and both clubs’ rates are stable across two seasons. The referee has 18 matches at 4.33 cards and 6.56 fouls per card — a long leash on a high whistle — and three this season at 3.00. The precedent is exact and cuts the other way: his Barcelona–Sevilla in March 2026 produced only 1 card on 19 fouls, so the one directly comparable match in his record is his quietest. The penalty rate is the live number: 0.33 last season, 0.67 this, and two in that reverse fixture.
CornersWell-suited — a large asymmetry with a fully visible mechanism
Barcelona win 8.17 corners per match and concede 2.83, against a divisional 4.94 and their own 6.92/4.50 last season — so the direction is two seasons old and the level has risen. The mechanism is in four other columns at once: 68.5% possession, 21.00 shots against 7.33 faced, 15.33 deep completions against 2.67 conceded, and a PPDA of 7.01 against opponents’ 19.67, meaning nobody presses them back. Sevilla are the counterweight and a stable one: 3.67 won and 3.67 conceded, below the divisional line at both ends, after 4.82/4.55 last season. The caution is the same as everywhere on this card: the 18-corners-to-nil against Racing Santander three days ago inflates the level, and the per-match counts (1, 12, 9, 4, 5, 18) are the widest spread of any corner figure on this board.

D · Early-season risk notes

  • Half of Barcelona’s 28 goals came against clubs carded on this board today. Rayo Vallecano (5–2), Athletic Club (2–0) and Racing Santander (7–2) account for three of the six, and the promoted side shipped seven with 18 corners conceded. Every extreme figure on their side — 4.67 goals, 4.31 expected goals, 8.17 corners, 2.17 first-half goals — carries that fixture list inside it, and no source reached publishes an opponent-adjusted version.
  • Sevilla’s attacking numbers are a quarter penalties. Expected goals 1.11, non-penalty expected goals 0.86 — a gap of 0.25 per match — against a much smaller 0.12 gap last season. That matters unusually here because the referee awarded two penalties in the reverse fixture in March and is running at 0.67 per match this season, so a chunk of the home side’s expected output depends on an event whose rate is measured over three matches.
  • Sevilla changed coach in March and rebuilt with loans and free transfers. Almeyda was dismissed on 23 March 2026 with the club 15th; Luis García Plaza is in his first full season. Eleven arrivals, of which four are loans and three free, including the first-choice goalkeeper on loan from Newcastle. Their 15.8% clean-sheet rate last season has become 33.3%, and the unit responsible has been together for weeks.
  • Two of the three Sevilla players named as unavailable or doubtful are summer arrivals, and one account contradicts itself. Vargas and Stassin are listed injured with Sanganté doubtful, yet the same source names Stassin in its probable eleven. On a side whose forward line was assembled this summer, that is a material gap.
  • LaLiga is running 0.38 goals per match above its own completed season — 3.07 against 2.69, and 56.7% over 2.5 against 50.0% — on 60 matches against 380. The totals row above leans on that baseline as well as on the clubs, and a sixth of a season is what it rests on.
  • Most sensitive: the Asian handicap — Barcelona’s smallest winning margin all season is two goals, which is a distribution with no lower tail, built partly against sides sitting 10th, 11th and newly promoted. Least sensitive: the first-half total and the discipline row — the first because a 2.17-against-0.17 gap extends both clubs’ full-season direction over six matches, the second because a 2.17-card club gap is stable across two seasons on both sides and the referee has 18 matches of record.

E · Short market-type guide

  • The first-half total is more robust than the match result here because Barcelona’s 2.17 first-half goals and Sevilla’s 0.17 both extend last season’s direction across six matches, whereas the result rests on a fixture list in which half of Barcelona’s opponents were clubs carded on this board today.
  • Barcelona’s team-goal rate is more robust than the handicap because 4.67 goals sits on 4.31 expected goals — a rate its own chance data accounts for, supported by shots, shots on target and deep completions all rising together — while the margin question has no result under +2 to calibrate against.
  • The card element of the discipline row is more robust than the penalty element because the 2.17-card gap between Sevilla’s 3.50 and Barcelona’s 1.33 holds across both seasons on both sides, whereas the penalty rate rests on 0.33 last season, 0.67 across three matches this season, and two in one previous meeting.
  • Sevilla’s defensive improvement is more robust than their attacking record because 1.16 expected goals against is corroborated by the clean-sheet rate doubling from 15.8% to 33.3%, while a quarter of their expected goals for comes from penalties.
  • The single fact that would most change which family I choose is an opponent-adjusted version of Barcelona’s 4.31 expected goals. Three of their six league opponents are clubs this board carded today, one of them a promoted side that conceded seven with 18 corners against, so the question is whether 4.31 describes the best attacking side on record or a very good one with a soft opening six. What would settle it is a published per-opponent or strength-adjusted expected-goals series — Understat gives the raw per-match figures but no adjustment, and no source reached publishes one — and until it exists the totals, team-goals and corners rows all rest on the same unadjusted number.

Across the card

  • Best structurally supported today — corners, on eight of the thirteen cards. It is the family whose rates most often survived the summer, and where they moved they moved with a visible mechanism rather than without one. The four largest figures on the board all have three or four other columns agreeing with them: Racing Santander concede 10.17 a game (f15), backed by 17.33 shots faced, 9.83 deep completions conceded and 46% possession; Angers win 9.00 (f19), up from 3.56, alongside shots rising from 9.29 to 15.75 and PPDA from 18.48 to 13.24; Udinese concede 7.75 and win 1.75 (f4); Troyes concede 1.50, the lowest on the board (f19), including none at all in a match they lost 2–6. Brighton and Arsenal (f9) and Osasuna and Rayo (f2) moved by less than one corner across a summer in which one replaced its whole centre-back line and the others changed coach.
  • Also well supported — BTTS, on five cards and for opposite reasons each time. Coventry have failed to score in all four league matches (f16) and Tottenham in all four (f1), both with shots, shots on target and xG agreeing; Rayo have had both sides score in six of six (f2) behind 11.83 deep completions conceded; Le Mans in four of four (f20) with 18.00 shots faced at one end and an unchanged shots-on-target rate at the other. In every case a transfer ledger sits behind the rate as well as a number.
  • Least supported — the Asian handicap, on all thirteen cards. Thirteen fixtures, thirteen Dangerous ratings, and the reason repeats: the margins are bimodal rather than merely uncertain. Cagliari have won three one-goal matches while being outshot 19.0 to 12.3 (f4); Brighton’s four results are +4, −1, 0, +5 (f9); Everton have drawn three of four (f10); Le Mans have drawn three of four (f20); seven of Tottenham and Villa’s eight results are a defeat or a goalless draw (f1).
  • Also poorly supported — 1X2, on ten of thirteen. The three exceptions are f9, where Arsenal have reproduced last season’s xG, xGA, PPDA and deep-completion figures almost to the decimal; f12, where Athletic changed coach and signed nobody, making five matches an unusually clean natural experiment; and f16, where Forest’s and Coventry’s underlying numbers point the same way as their results.
  • Where the evidence is thinnest — second-tier expected goals, and it affects seven cards. No public source publishes xG for the Championship, LaLiga 2, Serie B or Ligue 2, so Hull, Coventry, Ipswich, Racing Santander, Venezia, Le Mans and Troyes have no 2025–26 chance data at all. On every one of those cards the promoted club’s last season is a goal count and a league position and nothing more, and none of it is estimated. Their divisional baselines were computed here instead — 556 Championship matches, 468 in LaLiga 2, 390 in Serie B, 306 in Ligue 2 — so the goal counts at least have a comparison.
  • Second-thinnest — Ligue 1 refereeing and Premier League appointments, for different reasons. Both Ligue 1 officials carded here have exactly one top-flight match on record and no 2025–26 Ligue 1 season (Souifi on f19, Kubler on f20), and their substantial Ligue 2 records describe a division with a different card rate — both discipline rows are rated Dangerous for that reason despite the clubs having the cleanest card rates on the board. Separately, the Premier League publishes its ten matchweek officiating teams without saying which takes which fixture; three were matched by fixture-specific reporting and f11 Newcastle–Hull has no named official at all, so its discipline row is Dangerous on that ground alone.
  • The one ordinary fixture is f4, Udinese–Cagliari — neither club changed coach, neither is promoted, both sit near their divisional foul and card rates, and the referee has ten matches of record. Even there the actual and underlying records disagree by more than a goal a game on the away side.
  • Trust least: the Bundesliga card. f17 is the only German fixture carded, both clubs have played three league matches — the thinnest sample on the slate — and the division is running 0.72 goals per match above its own completed season on 28 matches. The totals row there is well supported precisely because the divisional signal is so large; everything else on it rests on three matches a side.

Scope. This page rates market families only. It contains no selections, no sides, no recommended lines and no confidence labels — by design. Where evidence is thin it says so rather than filling the gap.
